Key Results
Dec 15 - Final Minutes of Election Campaign

Lebed, Skokov Interviewed
LD1612000395

{FBIS Editorial Report] Moscow Mayak Radio Net-
work in Russian at 1830 GMT on 15 December broad-
casts a 30-minute election slot by the Congress of Rus-
sian Communities, represented by Yuriy Skokov and
Lieutenant General Aleksandr Lebed. After the opening
pleasantries, studio host Yuliy Semenov invites listen-
ers to phone in with their questions and then asked Gen
Lebed to start the program with an important announce-
ment.

Lebed says: “I have to make a statement as follows.
Under a ruling by The Russian Federation Central
Electoral Commission, an Aleksandr Ivanovich Lebed
— my full name — has been removed from the list
of candidates for the State Duma advanced by the
Dzhuna Davitashvili bloc. Here is the telegram issued
by the relevant electoral commission. However, only
the number of that bloc is given, No 34. Judging
from telephone calls we have been receiving, this is
being interpreted in the regions as an instruction to
remove my name — Lieutenant General Aleksandr
Ivanovich Lebed, running for the Congress of Russian
Communities No 31 — from the voting papers. I must
state that I am Lieutenant General Aleksandr Ivanovich
Lebed and that I am remaining and will remain on the
list for the Congress of Russian Communities socio-
political movement. I have not broken away from that
movement and I remain on the list of candidates for
No 31, the Congress of Russian Communities in single-
mandate constituency No 176 in Tula Region".

He concluded, “Este aed listeners, let me give this
warning: don’t mess with us.”

Semenov clarifies matters by saying that that Lebed had
been removed from that list, and that this Lebed is still
on this list of candidates and is here in the studio as
well.

A listener asks whether the Congress of Russian Com-
munities will try to change the rules on Duma deputies’
immunity. Lebed answers that “deputies’ immunity is
entireiy unnecessary. The law should apply equally to
all, from the President down to the caretaker, and vice-
versa. We also believe that people wishing to take their
place in representative forums should have an unblem-
ished reputation. If these people have blemishes, then
they have no place there and should do something else.
We think that incomes should be declared and that re-

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

strictions should be placed on presents so we know if
these really are presents or something else.”

In response to a question about the meaning and
nationalistic undertone of the name Congress of Russian
Communities, Yuriy Skokov explains it is a stepping
stone to a union of all peoples in the Russian Federation
to resolve nationality issues. There are already several
national coagresses, he said: of Tatars, of Circassians, of
Bashkirs and others. "On the recommendation of leaders
of these national) movements, we agreed we would set
up a congress of Russians — there are, after all, 120
million of us out of 148 million. This Russian congress
would initiate the creation of another organization, the
Union of Peoples, so that we can jointly agree on the
principles of nationality policy.”

A question concerning allegations by Vladimir Zhiri-
novskiy of financial irregularities at the 14th Army in
Dniester under Lebed’s command is given short shrift
by Lebed. If there had been anything going on, news of
it would have surfaced and an investigation launched.
"I don’t know what he’s on about", Lebed says. “This
is what Zhirinovskiy is up to — he has nothing to do
but throw mud at people in the hope that some of it will
stick.”

Asked about the Black Sea Fleet, Lebed says: “The
Black Sea Fleet question is being decided by rust.
Neither Russia nor Ukraine can resolve the issue. It's
a great idiocy and a crime against our shared future.
There's not a single archeologist or historian or anyone
else who can explain exicily what is the difference
between Ukrainians and F-ussians.”

The next question is about Chechnya and briefly an-
swered by Lebed. “There's no military solution to the
problem. It can only be solved by diplomatic, economic,
and political methods and at state level. Not at the level
of field commanders, whoever they may be.”

Skokov is asked how his party would finance its election
promises if elected. He replies that the money always
seems to be available in the budget for certain pressure
groups like the farmers. Also, up to one-fifth of the
budget is squandered. The money is there, he says,
and election promises would be funded from imposing
tighter control on the state coffers.

A listener asks whether restoration of the USSR features
in the Congress of Russian Communities’ manifesto.
The idea is dismissed as unrealistic by Skokov, on the
grounds that disintegration has gone too far. He is,
however, in favor of closer links, especially economic.

A listener asks what the Congress of Russian Communi-
ties will do if it cannot manage to win power by demo-
cratic means. Semenov puts this question to Lebed, on

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

account of his “tough image." "I think we’ll manage. If
we don’t, we'll live with it. No problem.” Skokov adds:
"It depends what happens on 17 December. If we are
able to create a political force and if the people uader-
stand that they can act against the rising tide c‘ crime,
thuggery and unbridled officialdom, against unlimited
abuse of power, by uniting into a single force — and
they do understand this and will unite — then there'll be
an opportunity to solve these problems in 2 truly tough
but democratic manner in the name of that force, in the
name of that movement. So we are waiting, asking and

hoping.”

Skokov uses a question about care for the future
generation of children as an opportunity to outline his
stance on the economy: "Answering this question, I want
to stress that we are for a strong national economy,
protection of the domestic market and protection of the
Russian producer. If we don’t have that, if our people
aren’t working, if we can’t create our own material
product, if we don’t have a strong national economy,
then we’ll have nothing. The government is committing
a crime when it directs all our raw-material resources
not to the domestic market but to markets abroad,
creating jobs over there and letting people over there
get rich.”

He elaborates on this after another question about the
Congress of Russian Communities’ economic mani-
festo: is it one of stabilization, or is reform required?
“It is a program to restore the country’s economy after
the destruction under perestroyka and the reforms. Our
economy has shrunk by over 50 percent, which is more
than it did during the war."

Semenov invites last-minute raliying calls before airtime
runs out. "Russians unite,” Skokov says. "Our people are
in trouble. If we want to survive, then let’s join together.
But not just us — together with other peoples.”

Lebed says: "In order to survive, we need courage.
Given courage, everyone can decide for themselves."

Gaydar, Communist Spokesman Debate
LD1612003695

[FBIS Editorial Report] Moscow Russian Public Televi-
sion First Channel Network in Russian at 1955 GMT on
15 December carries the 40-minute Vzglyad program,
hosted by Yuriy Lyubimov.

Lyubimov begins by saying that the last minutes of the
election campaign are running out, and that it just so
happened that two radically different parties asked them
to stage TV debates. This is followed by a video report
of a betting office where bets are being made on the
elections.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

Lyubimov is then shown again, and says “Here, face
to face, are Yegor Gaydar, leader of the Democratic
Choice of Russia, and Yuriy Ivanov, an official
spokesman for the Communist Party of the Russian
Federation." The video shows Gaydar, Lyubimov, and
Ivanov, live in the studio.

Gaydar and Ivanov accused each other of hypocrisy, of
various political blunders, of destroying the economy,
and of provoking the 1993 political crisis.

The fellowing text rolls up on the screen: “Gaydars,
Chubzayses and others. who hate everything Russian,
Soviet and national, and who do nct want us to live
in accordance with the laws of truth, good and beauty,
are trying hard to push us into the cheap intellectual
prison camp, but their attempts are doomed — Gennadiy
Zyuganov"

Gaydar and Ivanov then accuse each other of enjoying
various privileges, using illegal money for the election
campaign, and unleashing the Chechen war.

Lyubimov asks: "With whom does the future lie?"

Ivanov answers: “The future does not lie with Yegor
Timurovich Gaydar, of course. Yegor Timurovich repre-
sents today a dwarfish and half-broken party. They will
not gain more than 10 percent together with Yavlin-
skiy — his brother-in-arms — even if they try really
hard. The future lies with those who work with the peo-
ple. I know the people, I have been campaigning for a
month and a half, and I know all their sufferings and
pains. I represent the interests of the dispossessed ma-
jority, whereas Yegor Timurovich represents those fat
cats that were in the party nomenklatura in 1991 and are
at the top now. Yegor Timurovich, you are alien to the
people. Your future is very bleak. Our faction was de-
bating today whether Zyuganov should take part in the
TV debates, and all of us decided unanimously: how
long are we going to argue with Gaydar and Yavlinskiy
— with those political dwarves? Well, Chernomyrdin,
for example, is a person one can talk to. So for how
long? And then there was a decision: Gaydar needs a
lawyer. So they have sent me here. Yegor Timurovich,
in three years housewives will beat you — as well as
Chernomyrdin and Yavlinskiy — with their bags in the
streets. I will stand for you — I promise. And the present
Communist Party is in favor of a peaceful way of re-
construction...”

Lyubimov interrupts, saying: "So, tne future lies with
you.”

Ivanov says: “Of course, the future lies with the
Communists. I have no doubt whatsoever."

pete clea pee in ip geen gli aenaae
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

Gaydar then argues that the futvie lay with the
democrats, which had been proven by the world
history. He also said the Communists could come to
power for only a short period of time.

Gaydar and Ivanov then accuse each other of provoking
the 1993 political crisis.

Ivanov says: "The elections are bringing Gaydar’s
political career to an end. This is the last performance of
the artist, let us put it this way - do you remember a film
with this title? So I do not object to Yegor Timurovich
speaking. Let the viewers see him for the last time,
because his value will drop sharply in the eyes of our
commentators."

He accuses Gaydar of battering the legislature in 1993,
and then goes on to say: “That was like a flash of
lighting, and everyone saw that on the one hand,
there were dispossessed people with Communists in
their ranks and, on the other hand, pro-Westerners
and democrats, who were trying to establish a new,
pro-Western and pro-American regime at Uncle Sam's
bidding. You pro-Westerners have no future, Yegor
Timurovich. The elections are the end of your story.
And you know perfectly well that your bloc will not be
represented in the Duma. And we — we will be there
— for a very long time, forever! On this cheerful note",
then ke pauses and changes tack. "I will not prevent you
from saying goodbye to the Muscovites, so dear to your
heart, to the Westerners and the democrats.”

Lyubimov asks Ivanov why he is burying the opposition,
and Gaydar says that it is too early ‘o bury them.

Ivanov says: "The Communists have an ideology. They,
the democrats, have no ideology. What is their ideol-
ogy? To serve the West?”

Lyubimov says: “It may be a good job that they have
no ideology.”

Gaydar responds: "We have an ideology. We have a
liberal ideology — the ideology that has triumphed in
the world in the 20th century, the ideology that will
triumph in Russia in the 21st century without fail.
And Russia will without fail take the normal road of
interest, freedom, open society, and open economy.
Well, probably you would manage to steal a few years
from us in the end of 20th and the beginning of the
21st century. No more than that. There is no communist
regime that has not collapsed.”

Ivanov says: "Russia will throw out traitors who work
for the West. And you will have to seek a place of
residence in Brighton and to provide yourself with
gasoline.”

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

After this, there is a general discussion with Lyubimov,
Gaydar and Ivanov interrupting each other. Lyubimov
then signs off asking Gaydar and Ivanov to shake hands,
which they do.

Broadcast By Nur Muslim Movement
LD1512162195

[FBIS Editorial Report] Moscow Russian Public Tele-
vision First Channel Network in Russian at 0525 GMT
on 15 December carries the “Election 95" program and
a seven and a half minute election broadcast by the
Nur Muslim movement. Khalit Yakhin, live in the stu-
dio, reads out a statement outlining how Mu: im values
could be of service to the whole country.

“Our task,” he says, “has been to create a Muslim
grouping in the State Duma. How successfully this task
will be achieved depends now only on you, on how you
vote, esteemed voters.

"We are trail-blazers. Before us none of Russia’s
Muslim organizations had taken part in a parliamentary
campaign. The Nur Muslim movement is the first
Muslim political organization in the history of Russia
that has legitimately, in an equal political struggle with
other Russian parties and movements, taken part in the
elections to the State Duma.

“We call on all people of Russia, regardless of ethnic
group or religion, to adopt an understanding attitude
to the program aims of the Nur Muslim movement.
As citizens and patriots of Russia we are not striving
for divisions and discord. We pledge to organize our
activities in the interests of all Russia.

“Our movement puts forward, as the uniting idea, the
Muslim idea. The moral and ethical standards of Islam
can and should influence the whole fabric of society.”

Yakhin goes on to compare Muslim values with those
currently being promoted in society. "We are opposed
to the unbridled adoption of Western values which ev-
erywhere, including in Russia, take on the ugly shape of
increased crime, prostitution, the unprecedented growth
of alcoholism and drug abuse, the cult of violence, the
lust for profit by any means, sex and pornogr iphy on the
television screens. We are in favor of taking the most
valuable things from Western culture and from the rich
cultural heritage of the East."

Yakhin concludes by appealing to all of Russia's

MKI1612125395 Moscow MOSKOVSKIY
KOMSOMOLETS in Russian '6 Dec 95 p2

{Report by Vitaliy Bochkarev and Elina Nikolayeva:
"TV-Elections: There Will Be No Repetition of 1993")}

(FBIS Translated Text] The election will be the most ex-
Citing subject of television reports on Sunday and Mon-
day. All of the six main television channels received in
Moscow will cover its progress in a dry and restrained
manner. In gearing up for another Duma election, there
was a manifestation of the 1993 syndrome, when "demo-
cratically” disposed television chiefs wished to demon-
strate for the whole world to see the victorious progress
of democracy in Russia. They organized a get-together
to celebrate a "new political year." As everyone remem-
bers, it was a disgrace. Celebrating democracy proved
to be celebrating Zhirinovskiy.

ORT [Russian Public Television] viewers will be getting
all the necessary information in special newscasts. There
may also be interviews with political scientists, sociol-
Ogists, and public figures, who will comment on the
events. On Russian Television, in addition to “Vesti,”
which plans to go on the air twice an hour, there will
be live broadcasts of a set of programs with the general
title "A Night of Expectations.” The proposed programs
will range from Svanidze’s “Details [Podrobnosti]" to

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

Oleynikov’s and Stoyanov’s humorous "Gorodok”" pro-
gram. Also planned is an auction where the personal
effects of former deputies who have distinguished them-
selves in one way or another will go on the block.

On NTV [Independent Television] it will be business
as usual, the only difference being that its “Itogi"
{newscast} will go on the air at 2200 instead of 2100.
It plans to conclude with an additional segment of
“Itogi” around midnight. Thus, Yzvgeniy Kiselev will
score the winning point. He has already won the
journalists’ election marathon by managing to finally
organize regular live television debates at the end of

the campaign.

State Duma.

ble victory of communists despite a great number of
prognoses.” Yeltsin told journalists after voting in the
Barvikha sanatorium. “One should not be in a hurry,”

will improve cooperation between the president, the
government and the State Duma,” Yeltsin said. He

previous composition of the State Duma failed to adopt
more than a hundred decrees “because they wasted time
on unnecessary activities.”

Asked whether it is possible to restore a communist
regim« in Russia, Yeltsin said: “No, and once more no.”

Zyuganov Expects Communists To Lead
Parliament Coalition

LD1712132195 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1128 GMT 17 Dec 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW, Dec 17 (Interfax)
— The Communist party aims to form a coalition rather
than Communist opposition majority in the new State
Duma, which will make it possible "to some extent
balance the system,” Communist party boss Gennady
Zyuganov told journalists at a polling station Sunday.

For this purpose, he recalled, during the election cam-
paign the Communist party had pulled 63 of its candi-
dates from various constituencies in favor of electoral
blocs which are current or potential allies of the Com-
munist Party.

For instance, communist candidates have been with-
drawn from the districts where former Soviet Prime
Minister Nikolai Ryzhkov from the bloc Power to the
People, film director Stanislav Govorukhin from the
bloc named after him, and representatives of the Agrar-
ian Party and the Congress of Russian Communities run
for parliament.

Election results will show the country’s leadership that
"to govern the old way is impossible, and the current
course should be corrected," Zyuganov said.

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

Asked what percent of the vote the communists expect
to get, Zyuganov elusively answered, "the whole percent
will be our percent, if properly counted."

In an earlier interview with Interfax on the eve of voting,
Zyuganov said that Russia did not need "a sharp political
turn to the left or elsewhere."

“On a big ship, the wheel should be smoothly handled
in line with the laws of aerodynamics," Zyuganov said,
adding that “although the country was turned sharply to
the right, nothing good has come out of it.”

If the Russian Communist Party wins more votes than at
the previous elections, that can be regarded as a success
for the party, Zyuganov remarked.

At the December 1993 elections, the Communists won
12.4 percent of the vote.

Communist’s Zyuganov Interviewed on Future
Plans

LD1812051695 Moscow Russian Public Television
First Channel Network in Russian
0345 GMT 18 Dec 95

{Interview with Gennadiy Zyuganov, leader of the Com-
munist Party of Russia, by an unidentified correspondent

(FBIS Translated Text] [Zyuganov) We had a total of
10 percent in the Far East the last time. Today we have
around 20 and if the same trend is confirmed in central
Russia, then we have had 20 to 25 here. Therefore I
think that we have an opportunity to double our strength
in parliament and have quite a solid and large faction.
It should be said straight out that we have preliminary
data on a number of districts. [Svetlana] Goryacheva has
also been elected in a single-seat constituency in the Far
East. [Viktor] Ilyukhin in Penze. And I think there will
be a lot more,

[Correspondent] Do you have any grounds for saying
that the results of the elections may be falsified?

[Zyuganov] The grounds exist. According to data from
American electronic intelligence, nine million votes
were falsified the last time at the constitutional refer-
endum. It is impossible to check this now because all
the materials have been destroyed. Nonetheless the of-
ficial authorities have not denied this news. Well, not
only have we sent observers to all the polling stations
— we had our representatives on the electoral commis-
sions and they carefully checked that the elections were
fair and accurate. Wherever attempts are made to add
votes on, we will be able to check them. We are count-
ing the data from the polling stations and we will see if

a town, district or region has had votes added on. Under

[Correspondent] Do you intend allying with other par-

[Zyuganov] Well my immediate plans are for getting a

Nearly All Servicemen Participate in Elections
LD1812123395 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1037 GMT 18 Dec 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Dec 18 (Interfax)
— A total of 97.6 percent of servicemen participated
in the Duma elections, a representative of the Defense
Ministry’s Main Educational Department told Interfax
Monday [18 December].

Some 94 percent of servicemen of the Strategic Missile
Troops, 93 percent of servicemen of the Army, 92
percent of servicemen of the Navy, 93 percent of
servicemen of the Air Force, 92 percent of servicemen
of the Air Defense Troops, and 92 percent of servicemen
of the Air-Borne Troops came to the polling stations on
December 17.

The most active participants in the elections were
students of military schools (99.9 percent), military
builders (99.0 percent) and main departments of the
Defense Ministry (98.5 percent).

The voting was well-organized in all military districts.
It is impossible to say which parties or blocs were
supported by the servicemen, because they came to open
polling stations together with civilians, said the official.
According to preliminary information, over 70 percent
of servicemen who voted at closed polling stations
supported the Russia is Our Home bloc.

The closed polling stations were open for servicemen
ioned in Georgia, A is, Azerbaijan, Tajikistan,

Transdniester, Ukraine (the Black sea fleet), Belarus (the

strategic missile units) and far abroad, said the ministry

representative.

All in all, there were 92 closed polling stations outside

Russia.

Preliminary voting results have come from the Trans-
dniester region. A total of 43.2 percent of the service-
men voted for Russia is Our Home, 12.9 percent for the

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

Congress of Russian Communities, 10.9 percent for the
Liberal-Democratic Party, 9.8 percent for the Russian
Communist Party and 4 percent for the Women of Rus-
Sia.

According to preliminary information, nine servicemen
may be elected Duma deputies. The overall number of
military candidates to the State Duma in single-mandate
electoral districts was 118.

OSCE Official Claims Elections ‘Free and Fair’

LD1812113495 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
1109 GMT 18 Dec 95

(By ITAR-TASS correspondent Ivan Novikov]

([FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW December 18
(ITAR-TASS) — The parliamentary elections in Russia
were free and fair. Peter Emery, head of the delegation
of the Parliamentary Assembly of the Organisation for
Security and Cooperation in Europe (OSCE), said this
on behalf of all the 140 internationa! observers from
26 countries. They held a news conference in the State
Duma on Monday.

Observers unanimously concluded that the multi-party
elections were held successfully, Emery said.

International observers had an opportunity to monitor
the elections from the opening of the polling stations
to their closure and the counting of votes. Emery said
his colleagues arrived at the conclusion that this year’s
elections were held in a better way than in 1993. The
shortcomings during the elections were insignificant,
Emery said. He noted, specifically, that some voters cast
ballots “by proxy” and that the counting of votes was
somewhat slow at some polling stations. Asked about
observers’ opinion regarding the elections in Chechnya,
Emergy said they can say nothing as there were no
international observers in Chechnya.

Election Commission Official on Preliminary
Results

LD1812024595 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
0232 GMT 18 Dec 95

{FBIS Transcribed Text} MOSCOW, December 18
(Itar-Tass) — The Central Election Commission played
down fears that communists and nationalists may domi-
nate the new State Duma and advised journalists to wait
till Monday morning for exact early returns from major
industrial centres.

"It is too early to speak about a definite leader",
Commission secretary Aleksandr Yelmakov told Tass
on Monday, but admitted that the Communist party of
Gennadiy Zyuganov will be among the leaders.

“One should seriously look at the informaion by 10:00
hours Moscow time (07:00 GMT), although it will also
be selective and not objective”, he added.

The major cities are lagging behind from the countryside
in providing election results and therefore the current
election situation “does not correspond to the general
temperatrure of Russia", Yelmakov said.

He suggested that the Our Home is Russia election bloc
of premier Viktor Chernomyrdin “may be among the
leaders” as the final results are known.

He agreed that the Liberal Democratic Party of Vladimir

, the Women of Russia and the Yabloko
bloc of Grigoriy Yavlinskiy will eventually overcome
the 5 per cent hurdle.

However, some four-five other parties may overcome
the hurdle as well, Yelmakov predicted.

He added that a majority of the Russian population lives
from the Urals to the western borders of the country and
the most exact results can be expected by 16:00 hours
Moscow time (13:00 GMT) Monday.

Yeltsin’s Remarks at Barvikha Polling Station
Reported
LD1712110195

[FBIS Editorial Report] Moscow Mayak Radio Network
in Russian at 0730 GMT on 17 December carries a two-
minute report by Mayak correspondent Yuliy Semenov
on President Yeltsin’s remarks after casting his vote
at the polling station at Barvikha sanatorium outside
Moscow. Semenov states that Yeltsin “refused to reply
to a question about whom he had voted for, seeing in it
an element of propaganda on election day.” “As far as
various forecasts are concerned, the president believes
that there is no need to be in too much of a hurry to
make an assessment. One must wait until the results. For
all that, he hopes that the composition of the Duma will
allow it to be capable of working and that the tradition of
cooperation between the legislative and executive power
will continue and that the Duma will not allow itself
to become involved in political intrigues. He is hoping
for such cooperation. In his opinion, no change in the
political situation should change the course of reforms.
At any rate, he will not reject it. As far as the future
prime minister is concerned, just as before, Yeltsin is
inclined toward cooperation with Viktor Chernomyrdin
and if some changes, considering the balance of political
forces, suddenly become necessary, then this issue will
only be resolved with Viktor Chernomyrdin. For the
moment he does not visualize any other prime minister.
As far as Yugoslavia is concerned — there were
questions about it from our colleagues — the president
said that he has made the decision that Russia should
vote in the Security Council in favor of the process
of peaceful settlement in Bosnia, in which Russia is

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

taking an active part. Responding to a question about
his health, the president said that the process of his
recovery is becoming stable, there have been some ups
and downs, but things were getting better and better. He
joked that all he had to do now was to fight against the
council [of doctors], in order to return to taking an active
part in all the current matters more quickly and, from
a personal point of view, he had a completely natural
desire to see in the New Year with his sufficiently large
family.”

Semenov concludes by promising more details laser in
the day.

In the Brvikha sanatorium in Moscow Region, Boris

[Yeltsin] Nobody and no circumstances will make me
abandon the policy of reform that we have chosen. As
for the government, provided there is a certain alignment
of forces at the State Duma, it will be necessary
to consider who will be in the government. As for
Chernomyrdin, I am personally in favor of the idea that
he should head the new government.

[Correspondent] Once again the head of the country has
ted the press over whether he will stand for
president in 1996. But he said he will make a public
statement to that effect in February. As for Yeltsin’s
health, he said he felt better. [passage omitted]

Zyuganov Satisfied With Early Returns, Eyes

LD1812003995 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
0035 GMT 18 Dec 95

(By Tamara Ivanova]

{(FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW, December 18
(Itar-Tass) — Gennadiy Zyuganov, whose Communist
party is said to be enjoying a major success in the
Russian Far East and Siberia, is satisfied with the early
returns and hopes to enhance success in other Russian
regions as well.

The Communist party has “seriously gained" compared
to the 1993 State Duma elections, Zyuganov told Tass
on Monday adding that the party has “very good
positions” also in the central regions of Russia.

He did not conceal his plans to win the presidential
elections next year as well. The election technology
of the Communist party is planned for two stages: the
parliamentary and presidential elections, he said.

Early returns said the Communists won over 21 per cent
of votes in the Far East and Siberia.

However, Zyuganov was sceptical of whether the pos-
sible victory could allow him to change the situation in
the country.

“Even if 100 per cent of seats in the Duma is won by the
opposition, it would be hard to change anything under
the existing (executive) authority", he said.

Still he will work to create “a solid coalition, a serious
program in order to offer something solid and serious".

Zyuganov Anticipates Strengthening of Duma
Powers

LD1812112795 Moscow Russian Television Network
in Russian 2310 GMT 17 Dec 95

(FBIS Translated Excerpt) [Unidentified presenter] We
have in the studio Gennadiy Andreyevich Zyuganov,
chairman of the Communist Party of the Russian
Federation; Viktor Ivanovich Ilyukhin, chairman of the
outgoing State Duma committee on security; and Viktor
Petrovich Peshkov, secretary of the Central Committee
of the Communist Party.

Gennadiy Andreyevich, what are your feelings as the
results come in?

[Zyuganov] We believed that we would actually gain
everywhere and that is what is happening. We have
just had the figures from Volgograd — we have had a
considerable increase there, getting almost 27 percent,
and we have around 30 percent in Altay. Even in
Moscow we have made substantial gains; in Moscow
Region we have around 20-22 percent.

This success comes through the nature of the party,
which has regained its strength, and through the fact that
we have all, without exception, used new technology
and worked directly with people. We have been in all

the territories, without exception; we have had hundreds
of meetings. We have a pretty sure and solid team and
a clear and precise program, which provides for two
stages: the State Duma elections and the presidential
elections.

The first, highly important, step towards strengthening
the position of the popular patriotic forces has, I believe,
been taken. This will have a palpable effect on the
formation of a big coalition in the State Duma, which
is our main idea and which both the program and the
team can offer.

[Presenter] Your first steps in the Duma? We know that
you are one of the most influential forces there.

[Zyuganov] Well, Viktor Ivanovich [Ilyukhin] is work-
ing in the Duma now, and I hope he will be continuing;
this is exceptionally responsible work.

[Presenter] There remain 200 laws from the previous
Duma.

[Zyuganov] No, the key issues — wherever we have
been, people put first prices, personal public security,
and unemployment. Our first steps involve bringing in
basic order, providing security. If we do not guarantee
these rights, there is no point in all the rest. There has to
be public assistance, targeted assistance, for those who
this winter are ultimately unable to make ends meet —

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 11

people with big families, the disabled, the elderly, and
so on.

Another key issue is the power structure. The present
government is essentially neither overseen nor account-
able and the president has so many powers that a per-
fectly healthy and very highly experienced person would
not be able to cope. We will therefore be striving to
deal with things by not making the situation more diffi-
cult, but by strengthening the oversight functions of the
State Duma and at the same time being ready to give
the greatest powers to the executive and administrative
authorities, if they can be unitary and not duplicated as
at present. You know that the president’s team makes
decisions, so does the government, but neither takes re-
sponsibility for the outcome.

[Presenter] So, Gennadiy Andreyevich. If the forces of
the left are going to be so well represented in the State
Duma, have so many seats, many people are naturally
going to be worried that the pendulum will swing in
the opposite direction, that there will be many different
forces and movements, so people are of course worried
about stability and continuity. Will this not mean new
tendencies in the State Duma, above all in the power
structure, and too great shocks?

[Zyuganov] We discussed this matter today with Rus-
sian business to create conditions for very efficient work
regardless of the kind of ownership and for normal or-
ganization of life. We will be trying to do this. In my
view, a very solid left-center coalition could take shape.
A coalition, on the one hand, of people who are well
aware that without justice and friendship among peo-
ples there can be no prosperity in Russia, and on the
other hand, of people with experience, pragmatism, and
a knowledge of government.

In our team we have Aman Tuleyev, Petr Romanov,
Svetlana Goryacheva, Viktor Ilyukhin — who has a
good knowledge of security, Nikolay Gubenko, and a
whole series of uther people who have proven they are
able to operate effectively, both under the old system
and in the new conditions. [passage omitted]

Faction

were not unexpected to Alevtina Fedulova, leader of the
Women of Russia movement.

Commenting on the Central Electoral Commission re-
port that the Women of Russia movement his polled
tentatively 5.5 percent of the vote on party lists, as of
05.00 Moscow time (02.00 GMT), in 45 regions of the
country, Fedulova told Itar-Tass that the polling results
“confirm the initial estimates to a certain extent".

1993” (when the Women of Russia movement polled
8.13 percent of the vote) and will enable the movement
to enlarge its faction in the State Duma.

Fedulova reafffirmed the movement’s position — not to
ally with any associations — since she does not see so
far a political force which would regard the women as
partners in politics, "not just as the electorate”.

Regional Activities

Regional Election Results in Keeping With
‘General Picture’

LD1812112495 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
1110 GMT 18 Dec 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW December 18
(ITAR-TASS) — Preliminary information about the
results of the elections to the State Duma received by
ITAR-TASS correspondents in Russian regions are in
keeping with the general picture of the results of the
voting presented by the Central Electoral Commission.

Communists are leading in many subjects of the Feder-
ation on federal tickets. This situation is characteristic
not only of Siberia and the Far East but also of Cen-
tral Russia. For instance, the Communist Party of the
Russian Federation is leading by a large margin in the
Tambovsk region (35 per cent), Bryansk region (33.8
percent), Belgorod region (29.6 per cent), Smolensk

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 13

region (about 25 per cent). Communists earned approx-
imately 20 per cent of votes in the Samara, Voronezh,
Leningrad, Vladimir and some other regions in the Eu-
ropean part of Russia.

The LDPR [Liberal Democratic Party of Russia] is
ahead of Communists by some 1.5 per cent in the
Maritime territory. It earned 20.2 per cent of votes.
Yabloko bloc is leading in the Kamchatka peninsula so
far. In keeping with experts’ forecast, the leader in the
Sverdlovsk region is Transformation of the Fatherland
movement formed by the region’s governor Eduard
Rossel.

The democratic forces have the strongest positions in
Moscow and in St. Petersburg, while they have not been
able to overcome the five per cent hurdle in Russian
provinces.

There have so far been only sketchy reports about lead-
ers in one- seat constituencies. Moreover, they are pre-
liminary. Among the well-known personalities who are
ahead of competitors are Speaker of the State Duma
Ivan Rybkin (the Voronezh region), Russian Foreign
Minister Andrey Kozyrev who ran in the Murmansk
region, Boris Yeltsin’s associate in the recent past Gen-
nadiy Burbulis (the Sverdlovsk region), prominent eye
surgeon Svyatoslav Fedorov (Chuvashia), Far Eastern
entrepreneur Valentin Tsoy (the Khabarovsk territory),
former Speaker of the USSR Supreme Soviet Anatoliy
Lukyanov (the Smolensk region), USSR ex-Premier
Nikolay Ryzhkov (the Belgorod region), General Al-
bert Makashov (the Samara region), leader of St. Peters-
burg regional committee of the Communist Party of the
Russian Federation Yuriy Belov (the Leningrad region),
journalist Aleksandr Nevzorov (the Pskov region).

It is characteristic that preference in one-seat constituen-
cies is given to local chiefs as the population hopes that
they will exert efforts toward improving their life.

High Turnout in Far East, Low Turnout in North
Viewed

LD1712134495 Moscow /TAR-TASS in English
1328 GMT 17 Dec 95

(By Tamara Zamyatina]

([FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW December 17
(ITAR-TASS) — Explaining the reasons for a surpris-
ingly high turnout at the elections to the State Duma,
experts from the analytical centre at the staff of the
Russian president give two possible explanations. “It
may be the result either of the fear to see the present
authorities replaced by new ones, or of a protest against

the policy, pursued by the government.”

Emil Pain, head of the special task group created at the
analytical centre specially for the elections, told TASS
that “the growth of the turnout in comparison with 1993
in the Far East and Siberia is exceedingly high — by
five to 10 per cent." The voting is over in that part of
the country.

Against this background low turnout in the northern ar-
eas — the Magadan Region, Norilsk and the Taymyr
Peninsula — is especially noticeable. Leonid Smyrnya-
gin, head of the department of regional problems at the
analytical centre, made a telephone call to Norilsk and
found out that on the eve of December 17 Anatoliy Fila-
tov, director of the Norilsk Metallurgical Complex, had
said in his TV address that he was not going to take
part in the voting in protest ag<inst the policy of the
local governor and indirectly urged residents of the city
to boycott the elections. By 14.00 local time, only 2 per
cent of electors cast their ballots, Smyrnyagin learned.

The authorities of the Krasnoyarsk Region, Norilsk
and Taymyr have some differences on the problem of
administrative borders between their territories, and, in
the opinion of Smyrnyagin, the low turnout is the result
of that territorial dispute.

A low turnout in the Magadan Region and other
northern areas may be explained by the fact that the
extreme north is now being supplied with food and other

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

essentials far worse than two years ago. Smyrnyagin
believes that the situation in the Magadan region can be
described as the evacuation of the population: from five
to ten per cent of the population leave it every year.

The task group of the analytical centre is accumulating
information coming from representatives of the presi-
dent in all the regions of the country, from the Central
Electoral Commission, the department for the work with
territories at the staff of the president, from world, Rus-
sian and regional news agencies. Experts of the group
will refrain from making public the trends in the voting
by party tickets until 23.00 of today.

ITAR-TASS Describes Voting Situation at 0900
GMT

LD1712132595 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
1231 GMT 17 Dec 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW December 17
(ITAR-TASS) — By 1200, Moscow time [0900 GMT],
some three million people have already cast their
ballots. A total number of electors is 28.7 million.
Consequently, according to the information of the
Central Electoral Commission, the turnout was 9.8 per
cent by midday.

The figure reflects only the situation in 16 subjects of the
federation, mostly Eastern regions of the country. The
turnout was rather high in the Amur Region (44.6 per
cent). The figure for the Aguinsk Autonomous District
of Buryatia is 42 per cent, for the Jewish Autonomous
District also 42 per cent, for the Ust-Orda Autonomous
District of Buryatia 40 per cent, and in the Chukotsk
Region 34 per cent.

Elections in the Chechen Republic may actually be con-
sidered valid, which was pointed out with satisfaction
by President Yeltsin at the polling station.

Commenting on the elections in Chechnya, Oleg Lobov
told journalists today after he cast his ballot, that "the
activity of electors surpassed the expectations not only
of the opponents, but also of supporters of the elections,
which is evidence of people's striving for peace."
Speaking about a possible outcome of the elections
in the republic, he said that, most probably, Doku
Zavgayev will be elected head of the republic by a large
margin.

By 1200, Moscow Time, 70 per cent of officers and men
took part in the voting, the Defence Ministry reported.
According to the information received by TASS, a
working group for organizing elections in the Armed
Forces summed up the first results. According to their
information, 40,000 servicemen took part in the early
voting, specifically those who are now in Chechnya,

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

officers and men serving in the missile troops, who
are on duty today, the peacekeeping troops in Bosnia,
sailors deployed at the Camragn naval base in Vietnam
and those from the northern naval squadron, who are
getting prepared for a voyage to the Mediterranean.

Sir Peter Emery, British MP and head of the OSCE
delegation of observers, who are monitoring the elec-
tions to the State Duma, believes that there will be no
vote rigging or violations in the course of the elections.
Responding to a question of TASS, Sir Peter Emery
stressed that he had been convinced by his observations
at polling station No. 2702, situated in secondary school
No. 1130, as well as by his two-hour conversation with
Chairman of the Central Electoral Commission Nikolay

Ryabov.

Voting is now going on in the whole of the Russian
Federation — from the Chukotsk Peninsula, where it
has practically been over, to the Kaliningrad Region, a
Russian enclave, including Russian polar stations. Polar
workers from the Vostok and Mirny polar stations were
the first to vote in Antarctica. 150 Russian citizens doing
research work there, as well as sailors from research
vessels, have a chance to fulfil their civic duty.

The voting is taking place in an elated atmosphere,
partially because of good weather: true Russian winter
came today to about all the regions of the country.

Unlike previous elections, the atmosphere at Tyumen
polling stations, where numerous students are voting, is
rather excited. Programmes of political parties and blocs
are not the only thing that attracted them. A special
lottery is being held there, which was organized by the
student council of the Younger Generation for Elections
and Ovation movements. Prizes include 600 tickets to
the Nautilus Pompilius music group, which is extremely
popular in Tyumen, to disc and youth clubs. Besides,
Academician Kuntsev, Rector of Tyumen University,
instituted a special prize of one million roubles for the
one who gives a most accurate forecast of the results of
the voting.

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 15

permanently or temporarily in western Ukraine, will
come to the polls, but we have a list of some 200 people
who have the right to vote. The situation will become
clear before 2200," Consul General of Russia Anatoliy
Kovalev told TASS.

People working at polling station No. 162 in Odessa,
in southern Ukraine, do not know either how many
people are going to vote. Their list is longer, however,
because it includes people on business trips, sailors and
Russian citizens who just happen to stay at the Odessa
port now. Another mobile polling station No.165 opened
in Simferopol. It is also under the supervision of the
consulate-generai in Odessa.

People living in Turkmenia, who have double citizen-
ship — that of Turkmenia and Russia — will take part
for the first time in Russian parliamentary elections.

high.

have not been seen at polling stations this morning."

Central Electoral Commission Outlines Elections

LD1512154495 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1426 GMT 15 Dec 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Dec 15 (INTER-
FAX) — On December 17, simultaneously with parlia-
mentary elections, elections to the supreme representa-
tive and executive bodies will be held in Russia's con-
stituent entities, the Central Electoral Commission told
INTERFAX.

In 15 constituent entities, including the Altai, Buryatia,
Kabardino-Balkaria, Mordovia, Karelia, Khakas-
sia, the Krasnodar territory, and the Vladimir,
Voronezh, Kostroma, Lipetsk, Nizhniy Novgorod,
Omsk, Sverdlovsk and Tambov regions, elections to
the local self-government bodies will be held.

Referendums will be held in six constituent members of
the Federation.

A referendum on land ownership and on independence
will be held in Bashkortostan.

A referendum will also be held in Mordovia. The citi-
zens are to state whether they support the idea of pro-
longing the powers of the republic’s State Assembly,
local governments and the leaders of the local govern-
ments until December 1999.

In the Volgograd region a referendum will be held on
the charter of the city of Vologda and in the Kemerovo
region - on the charters of the local governments.

A referendum will be held in the Kursk region on
the organization of local self-government, and in the
Chelyabinsk region on the charters of the municipal
authorities.

Heads of the regional administration will be elected
in 13 regions, including the Maritime territory, and
the Belgorod, Lipetsk, Moscow, Novosibirsk, Orenburg,
Tambov, Tver, Tomsk, Yaroslav! and Omsk regions.
Governors will be elected in the Novgorod and Nizhniy
Novgorod regions, and a mayor in Yaroslavl.

The elections of deputies to the supreme legislative
bodies will be held in 13 constituent entities.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

18 DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

Bashkortostan will elect deputies to the State Assembly
from one electoral district, and Buryatia — to the
People’s Khural, also from one electoral district.

In Tuva deputies will be elected to the Supreme Khural
from two electoral districts and in Chuvashia — to the
State Council from 14 electoral districts.

The Maritime territory will elect deputies to the ter-
ritorial Duma from nine electoral districts, and the
Khabarovsk territory — from six electoral districts. In
the Belgorod region deputies will be elected to the re-
gional Duma from three electoral districts and in the
Volgograd region — from two electoral districts.

Deputies will be elected to the regional dumas in the
Moscow and Murmansk regions from two electoral dis-
tricts, and in the Novgorod region from eight electoral
districts. The Ulyanovsk region will elect deputies to the
regional legislative assembly and the Taimyr (Dolgano-
Nenets) Autonomous District will elect deputies to the
district Duma from three electoral districts.

Zyuganov Hopes for Success in Single-Mandate
Districts

LD1812122195 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1037 GMT 18 Dec 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW, Dec 18 (INTER-
FAX) — The leader of the Russian Communist Party
Gennadiy Zyuganov ‘opes that the list of the future
Communist group in the Duma will be considerably
expanded at the expense of deputies elected in single-
mandate districts.

"We expect a good addition from single-mandate dis-
tricts," he told INTERFAX Monday [18 December]
morning.

The preliminary election returns (over 20% of voters
supported Communists) are in line with his expectations.

"We are happy that we were supported by primordial
Russian territories - Central Russia, the Volga region,
the Kuznetsk coal basin, and this should make everyone
think,” Zyuganov said.

He hoped that Agrarians and Women of Russia would
clear the 5% barrier because the Communist Party
regards them as allies.

Zyuganov accounted the poor performance of the Con-
gress of Russian Communities to the fact that the move-
ment started operating only half a year ago and has not
won the necessary influence. "This time Russian citizens
evidently preferred to stake on better known parties,” he
felt.

Zyuganov thinks the Communist group in the Duma will
be the biggest and start out by taking steps to improve

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

the general situation in the country and in the economy.
Among the key problems he named the growth of prices,
crime and unemployment.

“We are not dropping the question of stopping hostilities
in Chechnya from the agenda,” he stressed. "I am
satisfied that elections were held there and such an
experienced and restrained politician as Doku Zavgayev
was elected its leader. People realized that it is better
to vote by casting ballots than using guns,” Zyuganov
declared.

Observers To Visit Polls in Moscow, Regions

LD1612123195 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
1211 GMT 16 Dec 95

(By Yuriy Kozlov and Vladimir Suprun]

{FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW, December 16
(ITAR-TASS) — Foreign observers plan to visit nearly
all polling stations in the Russian capital on the election
day to the State Duma (Russian parliament lower
house), Bernard Owen, secretary-general of the Paris
centre of comparative studies on holding elections, said
in an interview with ITAR-TASS here on Saturday.

He came to Moscow as an expert to coordinate activ-
ities of evidently the most numerous group of foreign
observers from among representatives of member coun-
tries of the Organisation for Security and Cooperation
in Europe, the European Union and other prestigious
international organisations.

Owen said that his group consisted of 434 observers
from 32 European countries as well as the United States
and Canada. Over a third of them (155) will work in
Moscow, another 65 — in St. Petersburg and the rest
(214) — in other Russian regions.

He noted that observers would seek to visit the greatest
possible number of polling stations across Russia and
plan to go to 91 constituencies out of 225. Observers
will be represented in 45 subjects of the Russian
Federation.

According to Owen, the tasks of observers are defined
very clearly and spelled out in a special booklet which
each of them has.

They are to turn up at a polling station on the election
day, meet, on the spot, representatives of election
commissions and law enforcement bodies, members of
political parties and non-government organisations as
well as to talk with rank-and-file voters.

Observers will be also present at ballot counting at all
the levels up to the Central Election Commission.

The aim of the group also is to show the great attention
paid by the world community to the Russian elections,
Owen continued.

Besides, the effect of the presence of foreign observers
is both important and extremely useful to a society

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

20 DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

following the road of strengthening democracy, he
stressed.

The elections over, foreign observers will submit indi-
vidual reports, on the basis of which a general picture
will be gleaned and appropriate conclusions drawn. The
final report is planned to be drafted and published by
next January 7.

According to information received by ITAR-TASS
at the Russian Foreign Ministry, over 900 observers
from more than 50 countries and 10 international
organisations were accredited to the Central Election
Commission.

Agrarian Leader: Most Parties Want Land
Redistribution

LD1512140195 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1131 GMT 15 Dec 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text) DUBNEVO, the Moscow
region, Dec 15 (INTERFAX) — Agrarian Party leader
Mikhail Lapshin warned that most of the parties and
blocs taking part in the December 17 elections are in
favor of land redistribution in Russia, as he met with
residents in his home village of Dubnevo in the Stupino
district of the Moscow region.

"The election platforms of most parties and election
blocs are pushing Russia towards another redistribution
of land,” he said.

Lapshin strongly criticized what he described as
“witches” sabath" over the referendum on private land
ownership in the run-up to the elections by the leaders
of Russia Is Our Home (Viktor Chernomyrdin), the
Democratic Choice of Russia (Yegor Gaydar), and the
Party of Russian Unity and Accord (Sergey Shakhray).

Lapshin said their allies who supported the disastrous
reform policy wanted the sale of land, also including
farmland, but concealed their intentions.

He said that the Yabloko movement (leader Grigoriy
Yavlinskiy) dreamed of a market of land and that
the Party of Economic Freedom (leader Konstantin
Borovoy) wanted unrestricted ownership and uncon-
trolled purchase and sale of land.

Lapshin believes that farm-workers should be very
cautious about the proposal of the Communist Party
(leader Gennadiy Zyuganov) for the restoration of
national ownership of land, because “we already know
what this is all about."

Lapshin believes that avoiding another redistribution
of land, something that always led to bloodshed in
Russia, will be possible only if a new parliament elected

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

on December 17 adopts the land code drafted by the
agrarian deputies.

Earlier, the State Duma turned down the draft after the
second reading. The draft permits the purchase and sale
of small plots taken by individual country gardens and
land taken by private homes and garages.

Federal Border Service Election Arrangements
Reported

MK1612124395 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian
15 Dec 95 p2

{Report by Yuriy Golotyuk: "Russian Border Guards
Cast Ballots at Any Cost. Delivery of Ballots May
Become Combat Operation"]

[FBIS Translated Text] Your SEGODNYA correspon-
dent has come to learn that about 1,000 enlisted men and
officers in the Russian Federation border troops have al-
ready voted. They include the crews of four patrol ships
on duty at sea and the personnel of 15 frontier posts and
security outposts who cannot be anywhere close to bal-
lot boxes on election day for purely objective reasons.
It is true that nobody can so far tell who they cast their
ballots for because the ballot boxes with the marked
ballots will only be opened the night of 17 December.
According to Colonel General Nikolay Bordyuzha, Rus-
sian Federation Federal Border Service [FPS] deputy di-
rector, this is, however, only a small part of the “border
guard electorate," which comprises about 200,000 vot-
ers.

There will be 39 polling stations functioning on Sua-
day at Russian border guard units (eight of them in
Tajikistan, 4 in Kyrgyzstan, 10 in Georgia, and 12 in
Armenia [figures as published]). A majority of border

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

guards will be casting ballots at 676 open, civilian sta-
tions designated by the FPS for its subordinates. Even
those currently guarding the Chechnya border will be
able to vote at ordinary polling stations although lo-
cated on the territory of Dagestan and Ingushetia.

Certain difficulties have arisen in the northernmost Rus-
sian frontier post of Nagurskaya located on Franz Josef
Land: It has so far been impossible to deliver ballot
sheets there because of the polar night and extremely
unfavorable weather conditions. Nevertheless, they say
in the FPS, “there are two voting options, approved
by the Central Electoral Commission (incidentally, it
is not ruled out that bundles with ballot sheets may be
air-dropped). Ballot sheets for Russian border guards
in Tajikistan at alpine frontier posts have made a no
less difficult journey: Shipping them, according to the
FPS, has proven to be a veritable “combat operation.”
Since airiifting them to the Pamir was prevented by bad
weather, the forms had to be routed to Osh in Kyrgyzs-
tan, whence they had to be brought to the Russian fron-
tier posts in the Pamir along the alpine trails running
across mountain passes at 4,000 meters above sea level
accompanied by armored vehicles (since that section of
the mountains lies in the zone of combats between the
Tajik opposition and the government forces).

Despite all the difficulties, according to Mr. Bordyuzha,
“all the formalities called for by the election law will
be impeccably observed among the troops.” Notably,
as the general put it, “none of the electoral blocs or
associations was allowed to campaign on the territory
of FPS units although such requests have come to the

headquarters from practically all of them.”

Yeltsin ‘Foe’ Goryacheva Elected To Duma From
Far East

LD1812024895 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
0211 GMT 18 Dec 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text] VLADIVOSTOK, December
18 (ITAR-TASS) — Svetlana Goryacheva, the ally-
turned-foe of President Boris Yeltsin, was elected over-
whelmingly to the State Duma in the one-seat con-
stituency in Vladivostok in the Far East, according to
early returns.

Tass learned from the local election commission on
Monday that Goryacheva, the No. 2 person in the list of
the Communist party of Gennadiy Zyuganov, won over
50 per cent of the vote in the region.

Goryacheva, born in 1947, became Yeltsin’s deputy
in 1990 when he was the speaker of the Russian
parliament. She was widely viewed as his closest ally,
but in February 1991 she sharply criticised the policy
of the Russian leader and switched to the opposition.
On August 31, 1991 Goryacheva resigned, joined the
Communist party and worked in her native Far Eastern
region since then.

She succeeded to become one of the most popular
politicians in the Far East. She enjoys the reputation
of the “most honest person" there.

{[FBIS Transcribed Text?! MOSCOW, Dec 17
(INTERFAX-EURASIA, — Elections to Russia's
State Duma have iasen place in the majority of

The percentage of voters who took part in the elections

At present the greatest activity is noted in the regions

Elections in Bashkortostan (near the Urals) are coming
to a close. On the average about 49 percent of voters
took part in the elections in this republic and 30 percent
of voters in the Sverdlovsk region.

gion voting districts reporting in.

YEKATERINBURG, Dec 17 (INTERFAX-EURASIA)
— A negative assessment of the elections to Russia's
State Duma by foreign monitors may hinder Russia’s
admittance to the European Union, EU monitors said at
a press conference in Yekaterinburg.

Four representatives of the European Union intend to
follow elections in Yekaterinburg and the Sverdlovsk
region where there are seven single mandate districts. At
the same time they said that overseeing the calculation
process at the State Duma elections is of an estimate
nature. Foreign monitors have no legal influence over
the outcome.

They declared that they have appropriate experience of
work in Russia in order to present an objective analysis
of the democratic process in Russia and, in particular,
in the Urals.

tenths of a percent.

The voter registers in St. Petersburg included 3,675,455
names, and about 60 percent of them went to the polls.
When the ballot boxes were opened, 1,943,070 ballot
papers were recognised as valid.

‘Scandal’ as Polling Station Runs Out of Ballot
Papers

LD1712183695 St. Petersburg Fifth Channel
Television Network in Russian 1630 GMT 17 Dec 95

[Video report by "Inform-TV” correspondent Nadezhda
Khokhlova]

{FBIS Translated Text] [Khokhlova] What happened
at many polling stations in Moskovskiy district today,
including the ones we visited, can certainly be called
a scandal. The election organizers were clearly caught
out by the high turnout of citizens. Polling stations ran
out of ballot papers at 1200. People waited in vain for
voting to start up again, but after two to three hours
many of them just went home and so did not vote. The
most determined tried to get justice.

[Begin recording] [Male voter] I tried to ring the city
Electoral Commission. I got through, but at first no one
answered the telephone. I rang for five minutes. I began
to demand that someone give me a number in Moscow
where I could contact Nikolay Ryabov at the Central
Electoral Commission. They all told me that they were
not in communication. How can it be that they are not in
communication with the Central Electoral Commission
in Moscow on elections day?

[Female voter] How many people left?

[Second female voter] Half the people.

[Third female voter] That is why they do it, so that
people do not vote. [end recording)

{Khokhlova] Packets of ballot papers were brought from
the Moskovskiy district administration at 1500. They
just needed to be given out to the voters.

[Begin V.A. Gerbolinskiy recording, deputy head of
the Moskovskiy District administration] This is not a
provocation. Of course, the turnout is, fortunately, better
than even we expected. [end recording]

{[Khokhlova] All these mistakes and violations could
lead to the election results being deemed invalid. [video
shows queues of people at a polling station inside a
sports hall]

Petersburg Commission Warns Voters About
Slander

LDI712103095 St. Petersburg Fifth Channel
Television Network in Russian 1630 GMT 16 Dec 95

(FBIS Translated Text] Just before we came on the air,
we received a statement from the electoral commission
for Northwestern Constituency No. 209, Constituency
Commission No. 209, for the northwestern electoral
constituency of St. Petersburg, has received informa-
tion that certain individuals have acquired voters’ home
telephone numbers and are telephoning them, identify-
ing themselves as the staff of a city sociological center.
While talking about the forthcoming elections, they are
deliberately spreading slanderous reports about a num-
ber of candidates running for the State Duma.

Given that under electoral law, all campaigning is cat-
egorically forbidden after 0000 hours on 16 December,
the electoral commission regards such telephone calls as
provocations and asks voters to be on their guard and
to take information about candidate deputies only from
legal sources. The city prosecutor's office is verifying
the incidents mentioned.

Chechnya Voting Activity Deemed ‘Exceptional’

LD1712190395 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
1841 GMT 17 Dec 95

[By Sergey Bazhenov]

{FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, December 17
(ITAR-TASS) — The chairman of Chechnya’ election
commission, Abdulkerim Arsakhanov, described as ex-
ceptional the voting activity in Chechnya’s co-elections
of its new leadership and of the Russian State Duma.

The elections, over at 15.00 p.m. today, have been far
more successful and smooth than expected, Arsakhanov
said.

He said there was a flood of people to Groznyy on the
eve of the elections. They were coming from remote
villages, where tension prevented full freedom of voting.

As a result, today’s turnout in Groznyy proved above
the figure of city’s population, exceeding 100 per cent,
Arsakhanov said.

He sounded sure that the elections have been valid, even
though the counting of votes is only in progress.

The commission chairman added that return of ballot
papers from some areas was problematic at nighttime.

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

Tatarstan Opposition Does Not Expect Fair
Elections

MKI1612160795 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian
16 Dec 95 p2

[Report by Igor Kotov from Kazan: "Opposition in
Tataria Anticipates Dirty Tricks by Authorities. Officials
Continue Tending to Their Business Undisturbed"]

{[FBIS Translated Text] The electoral fever in Kazan
is hardly felt. However, it would also be wrong to
say that absolutely nothing is happening. Communists,
holding loudspeakers, can be seen in the squares, streets,
city transport terminals, and by the gates of factories.
The social democrats, competing with them, have found
to their vexation that their opponents are in the lead.
They believe that despite massive supplies of printed
materials from Moscow with texts by Mr. Zhirinovskiy,
the Liberal Democratic Party is quickly losing support
in Kazan.

Meanwhile, on the eve of the elections, the NDR’s
{Russia Is Our Home] Tataria branch set up a youth
Organization headed by the last leader of the Tataria
Komsomol [Young Communists League] and the in-
cumbent chairman of the State Committee for Children
and Young People, Marat Bareyev. If it is to be recalled
that the older comrades of the young NDR adherents in
Tatarstan are led by the deputy premier of the govern-
ment, the question arises as to how the provisions of Ar-
ticle 45 of the election law can be implemented, since it
prohibits state officials from conducting electoral cam-
paign propaganda. The aforementioned state officials are
then confronted with the dilemma of either violating the
law or party discipline. Judging by the fact that on 15
December all local papers were unanimous in carrying
an interview with President Mintimer Shaymiyev ten-
tatively outlining the advantages of the NDR, the first
option seems more suitable to the local state officials.

The opposition, no matter what its political bias, antici-
pates a dirty trick from the authorities, as usual. Among
democrats, as well as in the communist camp, rumors
are persistent that the chairs of the local polling stations’
electoral commissions are being instructed to view com-
mission members appointed by candidates and electoral
associations as ordinary observers and not to certify the
protocols given out to the observers. The feeble hopes
for honest elections are pinned by the opposition on the
work of a small observer group from France. On elec-
tion day, the French intend to meet local journalists,
but it looks as if they are more willing to listen than to
speak, since it is already clear that everything shown to
them will be in strict conformity with the law.

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

28 DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

Tatarstan Election Campaign Viewed

MM1512140295 (Internet) VYBORY-95 WWW
in Russian 30 Nov-6 Dec 95

[Unattributed report: "Republic of Tatarstan”)
(FBIS Translated Text)

Political Publicity

Unlike the display material of March 1995 (at the time
of the election campaign for the Russian Federation
State Duma — elections were not held in the republic
in December 1993 — editor), the present election
campaigning appears extremely pallid. The leaflets
of the candidates for single-seat districts — Sergey
Shashurin, Aleksandr Saliy, Boris Isayev, and Fatikh
Sibagatullin — seldom appear on the streets at present.
There is virtually no publicity material for the electoral
blocs’ local candidates, and to all appearances they do
not have the funds for campaigning. It is this which
accounts for the absence of publicity materials in the
press.

In terms of display material and publicity in the press the
Agrarian Party and the KRO [Congress of Russian Com-
munities] are presently in the lead. Leaflets and posters
have appeared for the DVR-OD [Russia’s Democratic
Choice-United Democrats], “Stable Russia," the Pam-
filova Bloc, the Beer Lovers’ Party, and the “Bloc of
Independents." They are mainly being distributed in
Kazan’s higher educational institutions — which is not
entirely appropriate, since the majority of students will
hardly participate in the elections.

A. Ishkuvatov, publisher of the newspaper KRIS, who
is running on the lists of the “Forward, Russia!”
movement, has started pre-election campaigning for
Boris Fedorov by publishing several materials. KRIS
is a partly free newspaper with a print run of 200,000
copies.

It is curious that there is practically no NDR [Russia Is
Our Home] publicity, and a trailer featuring Mikhalkov
bewilders a lot of people, since the majority of the
population simply does not realize that it is an NDR
trailer.

Visits

The leaders of two electoral associations visited
Tatarstan 29 November: Nikolay Ryzhkov ("Power
to the People!") and Vladimir Burenin (“Duma-96").
On 29 November Nikolay Ryzhkov visited the city of
Naberezhnyye Chelny, where he met with voters, and
he arrived in Kazan 30 November. During his two-day
visit he met with Farid Mukhametshin (prime minister
of the Republic of Tatarstan) and with workers of the

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

KAPO [expansion unknown] Plant and gave a news
conference. Another news conference was held in
Kazan the same day — with Vladimir Burenin, leader
of the “Duma-96" Association, and Albert Bikmullin,
leader of the "Preduralye" regional list (a resident of
Kazan). The leaders of this bloc are not counting on a
sensational success in the elections. Advocating prag-
matism in all spheres of the life of society, they have
most likely prepared the ground for future elections.

Information on Candidates

1. Boris Mikhaylovich Isayev, candidate for the
Privolzhskiy Electoral District. He was born 18 June
1935 in the city of Mikhaylovka, Ryazan Oblast, he is
Russian, he has had higher education, and he graduated
from Ryazan Agricultural Institute in 1959.

During 1959-1962 he worked in engineering posts
at cuterprises in Ryazan Oblast. In 1962 he moved
to Kazan and worked in Tatarstan for 23 years, 12
of them as deputy and first deputy chairman of the
Tatar Autonomous Soviet Socialist Republic Council of
Ministers.

In 1985 he was appointed Russian first deputy minister
of grain products. During 1988-1990 he worked in the
city of Chelyabinsk as chairman of the oblast soviet
executive committee and the oblast soviet. In 1990, at
the First Congress of Russian Federation Deputies, he
was elected deputy chairman of the Russian Federation
Supreme Soviet. In 1991, after signing the political
statement of disagreement with. B.N. Yeltsin's policy
of reforms, he resigned. Boris Isayev is now president
of the Association of Deputies and the permanent
representative of the Tatarstan Ministry of Agricultural
Industry in Moscow. He is married with two sons and
three grandchildren.

2. Aleksandr Ivanovich Saliy was born into a worker's
family 9 January 1952, is Russian, and has had higher
military political education. In 1976 he graduated from
Kurgan Military Political Aviation School. In 1986 he
graduated with distinction from the social psychology
department of the Military Political Academy [adyunk-
tura], but he was not allowed to defend his dissertation
because he took part in the 3 February demonstration in
Moscow.

He served in the Army for 24 years. He started
as a private. In August 1994 he received an early
discharge from the Armed Forces with the rank of
lieutenant colonel for his leadership of the Communist
Party of Tatarstan. His wife is a medical worker, his
daughter a student, and his son a schoolboy. He is a
committed communist, inclined toward fanaticism, and

Russia for the Soviet Union, 4.6 - for Russia is Our
Home.

general in Narva. The number of voters could be much
greater if Estonia’s authorities had satisfied numerous
requests of the Russian party to open additional electoral
districts in the cities densely populated with Russian
citizens.

Communists Reportedly Leading in Baltic Voting
LD1812013895 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
0122 GMT 18 Dec 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW, December 18
(ITAR-TASS) — Russians in the three Baltic republics
voted overwhelmingly for communists and nationalists,
leaving only some ten percent for the reformists.

The communist party of Gennadiy Zyuganov enjoyed
36 percent of support in Latvia, Estonia and Lithuania
and the nationalist Liberal Democratic party of Vladimir
Zhirinovskiy and the Congress of Russian Communities
gained 34 and 21 percent respectively, according to
early returns.

The reformist Yabloko bloc of Grigoriy Yavlinskiy won
only 5 percent, while the Our Home is Russia failed to
overcome the 5 percent hurdle so far. It enjoyed only 4
percent of votes, the NTV network reported on Monday.

Polling Stations for Elections Open in Estonia

LD1712100395 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
0738 GMT 17 Dec 95

[By Albert Maloveryan]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text) TALLINN December 17
(ITAR-TASS) — Two polling stations open in Estonia
today, where Russian citizens living in Estonia will
be able to vote in the elections to the State Duma.
Both polling stations are attached to the Vsevoloshtsky
constituency of the Leningrad Region.

Some 82,000 Russian citizens are permanently living in
Estonia. According to the Russian Embassy information,
it is the largest community of Russians living abroad.
About 95 per cent of them have the right to vote, but
not all of them will be able to make use of it, because,
despite the appeals of the Russian Embassy, the local
authorities permitted to open only two polling stations
— in the Russian Embassy in Tallinn and in the general
consulate in Narva. This almost automatically bars from
the elections the Russian citizens living in other cities
of Estonia.

‘Double Victory’ for Communists in Jewish Region

1037 GMT 18 Dec 95

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] BIROBIJAN, Dec 18
(INTERFAX-Eurasia) — The Duma elections in the

victory of Communists.

Party committee Sergey Shtogrin collected a majority

organized. By 11 a.m. Monday only one complaint was
registered by the supporter group of Alla Gerber who
said the candidate’s biography was unavailable at some
polling stations.

Prepoll Political Machinations in Bryansk Region
Eyed

MMI1512144695 Moscow ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI
(“Political Milieu” Special Edition) in Russian

14 Dec 95 p4

[Report by Vasiliy Shpachkov: "Bryansk: A Glass of
Vodka to the Victims of Chernobyl"]}

{[FBIS Translated Excerpt] One year ago
ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI recounted the story of the
disappearance of the Chernobyl billion [rubles]. An
audit of the financial and economic activity of the Main
Administration for Chernobyl Questions established
that construction materials and equipment worth more
than 1 billion rubles (in 1991-1994 prices) had been
squandered. The inquiry established that the amount
of work performed had been exaggerated, and that
inordinate sums of money had been spent on main-
taining the functionary apparatus, acquiring equipment
and furniture for the main administration, and so forth.
Who is to blame for this? Who dared to profit from the
grief of unfortunate people?

Alas, after many months of inquiries and audits it turns
out that the mountain of threatening documents and
directives has once again brought forth a mouse. The
oblast public prosecutor’s office commissioned an audit
from the Audit Office, which sent the results of its
inquiries to the internal affairs administration; the oblast
internal affairs administration sent them all back to the
Audit Office. [passage omitted]

In the race for votes supporters of the Bryansk entrepre-
neur Oleg Kibalchich, notorious from many articles in
ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI, organized the sale of cut-price
sugar. All that was demanded of the custorner was that
he give his consent for his vote to be recorded on the
list of citizens nominating Kibalchich as a State Duma
candidate deputy. And although Kibalchich was even-
tually refused registration and "burned up" in the dense
strata of the election atmosphere, the fact remains that
this method was used.

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 31

Just as real is the report recently published in the
oblast newspaper, BRYANSK RABOCHIY. It turns out
that in Klintsy supporters of Zhirinovskiy organized
free drinks, inviting people just to vote for the LDPR
{Liberal Democratic Party of Russia] candidate at the
elections.

Well, a battle has always been a battle. And, as the
saying goes, the ends justify the means. Politicians
of all persuasions want power. However, it would be
naive to suppose that the Communists, having triumphed
at the elections, would immediately start to look for
those guilty of squandering the Chernobyl money. Just
like Zhirinovskiy’s supporters, they continue to offer
free refreshments to the inhabitants of Bryansk Oblast.
The usual election race for the “controlling block” of
political shares is under way.
