Election Guidelines
Ryabov Issues Message on Eve of Elections
LD1612174195 Moscow Russian Television Network
in Russian 1550 GMT 16 Dec 95

[Eve of election message by Nikolay Ryabov, head of
the Russian Federation Central Electoral Commission;
date and place not given — live or recorded]

(FBIS Translated Excerpt] Esteemed fellow country-
men. The election campaign is nearing completion. On

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

17 December 1995, on the day of the elections of the
deputies to the State Duma, over 96,000 electoral com-
missions at every level are ready to ensure your consti-
tutional right to power. Approximately 10,000 agents of
candidates, election associations and election blocs, tens
of thousands of observers, including around 1,000 from
abroad, and many thousands of representatives of the
mass media, are preparing for public supervision during
the elections.

The measures stipulated by the electoral law have
have been adopted within the timescale prescribed.
The necessary funds, equipment, and documentation
have been delivered to the polling stations where final
arrangements are being made. Communications have
been set up and the telecommunications network has
been connected. The polling stations and ballot centers
are being guarded.

Thus, every possible condition has been created for
the free expression of will by the Russian citizens
in the elections of deputies to the State Duma and
provision made for a public and open determination of
the outcome of the elections. It is now very much up to
every participant in the election campaign to make the
17 December election day its worthy culmination.

The Central Electoral Commission of the Russian Feder-
ation addresses the constituency electoral commissions
as the most numerous group of election organizers. In
exercising the powers granted to you in direct contact
with the 105 million electors, remember that we, your
colleagues, are beside you in all our thoughts and hearts
and are ready to help you and render the necessary as-
sistance. We realize how complex your task is, creating
the proper conditions for the citizens to vote, interact-
ing with observers and agents, and counting the votes
after a long period of intensive work for 24 hours or
maybe even longer in connection with holding the bal-
lot. Although we understand this, we call on you to
assess thoroughly and comprehensively every ballot pa-
per: any of them can prove to be decisive. We rely
on your competence and impartiality. We are confident
that, in the course of the ballot and in counting the votes,
you will be able to achieve perfect legal purity and to do
everything without any mistakes, publicly and openly.
Do muster your strength for this work to be completed
worthily. We have trust in you.

We urge the electoral commissions in constituent parts
of the Russian Federation, constituency and territorial
electoral commissions to ensure on the day of the
elections the due order of the ballot and to take
exhaustive steps in order to secure the electoral rights
of citizens, to adhere unconditionally to the principle
of independence of the electoral commissions, and to

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

create conditions for the work of observers, agents, and
representatives of the mass media. [passage omitted]

We ask the television companies and journalists to
provide an objective and balanced coverage of the work
of the electoral commissions, the course of the ballot,
counting the votes and establishing the results of the
elections.

Esteemed electors and citizens of the Russian Feder-
ation. For the first time in many centuries of Russian
history, our people have an opportunity to elect those
in power, to elect deputies of the country’s State Duma
within the constitutional timescale, freely and democrat-
ically. This is not simply a ballot and not simply another
election. On 17 December 1995 the Russian people lay
the foundation of their society and state in the form of
the most important democratic tradition which removes
from all of us the threat of social conflict and coup d'etat
and the danger of civil war. We call on you to take an
active part in the elections of the deputies to the State
Duma on 17th December. This is our day. This is the
day when every Russian freely determines by their will
who will be in power. We rely on the political wisdom
of the Russians.

On behalf of all of the country’s electoral commissions,
the Central Electoral Commission of the Russian Feder-
ation assures you that the final outcome of the elections
will be fully in accordance with the express wishes of
the electors and that it will be summed up and made
public in a timely fashion. We believe that every par-
ticipant in the election campaign, in whatever capacity
they act, realizes the responsibility of the moment that
has come. We wish all our compatriots peace, happi-
ness, accord in their families and society, and personal
well-being. Thank you for your attention.

Media To Carry Live Election Reports ‘Round The
Clock’

LD1612172895 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English

1711 GMT I6 Dec 95

[By Natalya Panshina]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, December 16
(ITAR-TASS) — Russia’s Central Electoral Commis-
sion has opened an information centre in downtown
Moscow on Saturday to help journalists keep track of
voting in the legislative elections scheduled for Sunday.
Commission .....-nan Nikolay Ryabov said the need
for such a centre _. -»™e obvious back in 1993 when

a whole army of counters failed to promptly process
information which amounted to about 100,000 pages.

Over 2,700 system administrators and about 1,000
specialists wili ensure multi-tier communications in

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 33

Russian regions. Computer centres hooked up to the
central terminal in Moscow have been opened in 87
Russian regions, except Chechnya and Kalmykia.

Ryabov stressed that because such a system will be used
in Russia for the first time, the traditional methods of
vote counting will also be employed to avoid errors.

The commission has already granted permission to use
the system to 1,132 Russian and foreign mass media,
including 450 Russian and 682 foreign journalists.

The elections will be covered by 17 news agencies, in-
Cluding the ORT, RTV and NTV television broadcasting
companies which will carry live reports from the centre
round the clock.

The first information from Russia’s Far East will
become available by noon Sunday. Preliminary results
from other regions will become known by 16:00, while
the first election summaries will start arriving after
polling stations in the Kaliningrad region, Russia’s
westernmost enclave, will close at 23:00.

According to Ryabov, Russia’s electorate is 105 mil-
lion. He said 89 local electoral commissions, 187 re-
gional electoral commissions, 2,719 territorial electoral
commissions and 93,122 polling stations have been set
up across Russia.

The commissions involve over 800,000 people who rep-
resent various public associations and working collec-
tives. Tens of thousands of observers, including about
1,000 from foreign countries, will monitor the elections.

Commission: Zhirinovskiy Inciting ‘Ethnic Hatred’

MM1512130895 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA
in Russian 15 Dec 95 Weekend Edition p 2

[Russian Federation Central Electoral Commission de-
cree No. 56/506 P "on the appeal from the Russian
Federation President’s Court of Appeal for Information
Disputes in Connection with the Speech by V.V. Zhiri-
novskiy, chairman of the Liberal Democratic Party of
Russia")

[FBIS Translated Text] The Russian Federation Central
Electoral Commission has received an appeal from the
Russian Federation president’s Court of Appeal for
Information Disputes on a speech by V.V. Zhirinovskiy,
chairman of the LDPR [Liberal Democratic Party of
Russia] within the framework of the election campaign

6 December 1995 in a morning broadcast on the Russian

Public Television channel.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

The Court of Appeal believes that some statements in
this speech by the LDPR chairman could be assessed
as a violation of the Russian Federation Constitution
and criminal legislation and has an abuse of the right
to conduct election campaigning within the meaning of
article 25 of the federal law “on basic guarantees of
Russian Federation citizens’ electoral rights."

The Russian Federation president’s Court of Appeal
for Information Disputes asks the Russian Federation
Central Electoral System to assess this speech by V.V.
Zhirinovskiy from the viewpoint of the requirements of
article 25 of the above-mentioned federal law and to
examine the question of the possibility and expediency
of applying the sanctions envisaged by part three of
article 46 of the federal law "on the election of Russian
Federation Federal Assembly State Duma deputies."

The Russian Federation Central Electoral Commission
has familiarized itself at its 9 December 1995 session
with the video tape of the speech by LDPR Chairman
V.V. Zhirinovskiy broadcast 6 December 1995 on Rus-
sian Public Television channel within the framework
of election campaigning and believes that V.V. Zhiri-
novskiy allowed expressions containing calls for vio-
lence inciting ethnic hostility, thus violating the relevant
clauses of federal legislation on the elections.

On the basis of the above and guided by the require-
ments of part three of article 46 of the federal law “on
the election of Russian Federation Federal Assembly
State Duma deputies," the Russian Federation Central
Electoral Commission decrees:

1. That LDPR Chairman V.V. Zhirinovskiy be warned
that if the LDPR continues illegal election campaigning
the Russian Federation Central Electoral Commission
in accordance with part three of article 46 of the
federal law "on the election of Russian Federation
Federal Assembly State Duma deputies” will appeal to

Election Monitoring Group Learns of Irregularities
LD1712091995 Moscow INFORMATSIONNOYE
AGENTSTVO EKHO MOSKVY in Russian

0720 GMT I7 Dec 95

(FBIS Translated Text] Viktor Luchin, acting chairman
of the Russian public organization For Honest Elections,
told Ekho Moskvy radio station that his organization
is getting dozens of reports of infringements of the
electoral law at polling stations in Russia.

One of the commonest types of infringement is the
presence at polling stations of campaign material in
favor of various individual candidates. Further, Luchin
said, at a number of polling stations the situation
regarding envelopes containing the ballot papers of
those «atitled to vote early is giving rise to dispute.
The Central Electoral Commission gave instructions to
electoral commissions at all levels to declare invalid
any ballot papers on which the surnames of candidates
in single-seat constituencies who decided not to run no
less than three days before the elections are not crossed
out. So when the envelopes containing the ballot papers
of people who voted before these candidates decided not
to run are opened, these ballot papers will be invalid. In
law, the envelopes must be opened as soon as the voting
Starts, but since the Central Electoral Commission has
not issued any instructions on this point, they have been
put aside for the moment, Luchin said.

Commission Head Rules Out ‘Massive’ Vote
Rigging

LD1712132395 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1128 GMT 17 Dec 95

{FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW, Dec 17 (INTER-
FAX) — Head of the Central Electoral Commission
Nikolay Ryabov speaking at a polling station in Moscow
has ruled out any chance of massive rigging of votes
from Russia’s general election getting under way on
Sunday [17 December].

However, he admitted to the likelihood of some techni-
cal mistakes.

On chances of massive electoral fraud, Ryabov said this
was "just impossible” because of the heavy presence
of observers and mass media representatives at polling
stations. The electorate’s main problem, he said, is how
to make the “right choice.” He said this was all the more
important given that the Russians would this time elect
a full-blown parliament for a full term of office and they
must do their best not to regret about their choice in a
year or two.

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 35

LDPR, NDR Leaders Named as Key Violators of
Campaign Rules

MKI1612135895 Moscow NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA
in Russian 16 Dec 95 pl

{Report by Konstantin Katanyan under the general
heading: “Yet Another Election — And Once Again
a Momentous One. But In Fact This Is an Absolutely
Ordinary Matter: The Russian People Are Electing Their
Parliament")

{[FBIS Translated Text] Zhirinovskiy’s Liberal Demo-
cratic Party of Russia [LDPR] and the “Russia Is Our
Home” [NDR] association have more often than others
breached campaigning rules during the election cam-
paign. This conclusion was drawn by Igor Yeremin,
deputy chairman of the Judicial Chamber on Informa-
tion Disputes under the Russian Federation president, at
a briefing for Russian journalists and foreign observers.

It is well known that Vladimir Zhirinovskiy offered “a
simple recipe for bringing order to Chechnya” on 6
December. Jn the LDPR leader’s words, “five or six
mountais vil'ages should be burnt down by napalm”
for every Russian soldier killed. The Judicial Chamber
thinks that such an extremist method of resolving
political problems “leads to the stirring up of ethnic
strife,” and, therefore, these calls should be dealt with
by courts of general jurisdiction. The chamber turned
to members of the Central Electoral Commission, who
passed this matter to the Russian Federation General
Prosecutor’s Office for consideration.

It is more difficult to pinpoint violations of the principle
of equal rights for all candidates running for the
State Duma that have been committed by the NDR.
Yeremin emphasized that “the frequency of Viktor
Chernomyrdin’s appearances on state television on all
conceivable occasions has transcended all borders of
the permissible.” The NDR leader started appearing in
news programs particularly often in connection with
the Russian president's illness because the premier has
essentially become the key person exercising executive
authority in our state.

However, as Igor Yeremin thinks, today “there are no
legal sanctions for such violations.”

So, with regard to the NDR, the Judicial Chamber con-
tented itself with an appeal to a!l television companies
urging them to minimize the air time allocated to the
candidates running for the State Duma who hold high
state posts.

Automated Vote Counter To Be Used
Experimentally

LD1612172295 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1413 GMT 16 Dec 95

{FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Dec 16 (INTER-
FAX) — The automated vote counting system will be
used in Sunday’s elections to the State Duma exper-
imentally, said Yuriy Baltrushevich, Central Electoral
Commission official in charge of introducing the sys-
tem.

As he said, the system is to be put into operation in the
first quarter of 1996. At Sunday’s elections it will only
be tested. Preliminary testing has already been carried
out. The Central Electoral Commission will not take
the results of its use into account — the ballots will be
counted traditionally (INTERFAX’s note: by hand).

According to Baltrushevich, the system speads to Russia
as a whole, with the exception of Chechnya and
Kalmykia, where its fitting has not been concluded yet,
and Ingushetia, whose leaders failed to grant premises
for accomodating the system in time.

The automated vote counting system is being introduced
in accordance with the president’s decree signed on
August 23, 1994. It includes electoral commissions
beginning from the second level (territorial commissions
and higher), and involves a total of 5,000 personal

. When it is put into operation in 1996, the
system will be used to collect information and count
votes automatically at the future elections.

(ITAR-TASS) — The Moscow election commission
said on Monday it faced problems with the computer
vote-counting system which initially reported that the
Our Home is Russia bloc of premier Viktor Cher-
nomyrdin was in the lead.

Olga Volkova, a spokeswoman of the commission, told
Tass on Monday that “some faults in the electronic con-
trol system of election results were reported in Moscow"
triggering delays in the publication of early returns.
“The information is being verified", she told Tass adding
that the specified results are to be announced by 08:00
Moscow time (05:00 GMT). .

In St. Petersburg the results in only one constituency
became known so far. According to the urban election
commission, the Yabloko bloc got 16.5 percent of
votes. The Communists of Gennadiy Zyuganov were
the second with 13.4 percent, the Our Home is Russia
of premier Viktor Chernomyrdin was the third with
12.9 percent, the Democratic Russia’s Choice of Yegor
Gaydar won 11.9 percent of votes.

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 37

CPRF: Yeltsin Statement in Breach of Election Law

LD1612180195 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1736 GMT 16 Dec 95

[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Dec 16 (Interfax)
- The Communist Party of Russia today circulated a
statement saying that in defiance of the election law
the media continued reporting statements “openly or
implicitly assessing parties and independent candidates”
running for parliament on the last day before the
elections.

The Communist Party named among such media reports
the statement made by Russian President Boris Yeltsin
on Friday evening, December 15, 1995. By making
this statement, the president “actually interfered in the
election campaign giving extremely biased evaluations
of the situation in the country,” the Communist Party
said. The president “once again frightened voters by
saying that undesirable for him election results may lead
to bloody redistribution of property,” the Communist
Party’s statement reads.

By urging voters to “make a reasonable choice” in
favor of the Our Home is Russia electoral bloc, the
president "directly violated article 45 of the election law
barring the federal authorities and officials from election

campaign or siding with a party tc the election struggle,”
the Communist Party said.

Reports from the regions show that such “instructions”
from the president are considered by local administra-
tions as an order, the Communist Party said.

"The Communist Party of Russia and its electoral organ-
ization’s headquarters express their decisive protest over
such violations of the law,” the party said. The Com-
munist Party said it would follow all such incidents to
determine "how the present Russian authorities observe
the law or regard free display of popular will.”

Zhirinovskiy: End Chechnya War By Napalming

LD1512184095

[FBIS Editorial Report] Moscow TV-6 in Russian at
0540 GMT on 15 December broadcasts a 30-minute
recorded interview with Vladimir Zhirinovskiy, leader
of the Liberal Democratic Party of Russia (LDPR), in
the "Elect Me!" program.

Zhirinovskiy states: "If we get a majority in the State
Duma, say 200-250 deputies, we will immediately end
the war in Chechnya. How? I will go to the president
in person — because I'll have the right, I could most
probably become the speaker of the chamber, so I'll
have the right to go to him just like Rybkin does now,
except that he fawns on Yeltsin.” The LDPR leader
continues: “I'll tell him: Boris Nikolayevich, give the
order immediately to the commander of the troops in
Chechnya, that if tonight even a single shot is heard, if
even one single Russian soldier is wounded or killed,
everything is to be burned to the ground. Another shot
— burn another village. If we burn three or’four villages
to the ground, napalm them, so that there is nothing left,
the war will end.”

The LDPR’s second move, Zhirinovskiy tells studio
presenter Matvey Ganapolskiy, would be to raise the
call-up age to 25-30 and focus on propaganda — “not
pornography, chewing gum, and contraceptives” but
propaganda to make young men proud to go into the
army to defend the fatherland.

Zhirinovskiy proposes five measures to solve the crime
problem: bring back the old cadres in the Interior
Ministry, the experienced investigators; set bandit gangs
against one another by playing the ethnic card and
using one gang to finish off another; get rid of criminal
groups abroad (he notes here that: “rotten, bourgeois
democracy won't accept me, the party leader, they
Close their air space to Germany for me, but they take
gangsters, prostitutes, drug addicts from al! over the
world"); liberalize business to stop evasion of the law;
and permit the militia and all in state service to use arms
more widely.

Zhirinovskiy calls for a cut in taxation to 40 percent,
and for a halt to imports of staple foods, to help Russian

agriculture.

Answering questions from Ekho Moskvy journalists
Aleksey Venediktov and Dmitriy Solopov, Zhirinovskiy
says the LDPR’s closest ally is the Agrarian party. He
would make up for the shortcomings in the constitution
which are in the president's favor by forcing Yeitsin to
heed the 40 million people who Zhirinovskiy hopes will
vote for him. Asked if he was not afraid of alienating the

18 December 1995

electorate by his call last week for an army of 3 million

All parts of a unified Russian state — whether the

istan, nor Azerbaijan, nor Armenia nor Georgia will ever
again be parts of the Russian state. And five years hence,
they’ll perish, swallowed up by their neighbors, Iran,
Turkey and Pakistan. I'll show the whole world that
Russia is not the colonizer, it’s others who are!" Rela-

Zhirinovskiy Election Address

LD1512230395 Moscow Mayak Radio Network
in Russian 1630 GMT 15 Dec 95

(Election address by Vladimir Volfovich Zhirinovskiy,
leader of the Liberal Democratic Party of Russia, LDPR,
on 15 December — recorded]

{[FBIS Translated Excerpt] Good evening, esteemed
citizens of Russia. In two days you will have to go to
the polling stations. We would all like to live in Russia
where each person could earn his living in accordance
with his work and abilities rather than in accordance
with his smart shift to the Bolsheviks in 1917, then to
Mensheviks in 1991, only to be followed by his move
to the arch-patriots. We are a little bit tired of those
turncoats. So we are offering you a realistic solution.
Generally speaking, we don’t want to break up anything
and build anything from scratch. For instance, let’s take
security issues: it will not cost much to reintegrate the
KGB [Committee for State Security]. Today it is divided
into four parts: the Foreign Intelligence Service, the
government communications [FAPSI] and others. If it
is brought under single command again it will be only
cheaper. Consequently our programs for improving the
economy and the state and solving the ethnic issue and
social problems don’t require additional expenses. On
the contrary, we indicate where money can be found to
improve living standards while all other parties envisage
new break-ups and revolutions again. It is Russia’s
misfortune that over 90 yuars it has experienced five
revolutions, wars, periods of reforms and so on. But why
are we so insistently urged toward the market today?
Did we not have a market before, did we not buy and sell
before? It’s a question of ideological fraud. The market
is needed today in order to weaken Russia. The version
of democracy that is being imposed is, too, needed to
weaken Russia. Nationalism, which is being hailed in

order to split up Russia once again, after the USSR was

the world, were shot down by a 19-year-old Pakistani

bad. But what’s so good about you? I reply: we are
clean, we tell the truth. Let’s expose them.

promises. But they don’t mention the fact that the LDPR
had only 60 seats, 60 buttons; 390 deputies belonged

Chernomyrdin Does Not ‘Intend To Resign’

LD1712123095 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service
in Russian 0836 GA'T 17 Dec 95

[By ITAR-TASS correspondents Artem Protasenko and
Gennadiy Yezhov)

(FBIS Translated Text] Moscow, 17 Dec — In the event
of Russia is Our Home crossing the five percent barrier,
Viktor Chernomyrdin, who heads the movement's fed-
eral list of candidates, will not become a State Duma
deputy. He told journalists this when he cast his vote.

Asked whether he was afraid that the new Duma would
put forward a no-confidence motion in the government,
Viktor Chernomyrdin said he did not "intend to resign."

Concerning the situation in Russia, the prime minister
said that “I do not consider the country’s economic
Situation to be successful.” At the same time he said
the economy in the past few months shows that “we are
doing everything right and the economy is: picking up.”

The prime minister said that he will definitely be
meeting with Boris Yeltsin as usual on Tuesday [19
December] when their planning discussions take place.

Valentina Chernomyrdina, the Russian prime minister's
wife, told journalists that she, like most of their family,
had voted for Russia is Our Home.

‘Russia is Our Home’ Considers Success To Be
3% of Seats

LD1712152495 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1401 GMT 17 Dec 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, De ~ (Interfax)
— The Russia is Our Home election bloc _.il consider
itself successful if it takes 30 perceni of seats in
the new Duma, head of the movement headquarters
Sergey Belyayev told a briefing in Moscow Sunday [17
December].

It is not obligatory that all the 30 percent of Duma seats
belong to the movement members. They can be joined
by independent candidates sharing the movement's
ideology and deputies of other parties and blocs who
fail to win the elections on party lists. “This will be
a victory,” said Belyayev. “There can hardly be any
doubt."

According to Belyayev, the movement aims to form a
“united democratic coalition of the democratic majority
in the State Duma, to become a real guarantor of further
reforms and new presidential elections.”

Asked about a possibility to form a coalition govern-
ment after the elections, Belyayev stressed it is not the
matter of a coalition government.

According to him, "the government should be controlled
by a real political force to be formed on the basis of the
faction of the "Russia is Our Home" movement."

Asked what will be viewed by the movement as a
failure in the elections, Belyayev said there will be no
failure. He reminded correspondents that the president

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 43

guaranteed continuation of the reform policy to the
government.

"We do not want to speak about a defeat," said the
representative. The President is the guarantor of reforms
and he guaranteed the government their continuation.

Referring to the voting results in the Far East, he said
that one conclusion about the elections can be drawn
for sure: The election results will be valid because the
majority of Far Eastern regions had over 50 percent
attendance.

Commenting on information of the movement experts,
Belyayev suid that the Liberal-Democratic Party of
Vladimir Zhirinovskiy had the largest broadcast time
amongst all parties and movements - 15.2 percent of
the overall political broadcast time.

It is followed by the Congress of Russian Communities
- 14.1 percent of the overall political broadcast time,
and the Russia is Our Home - 14 percent.

The bloc of Ivan Rybkin ranks fourth - 10.4 percent,
it is followed by the Democratic Choice of Russia (9.2
percent) and the Yabloko bloc (5.4 percent).

According to the movement, it was not one of the three
parties which gained most of broadcast time for political
commercials at the Russian Public TV and the Russian
state TV channel. Russia is Our Home was one of
the three leaders in political commercials only on the
independent NTV channel.

A total of 27 percent of Moscow residents came to the
polling stations by 2 p.m. [1100 GMT] over 30 percent
of local residents came to the polling stations by the
same time in the Moscow region.

High activeness of the electors is an evidence of Russian
society's support to the government policy of reforms,
said Belyayev.

Chernomyrdin Makes Final Campaign Speech

LD1512211595 Moscow Russian Television Network
in Russian 1540 GMT 15 Dec 95

[Address by Viktor Chernomyrdin, Russian prime min-
ister and leader of the Russia is Our Home bloc; date,
place not given — recorded; part of the "Election 95"
program]

[FBIS Translated Text] Dear fellow countrymen: We
have but a few hours to go before the elections begin and
this is probably my last opportunity before the elections
to address you directly. I would now like to tell you
what I, perhaps, have never spoken about before.

Yes, today they blame us for a lot. Nobody, however,
can blame me more than I blame myself. The only

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

Ae DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

driving force behind me is my belief in our great nation
that forms Russia. I want its every new day to be better
than the day before and its pain and sorrow to go away.

All this I feel today in my heart. I know full well what
it means to live hand to mouth because I myself come
from a working-class family and we have never had a
spare kopeck. I can imagine what it is like not to have
your pension paid on time and what need is. I hate
poverty. We must defeat it and we shall defeat it.

All our life and all our problems, believe me, affect
me. Any transition to a new life is extraordinarily
complex. Do not think that someone else’s reforms
somewhere went smoothly and without a hitch. It has
never happened to anybody or anywhere.

They criticize us relentlessly. Well, we may have
deserved it. There are, however, critics but there also
are people who criticize for criticizing’s sake. I often
picture to myself a man drowning in the river. Someone
tries to help him and pull him out of the water, while
those standing on the bank cry out: This is wrong and
you pulling in the wrong direction. Nobody, however,
takes a step to help by action. This is something I cannot
understand and cannot accept. Action is what is most
important now, which should be done together and not
apart.

The country has receded from the brink of the disaster.
Production has drawn breath, albeit with difficulty and
gratingly. It is no longer on the decline. Sectors and
whole industries have already emerged which work
well. We have not simply survived, we have begun to
live in a human manner, without permanent shortage of
supply and without the humiliation of lines.

Any changes and reforms are only good when they are
not indefinite and when they finally lead to a normal and
quiet life. We have now seen the light. We have stopped
the decline. We have begun to build. So should we once
again trip over ourselves? Should we once again try
to move forward with our head turned backwards or
sideways? Should we once again let ourselves be fooled
by our belief in stories of universal happiness overnight
on Monday after Sunday or on the 101st day after the
100 days? Should we once again shatter our land in the
frenzy of struggle for the total equality of everybody?

Will Russian trustfulness and our lack of malice become
a tragedy for ourselves, our mothers, our children and
our grandchildren? We are a great people whom for so
long they have attempted to force into forgetting about
this. Attempts have been made but failed and will fail.

What has been most difficult is now behind us. We
now know much better what should be done and how.
We say that to check rising prices, escalating crime and

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

corruption is what is most important today. We should
increase investment, enliven production, and revive
spirituality and belief. Reform is turning towards the
working people, the Russian producer, farmer, scientist,
teacher, serviceman, and senior citizen. It is probably
most important now not to stop and not to turn back at
the eleventh hour.

I dislike promising and not delivering. I dislike begin-
ning and not finishing or giving up halfway. I like to
work. I am used to building. This is my profession. I
have done this all my life in the name of my country,
our Russia, which has been and shall be great.

Dear fellow countrymen: I ask you to come to the polls.
It is your vote that what our common home will be like
depends on. I believe in your common sense and your
wisdom. I am convinced that everything is going to be
all right. Thank you.

Yavlinskiy Interviewed on Election Results,
Chechnya
LD1812022595

(FBIS Editorial Report) '..scow NTV in Russian at
2132 GMT on 17 December carries a 14-minute live

They start by summing up the preliminary results of
voting in the Far East. Yavlinskiy is satisfied with the
results which show an improvement on the previous
elections in 1993.

Asked about poss.sle coalition with other factions in
the Duma, Yavlinskiy says that it was an option when
solving individual issues. He goes on to say that “one of
the first issues, or even the first issue which definitely
has to be worked upon will be the Chechen war.
Regarding the Chechen war, I'd like to tell you that
no matter what names individual parties bear, if these
parties and individual deputies take a stand which would
contribute to stopping the killing of people which is
going on non-stop there, the names of these parties will
be of no importance." Asked about the essence of his
proposals on Chechnya he says that "we will have to
make the decision that was rejected a year ago, a law
on the methods and guarantees for holding relevant talks
in Chechnya, to stop the senseless and often provocative
activities by the federal bodies which lead to increasing
aggravation" of the situation in Chechnya.

Kiselev then asks Yavlinskiy whether Yabloko would
press for a vote of confidence in the government.
Yavlinskiy replied that “Yabloko believed and believes
that there are two circumstances: first, in the current
Situation it’s a senseless business, an absolutely sense-
less business because it’s the president who defines the
government’s course. So here we can speak only about
the president." He continues: "And the second circum-
stance: why should the deputies raise the issue of con-
fidence in the government after the elections? After all,
the elections will show whether the government has sup-
port or not.” Asked whether Chernomyrdin should go if
Russia Is Our Home fails to gain the majority of votes,
Yavlinskiy replies that "generally speaking, to be hon-
est, he not simply should — he must go. What Cher-
nomyrdin and Boris Nikolayevich [Yeltsin) are saying
today is of no importance." He continues: "In my view,

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 45

if Russia Is Our Home rates third, fourth, fifth, or I don’t
know, the president will have to have a conversation,
as the saying goes in such cases, and he will say: there
is a view that the elections have been lost; sorry but...
{you must go].”

Pressed by Kiselev on the subject, Yavlinskiy contin-
ues: “Otherwise what can happen? There will be a con-
tinuous and very sharp confrontation before the pres-
idential elections between the parties that have won.
For instance, if it’s the Communist Party it will say:
look, we’ve won the elections but the old government
and the president remain. And all this will be taking
place against a background of preparations for the pres-
idential elections, and this will create great difficulties.
So the president will have to find a way to open the
doors. Incidentally, he always acts like that. I believe
that what happened in Chechnya was a direct conse-
quence of Zhirinovskiy’s victory in 1993. Or for in-
stance, after sufficient pressure was exerted, members
of the Agrarian Party or the Communist Party — the
justice minister — suddenly entered the government.
The president therefore always believes that if it’s ad-
‘antageous to make certain moves favoring those who
are seemingly popular today he will make them.”

Asked by Kiselev about his plans, already well-known,
to run for the presidency, Yavlinskiy replies that “after
the results of the elections are summed up, two or three
weeks later relevant organizational operations will be
Carried out and the nomination will take place. These are
our specific plans. And after that we will be preparing
for the presidential elections.”

Yavlinskiy says he did not know anything about Alek-
sandr Shokhin’s proposal to Yabloko to cooperate con-
structively in the State Duma and spoke rather skepti-
cally about Russia Is Our Home’s chances to overcome
the five percent barrier. In Yavlinskiy’s view, if Viktor
Chernomyrdin fails to become a Duma deputy and loses
his post in the government Russia Is Our Home “will
vanish into thin air, like smoke."

In conclusion, Yavlinskiy thanks his voters for their trust
and support and promised to do everything to justify this
trust.

Russians intend to vote for the party which will

saga might become one of the main subjects in this
summer’s struggle for the Kremlin. The purpose of
privatization was presented as the creation of a middle

no middle class has been created as a result. It was
meant to create a more effective economy as well
but nothing of the kind can be seen. A raw materials
country is not producing anything of note except raw
materials. Jn fact what has been created is thousands of

themselves deceived.”

either the nomenklatura or businessmen. They behave as
shareholders, taking an interest in their property which

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

is good for the economy, Nayshul says, but problems

He says: "You will now find a very large number of
people who would like to condemn Anatoliy Borisovich
Chubays personally. I am not one of those people
for whom this is of particular interest. I am more

"On the whole privatization is carried out with two

things are different. Nobody has engaged in such stupid
politics, nor intends to do so.

“I favor enterprises being privatized or turned into joint-
stock companies, then they should be examined to see
if they work. well, if they yield any increment, if the
workers are being paid, if social rights are guaranteed,
if trade union privileges are being protected, well then,
thank God, if it is supported, it should work. Well, with
an enterprise where things are not going well, a look
should be taken at why things are the way they are. And
there are places where they are simply stealing, which
are being cleaned out and being destroyed completely.
It is not the party that should be concerned with this
but the responsible, competent bodies that should be
concerned with it.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

“In any state privatization is carried out when it is clear
that this enterprise, as a result of privatization, will work
better. For example in France, assessment is the most
important stage in privatization, 80 enterprises at most
a year. And those who buy it never take part in the
assessment of the enterprise. But here one person comes
along, puts up some money and it’s not clear how much,
does not announce a contest and that’s it."

The interviewer asks: "What happened with voucher pri-

says that Chubays is like the worst pioneer [words in-

fidiousness, deliberate destruction of all the production
bases. Just imagine, someone began to sell for a song
things from his house - the fridge, suite, furniture, his
clothes, for a song, just like that. And in a week he is
walking about (?completely) naked and in three weeks
he dies of hunger.”

Yeltsin Aide: ‘Parity’ Still Exists in Duma

LD1812123095 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1037 GMT 18 Dec 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Dec 18 (Interfax)
— “The myth that the recent Duma elections pushed
the Russian political pendulum left does not stand up
to criticism,” presidential aide Georgiy Satarov told an
Interfax exclusive interview Monday [18 December].
"The preliminary voting results do not bother me, and
should not bother Russians who care for the reforms,”
stressed Satarov.

The preliminary results of Monday noon are satisfac-
tory, because the approximate equality of votes gained
by the Russian Communist Party and the three politi-
cal forces of the Russia is Our Home, Yabloko and the
Democratic Choice of Russia, make a full parity of ret-
rograde forces and reform supporters, said Satarov. This
parity is similar to the one in the old State Duma, he
noted.

Commenting on votes for the Liberal Democratic Party,
Satarov said “they belong to people who stand for words
they heard from the liberal democrats, and not for any
programs or an ideology which the Liberal Democratic
Party does not possess.”

According to preliminary information, about 11 percent
of elections participants voted for the Liberal Demo-
cratic Party, Satarov said.

“This is a rather low index against the background of
lumpenization and the current realities,” he said.

Yeltsin Official on Expected High Voter Turnout

LD1712120695 Moscow Radio Rossii Network
in Russian 1000 GMT 17 Dec 95

{FBIS Translated Text) Our correspondent Aleksey Za-
gvozdkin asked Emil Pain, deputy head of the presi-
dent’s analytical department, what may account for a
high voter turnout.

[Begin recording) [Pain] The voting is proceeding in ac-
cordance with our forecasts. The voter turnout in 1993
was 5-10 percent higher there [not further identified}
than Russia’s average. A whole number of local fac-
tors affects it. For example, the parliamentary elections
in Maritime Territory coincide with gubernatorial elec-
tions, which increase the turnout. Predictions made by
our department with regard to the turnout are coming
true, but so far it is difficult to pinpoint the nature of
the high turnout. There are two versions: first, the high
turnout is a form of protest against the present situa-
tion, and, secondly, there are fears that the regime will
be changed radically.

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 49

[Zagvozdkin] What sections of the electorate are the
most active voters, according to your information?

[Pain] There are no changes in comparison with tradi-
tional elections. The old people cast their votes earlier.

[Zagvozdkin] Have you got any information about any
unusual events during the elections?

{Pain} So far — no. [end recording]

Yeltsin Aide Satarov Interviewed on Elections
LD1812065595

[FBIS Editorial Report] Moscow NTV in Russian at
0032 GMT on 18 December carries an 8-minute inter-
view with Georgiy Satarov, aide to Russian President
Boris Yeltsin, as part of the “Itogi” special election bul-
letins. Host Yevgeniy Kiselev interviews Satarov live in
the “Itogi" studio.

Asked how he assessed the results of voting in the Far
East and Siberia, Satarov replies: "In the first instance,
I consider them to be very preliminary results.”

He continues: "Speaking of the election successes, the
rather high vote received by the Liberal Democratic
Party of Russia really was unexpected. As far as the
Communists are concerned, according to our estimates,
we thought that they would receive a bit more in the
Far East.”

Asked how he saw the new State Duma, Satarov
replies: “I want to remind you that at the end of
December 1993 and the beginning of January 1994,
when the results of the Duma elections were regarded as
extremely dramatic, we managed nevertheless to work
normally with the Duma, in comparison, naturally with
the preceding period. I don’t think that the results of
these elections will hinder such work. I don’t think that
it will have a critical majority, let’s say, of left-wing
forces, which will hinder such work".

Asked whether they considered the election results to be
a personal misfortune, Satarov answers, sighing: “I think
that the most weighty factor which hindered the creation
of two strong blocs was the electoral system, first of all,
which, in principle does not stimulate unification in big
blocs.”

He continues: “The political structure has not changed a
great deal. The potential Communist electorate has not
increased much in comparison with 1991, for instance. It
was around 30 percent of the entire electorate and it still
is. What has changed is that the democratic electorate
has decreased somewhat.”

Satarov said he did not know whether President Yeltsin
would decide to run in the presidential elections in June.

Party Leaders on first Steps in Future Parliament

MK1612145195 Moscow KOMMERSANT in Russian
No. 46, 12 Dec 95 p 9

[Answers by Gennadiy Zyuganov, Mikhail Lapshin,
Yuriy Skokov, Viadimir Lukin, Yegor Gaydar to KOM-
MERSANT questics}

{FBIS Translated «ext) [Question 1] “What law will
your faction submit to the next State Duma for consid-
eration first of all?”

[Question 2] "Which of the current laws do you think
should be immediately annulled or amended?”

KOMMERSANT put these two questions to leaders of
major political associations and blocs.

Gennadiy Zyuganov, leader of the Communist Party of
the Russian Federation:

1. The laws on the subsistence minimum, on agricultural
cooperation, on the fight against corruption, and a
number of others. In a word, in the first place, we will
focus on a whole range of laws aimed at protecting
domestic production and the people. These laws have
already been passed by the Duma, but have yet to be
considered by the Federation Council or have not been
signed by the president.

2. The Communists think that the executive branch's
wide-ranging authority should be complemented by its
accountability to the parliament. For this to be done, it
is necessary to make corresponding amendments to the
Constitution and vest the parliament with supervisory
functions.

Mikhail Lapshin, leader of the Agrarian Party of Russia:

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

1. The first piece of legislation will concern land. We
have not yet tackled this matter, so we ought to return
to it and to matters of agricultural policy in general. The
absence of normal land legislation may lead to complete
lawlessness.

2. The first piece of legislation that we will urge
to be revised or declared null and void will be the
Belovezhskaya Forest accord. We ought to resolve this
painful problem of the country’s split.

Yuriy Skokov, leader of the Congress of Russian
Communities [KRO]:

1. As its first legislative initiative in the next State
Duma, the KRO faction will submit the law on the
government’s responsibility for a falling standard of
living.

2. First of all, KRO deputies will propose amendments
to the Constitution of the Russian Federation to include
in it the questions that have already been put to
a referendum initiated by the Congress — on the
government’s responsibility and on introducing the term
“union of peoples” as the underlying foundation on
which to build the Russian Federation.

Grigoriy Yavlinskiy, ieader of the “Yabloko" bloc.
No answers have been received.

Vladimir Lukin, a leader of the bloc, answered the
questions:

1. The first piece of legislation that “Yabloko” will
submit to the next Duma will be amendments to
the Constitution curbing the president’s authority and
broadening the parliament's supervisory functions.

2. The first piece of legislation that "Yabloko" will
propose to be revised will be the law “On Production
Sharing Contracts,” which, as approved by the Duma,
guarantees anything but foreign investment.

Yegor Gaydar, leader of the Russia's Democratic
Choice party:

1. To adopt a new pro-market land code that will open
up a path toward private ownership of land and private
circulation of land.

2. First of all, to repeal the law on the amendments to
the Law on Universal Military Service Obligation which
increase the length of service. We insist on repealing it
because military spending and the money spent on the
armed forces and their maintenance costs account for
an immense share of the overall expenditure, and the
military itself makes up a considerable portion of our
society. Remilitarizing our society and economy would
mean to sign them a death sentence.

European Observers Find Russian Elections

LD1812082695 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English

[By Natalia Cherepanova]

(FBIS Transcribed Text] ST.PETERSBURG, December
18 (Itar-Tass) - The Russian elections are “deemed 100

of Europe David Atkinson told Itar-Tass. He is to
deliver one of the three main reports on January 25 in

Council of European would be recommended to grant
Russia membership.

Election District of St. Petersburg on Sunday, where
he visited fifteen polling stations. “All the procedures
were in keeping with the European standards of free
democratic elections,” he commented.

[By Nikolay Kalintsev]

{FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW, December 12
(Itar-Tass) — Today’s election to the Russian State
Duma cannot be rigged, at least not at polling stations,

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 51

member of the Europarliament’s delegation of monitors
Constanze Krehi said in an interview with Itar-Tass
today.

She said concern over possible rigging has been reit-
erated to the European monitors over the past weeks.
Krehl said she personally was warned about some ten
feasible ways of fooling the voters.

However, such apprehensions in all appearance are not
coming true, Krehl said.

She said she had visited several polling stations in
Moscow, and made sure that fraud was impossible there,
with local observer delegates of parties on the vigil.

Other monitors of the Europarliament acknowledged
correctness of the procedure and good arrangement of
the elections in St. Petersburg, Rostov and other Russian
Cities.

They also found the activity of voters to be higher than
in the 1993 parliamentary elections.

Krehl said this was an indication that the Russians came
to an understanding that the parliamentary elections are
as important as presidential ones.

She said the high turnout provided an additional safe-
guard against manipulations and promised that the new
Duma would be more moderate.

under way in Russia,” international observer Rabbi

Army Legislation Moves Seen as Electioneering

MMI1512163795 Moscow KRASNAYA ZVEZDA
in Russian 14 Dec 95 pl

[Article by Nikolay Poroskov: “Amendments to Amend-
ments. More on Manpower Acquisition and Politicians’
Populism, Which Is Preventing a Systematic Solution to
This Complex Problem"]

{[FBIS Translated Text] The pillboxes and bunkers of
the mass media of a certain (for which read anti-Army
and consequently antistate) persuasion, which had only
just fallen silent for a while, have swung into action
again, spitting out machine-gun bursts of “denunciatory”
newspaper phrases. Their field of fire is the same as
before — the Armed Forces, and above all problems
regarding the draft and manpower acquisition for the
Army, Navy, and “other” forces. In order to portray
the current situation more clearly let us recall the
background.

As is known, on 29 April 1995 the Federal Law “On
Amendments and Additions to the Russian Federation
Law ‘On the Military Service Obligation and Military
Service’" was passed. Among other things it abolished
certain types of military service draft deferment and
increased military service to two years. Everybody in the
Armed Forces then breathed a sigh of relief as if to say:
At last we will be able to bring units and combined units
up to normal strength. Optimists mentioned a figure of
over 80 percent.

The rejoicing, however, was premature. On 20 July
this year the Duma adopted three laws with titles that
are extremely difficult to pronounce, therefore I will
mention only one: The Federal Law “On Amendments
and Additions to the Federal Law ‘On Amendmenis
and Additions to the Russian Federation Law "On the
Military Service Obligation and Military Service."*"
These acts envisaged the return of draft deferment for
a youth who is being brought up and maintained by
his parent (or the person in loco parentis) if the latter is
over 50 years old, the reduction in the service period for
soldiers and NCO’s in “trouble spots” to 18 months, and
the application of the two-year service period only to

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

people drafted for service since 1 October 1995. That is,
under the Duma members’ scheme three whole groups
drafted for two years under the previous— existing —
law would have to serve only 18 months.

At the “instigation” of Sergey Yushenkov, chairman of
the State Duma Defense Committee, innovations have
several times been "folded into” the mechanism for pass-
ing laws (Duma — Federation Council— president),
but on each occasion they did not come off, for var-
ious reasons. But Rome wasn’t built in a day and on
15 November the State Duma overcame the Federation
Council “veto.” But Russian Federation President B.N.
Yeltsin sent back these laws to the State Duma without
considering them — “because of the failure to observe
the provisions of Article 105, Section 5 of the Russian
Federation Constitution when they were considered a
second time by the State Duma after being rejected by
the Federation Council.” Let us remind our readers of
this article of the Constitution: "In the event that the
State Duma disagrees with the Federation Council's de-
cision the federal law is passed if at least two-thirds of
the total number of State Duma deputies vote for it in
the repeat voting.” That is, it is a question of a contra-
vention of procedural norms.

There are many illogicalities in the laws that have
been submitted for approval. The paradoxical situation
whereby one section of the spring 1995 draft will serve
18 months (thos: Jrafted in April) and another will serve
24 months (those drafted in May-June) will lead to un-
fortunate consequences for military collectives and so-
ciety as a whole. The president did not discern any ob-
jective preconditions for restoring the abovementioned
deferment — at 50 years old a parent or person in loco
parentis is quite capable of working. But if the parent
really does need outside help or care, his son can al-
ready be granted deferment in accordance with Article
21, Section 1, Paragraph (b) of the Russian Federation
Law “On the Military Service Obligation and Military
Service." In addition, in his edict of 3 December the
Russian Federation president discharged soldiers and
NCO’s who have carried out missions in armed conflict
conditions in the Chechen Republic and the Republic
of Tajikistan: If they have served at least a month in
such conditions they have to serve only 18 months, and
likewise even if they have served less than a month but
have been wounded, concussed, or maimed.

The president’s decision provoked a stormy reaction
both from certain politicians and from the mass media,
which are switched on as if by command. Let us listen
to their main arguments: Mothers are waiting for their
sons; the president does not need voters’ votes; such a
large Army cannot be fed; the law is not retroactive,
etc.

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 53

The first two hardly need any comment. It is easy to
play on mothers’ feelings, it is much harder to make
unpopular but necessary decisions. The adoption of
“amendments to amendments” surprisingly coincided
with the election campaign. In specialists’ opinion
the commentaries and assessments are of a populist,
opportunist nature and are exclusively associated with
election games and in no way with the interests of the
country’s security.

As regards the Army’s size, there are limits to cutting
it (and not only our Army). Specifically, they depend
on the size of the territory and the length of the state
border. In China there are 123 servicemen for every
kilometer of the border, in the United States there are
99, and in Russia only 28. We would get approximately
the same ratios using the percentage ratios of the size
of the population to the size of the Army.

In saying that “the law is not retroactive," the people
lobbying for amendments are at the very least dissem-
bling. Under Article 54, Paragraph | of the Russian
Federation Constitution the only law which cannot be
retroactive is one which “establishes or aggravates re-
sponsibility." That is, if a citizen were given a year for
libel, for example, but later they had second thoughts
and said: No, make that five instead. "Champions of
human rights and the citizen" would have nothing to
complain about: Article 55, Paragraph 3 of the Consti-
tution makes it possible to limit them “as much as is
necessary in the interests...of the country’s defense and
the state’s security.”

On 9 December the State Duma approved the statute of
the 3 December edict, incorporating it in its entirety
in a separate law. But it made additions to it. The
first thing was the age of the parent whose son has
the right to defer; they increased it to 52 (?). Tae
second was the provision that the two-year service
period is still effective from 1 October 1995. Thereby
an impetus was given to a new twist in the process
of pushing populist amendments through. Because the
mechanism for passing laws is still the same, it is
entirely reasonable to expect that the president will
send back this document to the State Duma as well
— by which time it will be a new State Duma. So
why are the current deputies making all this fuss, if not
for election campaign purposes? Meanwhile everybody
should understand what the current 64 percent Armed
Forces strength level means. Many combined units
and units are virtually unable to carry out their daily
activities even at the minimum necessary volume, while
units and ships, including those on alert duty as part
of the strategic nuclear triad, are on the verge of
losing their combat readiness. There will be nobody
to replace the 230,000 people who under the Duma

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

Ss DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

members’ scheme are liable to discharge this fall
from the Armed Forces and other forces. We will not
be able to- guarantee peacekeeping functions in the
Dniester region, Tajikistan, Abkhazia, and Yugoslavia
or the protection of the state border, and maintaining
force groupings in the Chechen Republic in a state of
combat readiness will become an insoluble problem.
The State Duma’s decision — intentionally or not —
is objectively aimed at undermining the state’s defense
capability and destroying its Armed Forces, and could
have unpredictable consequences.

It is a great pity that attempts to make preelection politi-
cal capital out of the Army are continuing. Evidently we
will soon hear new "dispassionate" commentaries. On
5 December the State Duma adopted the Federal Law

"On Higher and Post-Higher Vocational Education." It

extends the list of grounds on which students at higher
vocational educational establishments can obtain draft
deferment. For example, it gives them the opportunity
to obtain the desired deferment irrespective of training
already undertaken at the preceding level. It addition-
ally grants deferment to students in higher educational
establishments which have only a state license and not
State accreditation — there are over 500 of them in Rus-
sia. This contradicts existing legislation and creates the
conditions for anybody to evade service on entirely le-
gal grounds. The adopted legal provisions concerning
deferments are also contradictory in that they are not a
subject which falls within the said law’s terms of refer-
ence.

Just how long can military collectives be kept on ten-
terhooks by endless amendments? When will the final
decisions finally be made on the questions of service
periods and deferments from service? Do the initiators
of all sorts of amendments really not understand that in
order to suit the political conditions it is impermissible
virtually every month to bring confusion to such a com-
plex and crucial matter as manpower acquisition?

The answers to these questions will largely depend on
the new State Duma. So the uniformed voters now have
some food for thought when they go to the polls.

was needed here and now.

What was clear to other people well in advance became

stands to lose. At any rate, whatever the real score, it

is needed for a "nationwide" sanction for power.

This does not at all mean that the premier will have
to quit — far from it. After a public demonstration
of the fact that there is no democratic sanction for
power, it will become obvious that all the powers

There is, obviously, the view based on a well-known

concentrated immense power, and ‘somebody’ is not
sure he will be able to make correct use of it." Especially

the government in the structure of the Russian power
system, in the way government teams are formed and
power distributed. One can only wait and see who will
avail themselves of this opportunity and how.

Differences Between Duma Election, Previous Polls

LD1612111295 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
1059 GMT 16 Dec 95

{By Lyudmila Alexandrova]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, December 16
(Itar-Tass) — Only two years separate the coming De-
cember 17 elections to the State Duma (Russian parlia-
ment lower house) from the previous polls. However,
both the situation in which they will be held and their
political importance will differ considerably from the
situation in December 1993.

The present elections will be held, above all, in a more
politically normal situation.

Preliminary data show that a turn-out may be higher
than initially forecasted. While 50.6 percent of the
electorate participated in the 1993 polls, it is expected
(according to forecasts) that the turn-out will be at least
60 percent.

However, no matter what the composition of a new
Duma will be, it is highly improbable that sweeping
changes will take place in Russia’s political life in the
next few months.

Small doubt that right after the December 17 voting,
all the major political leaders will get down to bracing
for the next stage of the election race — the 1996
presidential elections.

The general elections will, in actual fact, become the
first round of the presidential poll and will enable
politicians to start, in a new way and conscious of their

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 55

real influence, haggling with possible allies to conclude
election alliances.

It is self-evident that the first six months of the new
Duma’s work will be also subordinated to the logic of
preparations for these main elections for Russia.

(FBIS Translated Text] Moscow, 18 Dec (ITAR-TASS)
— Preliminary results at 0515 Moscow time [0215

tendencies. Leonid Smirnyagin, head of the regional
problems department at the Analytical Directorate under
the Russian president, said in an interview with ITAR-
TASS today that the picture is more or less the same in
all these regions: the Communist Party of the Russian
Federation and the Liberal Democratic Party of Russia
{LDPR] are in the lead with a considerable gap; and
Women of Russia, Yabloko, Russia Is Our Home, and,
a surprise for many people, Svyatoslav Fedorov’s Party
of Workers’ Self-Government [PWSG] have passed the
five-percent threshold.

Smirnyagin said that the selection had been compiled
on the basis of the preliminary voting results in the
Far East, Siberia, and the European part of Russia,
in particular, in Kaluga, Kalmykia, and Tatarstan. Its
analysis shows that the LDPR, in spite of a certain
success, will not be able to repeat its result in the 1993
elections when 23 percent of the electorate voted for
the liberal democrats. According to Smirnyagin, it looks
as if part of the LDPR’s previous electorate this time
supported the Congress of Russian Communities which
nevertheless — and this was yet another surprise of the
present voting — on average will not overcome the five
percent barrier in these 30 regions.

Smirnyagin also noted that the Democratic Choice of
Russia - United Democrats bloc has still not reached
the five percent mark, with only 2.5 percent of voters
expressing this preference. From one to two percent of
votes have gone to Ivan Rybkin’s Bloc.

The expert from the Analytical Directorate under the
Russian president indicated that the data he had quoted
could undergo significant changes. In particular, the
share of votes for the PWSG will shrink, and is unlikely
at the end of the day to break the five percent barrier.
At the same time, following the summing up of election
results in the black-earth regions of central. Russia, the

number of votes for the Agrarian Party will grow, and as

Poll Results Will Not Imperil Chernomyrdin

MMI1512155495 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian
16 Dec 95 pl

[Report by Vasiliy Kononenko: “Regardless of the
Results 17 December the Chernomyrdin Cabinet Will
Survive")

(FBIS Translated Text] Despite the fierce election
battles, an Olympian calm reigns in the Kremlin. Even
the unconcealed intentions of the Communists, like
those of “Yabloko" leader G. Yavlinskiy, to change
the composition of the cabinet of ministers if they are
successful in the 17 December elections have not caused
special alarm.

Long before the election marathon entered the home
straight, the president’s experts and analysts came to the
conclusion it will be impossible to form a parliamentary
majority in the new State Duma and therefore the
president will be able to view the results of the
nationwide vote as he feels fit. In other words, he can
take account of the powerful factions’ demands for
ministerial portfolios or he can ignore them.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

As is well known, after the 1993 election results
the president agreed to a cosmetic change in the
composition of the government. It was mainly the
Gaydarites who left and the proteges of the Communists,
the Agrarians, and the centrists who came in. But,
despite the persistent attempts by V. Zhirinovskiy, the
leader of the largest Duma faction, to "grab" some
portfolios for his party, B. Yeltsin completely ignored
his demands. Why? M. Krasnov, the president's legal
aide, explained in conversation with your IZVESTIYA
correspondent that the current constitution does not
provide for the automatic formation of a gc.ernment
on the basis of the parliamentary majority (that norm is
very widespread in the constitutions of many developed
States). The results of parliamentary elections are taken
into account to some extent in the formation of the new
cabinet of ministers, but that is a different matter. This
is done for the purpose of avoiding constant “voting”
{"votirovaniye"] — the submission by parliament of
votes of confidence in the government and the blocking
of draft laws put forward by the executive. In our
conditions, M. Krasnov noted, when the replacement of
the cabinet of ministers means not an adjustment to but
a drastic change of policy, the president is entitled to
ignore the demands of the contenders for key ministerial
posts. And the head of state has sufficient powers to do
that.

Under the Constitution only he has the right to form
the cabinet of ministers and propose a nominee for the
premiership. He can also block the passage of laws
which contravene the spirit of the Constitution. Finally,
the president has so-called “latent” powers stemming
from Article 80 of the fundamental law, whereby the
head of state is guarantor of the Constitution. These
powers are not a legal basis for a decision of the
Constitutional Court but are taken into account in the
examination of disputes between the branches of power.
To sum up, it may be said with confidence that if the
Duma factions demand a change of government this will
have no impact, M. Krasnov stressed.

This confidence on the part of the president's legal aide
is not solely based on his interpretation of constitutional
norms but also on his knowledge of the presidential
team’s real intentions and actions, not to say those
of the president himself. No one in the president's
entourage now doubts that V. Chernomyrdin will keep
the premiership for the next six months — until the 1996
presidential elections — regardless of the results of the
17 December vote. At the same time my interlocutor
cited the following arguments. First and foremost,
there are no signs of a repeat of the “Zhirinovskiy
phenomenon” in the coming elections — even the
Communists will not be able to put a considerable

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

distance between themselves and their rivals in terms
of the number of seats gained. Second, even if “left-
wingers" succeed in forming a coalition that claims to
hold the majority in the parliament, the president will
not give them the premiership since they refuse to sign
the civil accord pact and guarantee the continuation of
the reform course. Third, the Communists themselves
will hardly want to demand conditions for the formation
of a so-called "government of people’s confidence." It
is after all obvious that the life of such a government
would not extend beyond the date of the presidential
elections. That is why the tactic of attacking the
government is preferable for all opponents of the current
reform course involved in the presidential election
campaign.

Both political scientists and professional analysts are
saying that such attacks will immediately follow once
Duma structures have been formed. M. Urnov, head of
the Presidential Staff's Analysis Administration, said in
conversation with your IZVESTIYA correspondent that
"no-confidence votes in V. Chernomyrdin’s cabinet will
in all probability become cyclical in nature since nei-
ther the opposition nor other potential presidential con-
tenders have any more appealing way of campaigning
for votes."

If this forecast is correct, it gives rise to another
important conclusion: The president will nonetheless
have to surrender some government portfolios in order
to avoid a political crisis. There are not only rumors
but also facts attesting to turn of events. As for the
rumors, they focus on the imminent departure of Vice
Premiers S. Shakhray and A. Zaveryukha. It is these
posts that should be given to representatives of the
main Duma factions in the new State Duma. As for
facts, according to informed sources, shortly after the
elections V. Chernomyrdin will submit to the president
a new government structure presupposing the abolition
of over 10 state and departmental committees along
with certain ministries. Some kind of superministry of
industry and trade with political as well as managerial
functions may be formed as a result. It is intended that
personnel and structural changes in the government will
be implemented in January. The president is expected
at the same time to sign an edict on the new structure of
his staff, which is being organized to perform the tasks
of his election campaign.

Communist Victory Said To Mean ‘Disaster’

MMI1512170395 Moscow ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI
in Russian 15 Dec 95 p 3

[Article by Andrey Kolesnikov under “Anti-Utopia"
rubric: "If the Reds Come. A Second Large-Scale
Redistribution of Property Will Be a Disaster")

([FBIS Translated Text] Brezhnev’s mother traveled to
Moscow to visit her son. On seeing his apartments full
of expensive things and servants and his garages, she
burst into tears: "Oh, Lenechka, my little son! What
will happen to us if the Reds come again?!" (A joke
from the early seventies.)

A capitalist revolution has been accomplished economi-
cally, politically, and psychologically. The country is on
the brink of a period of stabilization, but the "removal
of injustice" in privatization, apart from the fact that it
could become the linchpin of the left-wing nationalist
forces’ whole election campaign, is capable of bringing
down Russia’s economy if it is put into practice.

Everyone remembers how the stock index reacted to
merely verbal statements by Vladimir Polevanov, then
chairman of the State Committee for the Management
of State Property. It seems that it is possible to put
together an entire political movement on the idea of
nationalization (after passing through several reincarna-
tions, Polevanov ended up heading a movement with
a characteristic name — "For the Motherland,” in the
sense of being against the "sell-off of the motherland,"
a slightly less obvious allusion being “For Stalin!").

"Why are you uniting with the national patriots, since,
according to the logic of things, you and they have
different ideologies?” the author of these lines once
asked Gennadiy Zyuganov. "When the communal home
is burning, everyone trying to put out the fire is with
us." The communal home really has caught fire. If it
had not caught fire, it would certainly have collapsed
— it was in such dire need of repairs and a responsible
master...

I recently chatted with an industrial entrepreneur who
voiced concern at the fact that someone is changing the
present government and its economic priorities as he
goes along. Despite all its shortcomings, he remarked,
the government has nonetheless set the economic mech-
anism in motion and formulated its operating rules. Even
if the Communists accede to power and decide to nation-
alize the commercial banks, they will fail completely —
on the very first day the bankers will reduce them to a
pre-bankrupt state.

Indeed, will the major and considerably smaller bankers
of the present day really leave their money to be used

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

58 DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

by the Communists who have acceded to power? Even
those who amassed their capital in a profoundly criminal
way will at once return these selfsame money bills
to the criminal path, admittedly without paying their
hit-man acquaintances for the wasted years. Because
the money bills will usually end up in accounts in
Swiss, German, and other banks, to which the ordinary
Russian hit-man does not have access. Not to mention
respectable and legal bankers, who, moreover, are
concerned about not only the financial but also the
physical well-being of their families. An attempt to
nationalize “white” bankers will deprive the Russian
commodity producer of his last investments, and he
will still recall with warm nostalgia those times when
commodities were cheerfully dispatched and when, for
some reason, no one reproached him for the fact that
nobody needed those commodities. The chief thing was
that there was the prospect of receiving money at least
for some commodity. But now, under the Communists,
there will be no money at all. The expropriation of
the expropriators is impossible in view of the latter’s
geographic remoteness.

The existence in the Russian economy of upgraded, i.e.
improved, production facilities and enterprises and, con-
sequently, of directors, managers, shareholders, work-
ers, etc. who think in a new way also complicates the
position of the patriots and Communists, who intend se-
riously to appeal to class solidarity and the proletariat’s
heightened sense of justice. Paradoxical though it may
seem, the proletariat is no longer the same. Now it not
only knows the words to the effect that "outside man-
agement” is capable of saving the plant from ruin. It
is prepared to see in practice how an efficient private
employer will give it an opportunity to work and at the
same time receive a normal wage for it.

As a result of their own victory the Communists risk
losing an electorate which, in terms of its degree of
activeness and scale, exceeds the electorate of all the
other parties, i.e. the pensioners. Communist ideals fade
against the background of nonpayment of pensions and
social benefits. Any Communist who accedes to power
and wishes to familiarize himself with the contents of
the state treasury will discover an “abundant absence"
of any funds. It is altogether impossible to feed socially
unprotected groups of the population by Communist
and national patriotic methods, becaus. the sources of
budget revenue as such will disappear (see the above
story of bankers and entrepreneurs).

What is there to say about the sell-off of Russia? All
that is left is the joke: "Is it true that the Jews have sold
Russia? Good, but can I have my share?” What would
Comrade Zyuganov be able to reply to this?...

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

Those who have something (in the positive sense) as a
result of the reform — whether bankers, directors of
enterprises getting on their feet, or, in short, private
owners — will never leave their capital, which will be
able to raise up the Russian economy, to those who do
not know how to handle this property and who can only
squander it.

Therefore it will no longer be possible to take private
property away by force and share it out. There will be
nothing to take away....

Political Commentator Warns Against Wasting
Votes

MMI1S512155095 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA
(Weekend Edition) in Russian 15 Dec 95 p5

[Article by political scientist Aleksey Kiva: "Some Ideas
From Well-Known Political Scientist Ahead of Crucial
Vote: Elections Take Place the Day After Tomorrow.
Whom Will We Choose?"}

(FBIS Translated Text] It must be admitted that people
have gotten sick of living on the edge of a volcano. They
would like stability. They would like the development
of events to be more predictable. And to have greater
confidence in the future.

However, what they want is not so very much. They
want elections — parliamentary or presidential — in
our country, as in Germany or Britain, Sweden or Japan,
or even in Hungary and Poland now, not to spell new
cataclysms of some kind.

But this “not so very much" actually means a very great
deal for us. In essence if not in terms of time. When it
comes to time I am sure that given another three or four
years’ development on its chosen course the country
will be unrecognizable. As soon as the economic upturn
begins — and it will definitely begin, since it is already
taking place in Europe’s postcommunist countries that
embarked on the same path as us, only a little earlier
— Russian citizens’ prosperity will increase literally
before our eyes. And then I think that the confrontation
in society will ease, political extremism will fade, and
law and order will gain a firm foothold. The change of
the political forces in power will also take a civilized
course. Because a change of authority will not mean
destroying the basis of their lives, people’s property will
not be seized, they will not be herded into kolkhozes,
tagged "enemies of the people,” terrorized, subjected to
repression, or shot, as happened everywhere after the
Communists took power.

What worries me when I think about the 17 December
elections?

18 December 1995

First, a number of electoral blocs, including those de-
