factions, they will seek a complete change of economic
and political course, a change of the reform model, and
even of the model for the sociopolitical system. I con-
sider these intentions not just dangerous, but ruinous for
the country. The change of political system in 1917 was
followed by catastrophic cataclysms that are still mak-
ing themselves felt to this day. The collapse of Com-
munism in 1991, which predetermined the breakup of
the USSR, on the one hand, rid the country of an an-
tipeople, dead-end system but, on the other, wrecked the
lives of millions of people, particularly the elderly, who
are having difficulty adapting to market conditions, and
plunged the country into crisis.

The people simply may not withstand another dramatic
shift in our social development, when the worst is
already behind us. Turning back the clock might be to
the liking of some members of the older generations, but
it would wreck the lives of the most active and lively
members of the younger generations. What is more, it
would put us back at least 15-20 years, but by no means
to the start of Gorbachev’s perestroyka in 1985 as some
people think. In the event of more upheaval the potential
for growth in conditions of a market economy — the
only effective kind of economy — that has now been
accumulated with such difficulty would be destroyed. It
is simply impossible to return to the former cost-based
system of economic management based on a military
economy. Attempting to turn back would be a supremely
stupid act, no, a supreme crime against the people.

Second, Ccsumunists and forces close to them are threat-
ening to reprivatize things that have been privatized.
During the massive privatization in a vast country like
Russia, there have certainly been mistakes, abuse, and
sharp practice, which can and must be set to rights. But
it would be no exaggeration to say that attempting an-
other mass division of property would mean civil war.
And this must be said openly.

Third, a number of blocs’ leaders are saying that, if they
are victorious in the elections, they will not only try to
restore socialism and Soviet power but also denounce
the Belovezhskaya agreements and revive the USSR.
And the utmost clarity is needed on this point. The
denunciation of the Belovezhskaya agreements alone
might bring about the collapse of the CIS and the
automatic expansion of NATO’s sphere of operation,
first and foremost through the admission of Ukraine and
a number of the other former Soviet republics, not to
mention the countries of Eastern Europe. This would
soon be followed by Russia’s international isolation,
including an economic blockade. It is simply stupid to

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 59

think that we cou! respond as before by successfully
resorting to the poucy of confrontation, militarization, or
nuclear blackmail. This would mean more exhaustion,
degradation, and complete catastrophe for us. One must
take a realistic look at Russia's current potential and not
forget that the USSR suffered defeat in the Cold War
and that its exhaustion in this war in many respects
predetermined the collapse of real socialism.

Fourth, there are electoral blocs whose leaders do
not verbally go as far as restoring socialism and the
USSR but who want to once again impose on us the
administrative-edict system, obviously not having a very
good idea of what this might mean for us. Namely
starvation. And ultimately collapse. It would only take
a return to the state monopoly on foreign trade or
just on the most important raw material resources (no
matter what fine aims are proclaimed when so doing) for
shelves in the stores to immediately empty and for us
to return to the situation in late 1991 where the country
was threatened with starvation. Only then we had an
extremely good harvest and massive humanitarian aid.

What conclusions suggest themselves?

Conclusion one. The parliamentary elections must be
treated with all seriousness and not considered, as some
people are doing, as just a dress rehearsal for the
presidential elections. Those political forces that achieve
impressive success in these elections will thus prepare
a good grounding for themselves in the contest for the
presidential post. What is more, the Federal Assembly
is by no means a powerless organization, as members
of the opposition claim. It is an instrument that can be
used to change the country’s political system.

Conclusion two. There is no reason today — just
as there was none six months or a year ago — to
predict a virtually absolute victory for the Communists
in the elections and paint the position of reformist
forces in tragic colors. Incidentally, public opinion polls,
for all their relative significance, give no grounds for
this. Provided, of course, they are interpreted properly
without lumping together political forces of differing
hues and calling them all Communists. Many of our
analysts completely lose sight of the fact that half the
State Duma deputies are elected in single-seat districts
and there will probably be more of them who look to
the future rather than to the past. It is still hard to tell
whose faction will be the larger — that of the CPRF
(Communist Party of the Russian Federation] or NDR
("Russia Is Our Home"}.

Conclusion three: But for all that, anti-reform forces still
have a chance of breaking into the legislature and we
must realize that too. In the crucial days when voters
are deciding who to vote for — and polls indicate that

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

6 DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

many people make that decision literally in the last few
days before the election — the mass media simply had a
duty to help Russian citizens understand “who is who."
Because many blocs, if we are to believe what they
say, have similar programs, they are overflowing with
promises of a heavenly life, and their names hide rather
than reveal the bloc’s true nature, with the possible
exception of the Beer Lovers’ Party.

Given what I have said I would divide all the best-
known parties and blocs which have a chance of being
represented in the State Duma, into two large groups.

One of them comprises those who want to take us back
in one way or another to the administrative-edict system,
to the well-known shortages, worthiess ration coupons,
crazy waiting lines, the country’s departure from the
world economy again, renewed isolation, and the use
of force and coercion, if not terror; they want to rob
us of the rights and freedoms that we have all en-
joyed for several years now — Communists, nation-
alists, and democrats included; and some of them even
want to take our property away from us as the Bol-
sheviks once did. These parties and blocs include Gen-
nadiy Zyuganov’s CPRF, Vladimir Zhirinovskiy’s Lib-
eral Democratic Party of Russia, Aleksandr Rutskoy’s
“Derzhava,” Nikolay Ryzhkov’s “Power to the People!",
and to a certain extent the Stanislav Govorukhin Bloc
and Mikhail Lapshin’s Agrarian Party. The Congress
of Russian Communities is also gravitating toward the
administrative-edict model although it stipulates that
this model is only needed for a transitional period. The
above parties and blocs claim a special path of Russian
development, with which the world is as yet unfamiliar.
That is a myth, if not downright deception.

The other group of parties and blocs consists of those
who — no matter what their leaders may say, as
Boris Fedorov or Grigoriy Yavlinskiy are doing — will
continue in one way or another the course that we are
now following, although they may make fundamental
adjustments to that course. They will follow in one
way or another the path of development which all
mankind follows and which is the only path where
real rather than imaginary results are achieved. Like
in Japan or Germany. Like in Sweden or Denmark.
Because there is simply no other route to well-being
and prosperity. And this is graphically demonstrated
for us by the backward countries of yesteryear like
South Korea, for instance, whose rate of growth (and
prosperity) exceeded several times the rate of growth in
the countries of real socialism.

The parties and blocs in this group include Viktor Cher-
nomyrdin’s NDR, Grigoriy Yavlinskiy’s "Yabloko,” the
"Ivan Rybkin Bloc," Yegor Gaydar’s “Russia's Demo-

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

cratic Choice-United Democrats," Boris Fedorov’s "For-
ward, Russia!" and certain others.

Conclusion four. We must also honestly tell Russian
citizens that the election law adopted by the State
Duma is very imperfect. Borrowed from other, Western
countries, it fails to take account of the fact that in
our country party organizational development has only
just begun, as indicated by the fact that 43 electoral
blocs are participating in the elections. And that means
among other things that the wishes of many voters will
be ignored, since their votes will not go to the blocs they
vote for. After all, even in purely theoretical terms only
20 blocs can clear the 5-percent hurdle, and in actual
fact the number doing so will be one-half or even one-
third of that figure.

in the light of this one must be aware of the following.
If those of us who in theory advocate reforms and do
not want to turn back onto the road to nowhere vote
for their favorite bloc, which is, however, little known
or not very influential and in practice has little chance
of getting into parliament on the party lists, or else its
chances are known to be zero like most of the 43 blocs,
they will not necessarily be voting for reform. They will
be adding their votes to the votes of the Communists,
the “Zhirinovskiyites,” etc. So, is it not better — faced
with this grim reality and mindful that we are not fully
guaranteed against revenge and attempts to restore an
antidemocratic system and the lifeless, inefficient state
economy — to vote for those reformist blocs that will
probably get into the parliament? Let me tell you a
secret: Many of my friends who voted for “Russia's
Choice" last time have decided not to take the risk this
time and will vote for the NDR. There are also those
among them who could with every justification be called
liberal democrats. And, moreover, some who admitted
that they voted for Zhirinovskiy in the last election.

Conclusion five. These parliamentary elections may
be the most serious test of our maturity in the years
since the beginning of perestroyka. First and foremost
of our public and political maturity. But also of our
spiritual and ethical maturity. A test of our readiness to
live in democratic conditions and play by the rules of
democracy. What do I mean? Will the leaders of those
parties and blocs which by every indication will not
get into parliament be responsible enough to urge their
supporters on the eve of the elections to vote for the
most influential reformist blocs?

I am not afraid to be specific. The parties that I would
urge to do this are the "*89° (89 Regions of Russia)"
bloc led by its coordinators Pavel Medvedev and Vadim
(Zheltsin?), the Party of Russian Unity and Accord led
by Sergey Shakhray, “Transformation of the Fatherland”

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

led by Eduard Rossel, the "Stable Russia" and "Duma-
96" movements, and the “Interethnic Union" bloc. And
perhaps "Common Cause" led by Irina Khakamada. This
is precisely in the interests of the common cause. And
even Boris Fedorov’s “Forward, Russia!" if his chances
of failure finally become clear. Bluffing, Zhirinovskiy-
style, is all very well, but only if it works toward
success. And in this case success means the continuation
of the reforms that he [Fedorov] began with Gaydar.

And another point. If we uphold the historical approach
and ultimately consider it inevitable for “right-wingers”
("drastic market reformers," market monetarists, etc) to
be replaced in power by "left-wingers,” or rather social
democrats, as in Europe’s postcommunist countries, we
must work toward that. And do so now. In order to
ensure that the social democrats’ place in our country
is not taken by the Communists, whose leaders are
evolving more toward arrant nationalism and national
bolshevism than social democracy. Euphemisms are
inappropriate here, we must call things by their names.

But what does "work" mean? Try to create a powerful
faction of social democrats and politicians close to
social democracy in parliament.

But it is quite obvious that virtually all the blocs waving
the social democratic banner will not get into the State
Duma on the party lists. The Ivan Rybkin bloc has the
greatest chance in this respect. In this case elementary
political pragmatism suggests that efforts should be
pooled around the bloc of the leader capable of forming
a powerful social democratic movement.

Therefore it would be a great boon for the development
of Russian social democracy — and at the same time
for advancing the cause of reform and democracy and
consolidating the irreversible nature of these phenomena
— if the leaders in question were to cast aside ambition,
short-term considerations, and possibly mutual bones of
contention, grievances, etc., and urge their supporters
too to vote, to put it crudely, for the favorite. No offense
meant, but the "Social Democrats” led by Gavriil Popov
have zero chance of getting in on the party lists.
The "Pamfilova-Gurov-Vladimir Lysenko (Republican
Party of the Russian Federation)" bloc has virtually no
prospects. And blocs like "My Fatherland” and “Trade
Unions and Industrialists of Russia-Union of Labor,"
which were recently part of Rybkin’s bloc, have slim
chances. If the Agrarian Party fails in the elections,
the votes of those who can be considered supporters
of social democracy will also be lost. There are some
of those in the Agrarian Party.

But there are also a number of other blocs which
do not espouse the same line as the reactionaries
but which have remained on their own and will be

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES j 61

forced to surrender their votes to others, including the
reactionaries. I am referring to the "Kedr" Ecological
Party of Russia, for instance.

So both the voters and the blocs and parties should,
so to speak, ponder the matter. And if we have
enough common sense and responsibility, we will
not miscalculate. But if not, once again, no one can
blame us. It means that we are not mature enough for
democracy and we have no one to reproach.

Ryzhkov Says Elections Should Lead to Reshuffle

LD1712154495 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1401 GMT 17 Dec 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Dec 17 (Interfax)
— The Russian parliament elections, to be won by the
left-wing forces and lost by the so-called democrats,
should result in a government reshuff , leader of the
"Power to People” election bloc and ex-Soviet Prime
Minister Nikolay Ryzhkov told an exclusive Interfax
interview Sunday.

“Bearing in mind drastic changes in the balance of po-
litical forces, the president, the guarantor of the Con-
stitution, prematurely insists on Chernomyrdin heading
the new government,” said Ryzhkov.

In his opinion, the cabinet membership will result
only from the balance of political forces in the new
parliament.

Ryzhkov said he is sure that his bloc will overcome the
5 percent barrier and win deputy mandates. Together
with the factions of the Russian Communist Party (of
Gennadiy Zyuganov) and the Agrarian Party of Russia
(of Mikhail Lapshin), the bloc deputies plan to form a
"strong left-centrist parliament bloc in the new Duma,"
said Ryzhkov.

Deputies of the Rybkin bloc “will hardly participate
in this coalition, because their leader fully discredited
himself in the election campaign,” he noted.

Russ‘an Peacekeepers To Go to Bosnia 18 Dec

LD1612162495 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1413 GMT 16 Dec 95

[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Dec 16 (INTER-
FAX) — An official representative of the Russian De-
fense Ministry told INTERFAX today that the first
group of Russian peacekeepers, including 12 officers,
would go to Bosnia on December 18, Monday.

This will be a reconnaissance group of the Paratrooper
Troops’ headquarters, the official said. The group will
be led by Deputy Commander of the Russian Para-
trooper Troops, Major General Nikolay Staskov.

The group’s tasks will include coordinating the area for
deployment of a Russian peacekeeping brigade which
will take part in the operation of multi-national forces
in the Balkans.

The official said the group would also have to set up
an air-traffic control service at the Tuzla aerodrome
and determine the routes for the Russian peacekeeping
brigade moving to the area of its responsibility.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

The Russian peacekeeping brigade will be deployed
on Bosnian Serb territory to the east of the Posavin
corridor. The Russian peacekeepers will interact with
the U.S. First Tank Division.

The Russian peacekeeping brigade was created on the
basis of the 106th paratrooper division, the official said.

Karasin Satisfied with UN Resolution on Bosnia
Accord

LD1712190195 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
1847 GMT 17 Dec 95

[By Yuriy Kozlov)

{(FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW, December 17
(ITAR-TASS) — The U.N. Security Council’s recent
resolution on the implementation of the Paris peace
accords on Bosnia corresponds to Russia’s stance,
Russian Foreign Ministry senior spokesman Grigoriy
Karasin told a briefing today.

He said it is essential for Russia that the resolution
covers both military and civilian aspects of the peace
operation in Bosnia- Herzegovina.

Also of importance for Russia is the fact that the
multinational peace implementation force will act within
limits to which the Bosnian parties and leaders of
Balkan states consented, Karasin said.

He stressed that the resolution calls for equal say of
parties to the conflict in the settlement.

As for the duration of the peace operation, Russia
always advocated effective control by the U.N. Security
Council in the region and wanted “prerogatives of the
(Security) Council in no way to be substituted by the
NATO structure", Karasin continued.

He voiced satisfaction that with one year of the peace-
keeping mandate elapsed, "just the Security Council, not
NATO or some other regional organisation will make
a decision on extension of powers of the multinational
force for further conduction of the operation”.

Karasin quoted emphasis of the Russian delegation to
the Security Council on political facets of the settlement
in Bosnia and complete lifting of the sanctions against
the former Yugoslavia and Bosnia.

The ministry spokesman stressed that the Security
Council’s resolution, adopted in intense diplomatic
debates, have put the Dayton peace accords into the
practical plane, opening the way to the peace operation
in Bosnia.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

Karasin on Former Yugoslavia Disarmament
Conference

LD1812095995 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
0952 GMT 18 Dec 95

[By Viktor Khrekov]

{(FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, December 18
(ITAR-TASS) — A conference opening in Bonn on
Monday [18 December] to work out measures for sta-
bilisation, confidence-building and control over arms
for former Yugoslavia may become an important ini-
tial step to consolidate the results of the political settle-
ment. Grigoriy Karasin, the Russian Foreign Ministry
spokesman, told this to ITAR-TASS in Moscow. He
noted the importance of shaping a situation in the mili-
tary area that would serve consolidating peace, stability
and confidence-building in the region.

"We believe specific measures should be worked out on
a basis of such fundamental principles as the quality of
the sides and equal security, voluntary assumption of
obligations and the need for their strict observance, and
restraint in the military area," Karasin said.

The talks in Bonn, in which Russia is represented
by First Deputy Foreign Minister Igor Ivanov will
be phased and will begin with working out priority
measures for stabilisation, Karasin said. These measures
will help impart stability to the military aspect of the
Paris peace process and will create an atmosphere of
greater confidence in which, in Karasin’s opinion, “it
would be much easier to seek a balance of forces
suiting security of all the parties”. "And this will make
it possible to expand the negotiations framework and
comprise entire south-east Europe."

Countries of the Contact Group, including Russia, are
to play an important role at the talks, Karasin said.
It is also important to ensure considerable role for
OSCE [Organization for Security and Cooperation in
Europe], not only in the talks but also in fulfilling future
agreements.

"Russia also believes that a success of the settlement is
largely determined by the readiness of the sides to keep
from building up their military potentials," Karasin said.

Duma’s Bezborodov on Russia’s Role in Balkans
LD1512132395 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English

Military Officer Discusses Reasons for SU-27 Crash

LD1612165295 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1413 GMT 16 Dec 95

[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Dec 16 (INTER-
FAX) — The main headquarters of the Russian Military
Air Forces told INTERFAX today that a group search-
ing for three Russian Sukhoi-27 planes was currently
moving towards the area where the planes had crashed
in Vietnam.

A MI-8 helicopter is guiding the group through the
jungle.

Chief of the Russian Military Air Forces’ Central Com-
mand, Maj. Gen. Dmitriy Gavrilov said the helicopter
could not land in the area of the air crash but could take
the group on board by rope. Fog covered the area of the
air crash in the afternoon.

The operation was suspended at 2:00 p.m. Moscow
time after darkness fell. The search group is now in
4 kilometers away from the scene of the air crash. The
operation will continue Sunday morning, Gavrilov said.

Gavrilov said the remains of the planes, which had
crashed on December 12, had been discovered Saturday
at around 7:00 a.m. Moscow time in 22 kilometers to
the south of the Cam Ranh aerodrome, Vietnam. A
helicopter firstly discovered a parachute hanging on a
tree and then saw the remains of the planes. The fate of
the planes’ four pilots remains unknown.

According to updated information, the brake parachute
belongs to one of the three planes and is likely to have
opened when the plane crashed into land.

Gavrilov said that when landing the three planes had
turned only for 20 degrees instead of the required 90
degrees. The group included six planes: a military-
transport Ilyushin-76 led five Sukhoi-27 fighters.

Gavrilov said the responsible for the air crash had not
so far been revealed. No criminal case has so far been
instigated, he said. The issue will be clarified after ex-
perts examine the planes’ “black boxes." Leading Rus-
sian aviation experts headed by Deputy Commander-in-
Chief of the Military Air Forces, Col. Gen. Viktor Kot
are now in Vietnam.

Various causes could provoke the air crash, Gavrilov
said. In particular, the three Sukhoi-27 planes could
have run into mountains, land or collide with each

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

other. The air crash could also have been caused by
an explosion of one of the planes.

Gavrilov said none of the three Sukhoi-27 planes had
Carried secret units. He said the planes were intended
only for the air show. The planes will remain where they
crashed. Only equipment necessary for the investigation
will be evacuated, including the control equipment and
the "black boxes."

The three Sukhoi-27 fighters were returning from
Malaysia, where they participated in the LIMA-95
international air show, to Russia within a six-plane
group. The three planes crashed when preparing for
landing in Cam Ranh. The rest of the group — the
Ilyushin-76 plane and another two Sukhoi-27 fighters
— landed safely.

The three crashed planes were driven by four pilots of
the elite Russkiye Vityazi (Russian Heroes) group.

begun in the presence of journalists, the heads of the
two states’ military departments expressed their mutual
interest in strengthening Russian-Norwegian ties.

sonal contacts between top leaders, which help establish
a relationship of trust. "We must afford greater opportu-

Norway,” he said. In the Norwegian minister's opinion,
joint efforts are essential in this region not only to en-
sure military security but to protect natural resources,
ensure compliance with fishing regulations, and elimi-
nate the consequences of military activity.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 67

Pavel Grachev stated that the problems and prospects of
Russian-Norwegian relations, progress in implementing
the Treaty on Conventional Armed Forces in Europe,
the Bosnian question, peacemaking activity, cooperation
in northern Europe, and NATO expansion are being
examined during the talks. It is planned to sign two
agreements Friday [15 December]: One on cooperation
in the defense sector, the other on cooperation in
protecting the environment.

Agreement Signed on Tumannaya River Basin
Development

MM1812113895 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA
(Departmental Supplement) in Russian 16 Dec 95 p9

{Unattributed report: "Another Hong Kong on Tuman-
naya River?"}

{[FBIS Translated Text] Government representatives
from Russia, China, the DPRK, the ROK, and Mongolia
have signed three agreements at the United Nations on
promoting the economic development of the Tumannaya
River basin in Northeast Asia.

This is a 20-year plan, the idea for which emerged in the
early nineties. It envisages the comprehensive economic
and social development of a zone with a population of
300 million people where the borders of the PRC, the
Russian Federation, and the DPRK meet, in particular
the creation of a modern infrastructure and a power
generation base. The cost of the project, which is being
implemented under the auspices of the UN Development
Program, is estimated at $30 billion. It is envisaged that
the zone will have flexible borders, and neighboring
regions may also take part in the joint exploration of
the area.

Political Issues
Yeltsin Addresses Nation on Elections

LD1512173295 Moscow Russian Public Television
First Channel Network in Russian
1500 GMT 15 Dec 95

[Address to the nation by Russian Federation President
Boris Yeltsin at Barvikha Spa on 14 December —
recorded]

[FBIS Translated Text] Good afternoon, esteemed Rus-
sians: In one day, on December 17, there are elections
of the deputies to the State Duma. On the eve of the
elections I would like to share my thoughts with you.
The upcoming event is no longer a formal vote which
we were taught to accept in the past decades and when
the results were known to everybody in advance.

These are true elections today. I am attentively follow-
ing the course of the election campaign. Dozens of par-
ties and movements want to get into the State Duma.
There are an enormous number of candidates for each
deputy’s seat. And many of them really want to help
Russia. They have interesting, business-like proposals
and major ideas of the state scope.

At the same time, I cannot accept everything that has
been voiced. Unfortunately, some candidates have told
the voters: everything is wrong, everything is bad. Elect
me and the situation will then change for the better
and all problems will be solved: the problems of the
army [will be solved], plants will begin to work at full
capacity, calm and well-being will come to every home.
Promises, promises, promises...

What can I say? This is probably natural. There will
always be people who would like to get a seat in the
Duma at all costs. I have met such people. It is very
easy to promise much and make people like you. But
you and I know that everything can go smoothly only
on paper. It is most dangerous when representatives of
certain parties want to take the country to the past. It is
possible that they sincerely believe that such is the only
correct way out. However, I am sure that this is a tragic
mistake.

We have been travelling the path that they want to take
us back to, for over 70 years. This is enough to make
a conclusion. So what? Did we really have a better life
than in other countries? No!

The country failed even to come close to the living
standards of developed states, including those whose
economy, like ours, was ruined in the years of war. It
is impossible to argue with that.

Why then are there many people today who look at
the past with nostalgia? It is because our young age,

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

love and personal honest labor are there. The experience
of very many years, of several generations, makes us
acknowledge the following: There will be no salvation
either in the planned economy, or in tough control over
prices. An economy can never be made to work properly
by commands and will not work. It cannot be boosted
by a general’s order either.

We will never get rid of need, if, like it was in the past,
the free ownership of land is banned and property is
reshared anew. What and who would gain from such a
bloody redistribution?

Dear electors: The aggravation of relations with our
neighbors, even for the sake of restoring the former
Soviet Union, will become a tragedy. And it was these
ideas that were voiced during the election campaign
by representatives of a number of parties, blocs, and
candidates well known to you. This is not the light at
the end of the tunnel for Russia. Rather it is a reflection
of new and even more horrible fires of a civil war.

Let us think: Will it be better if we are again divided
by hostility, anger, and hatred? Will it be beneficial? No
and no once again. What can be more dangerous when,
in the middle of a ford, some are pulling back, others
to the left and others to the right? Beware: Russia could
be overturned again.

Our country needs no revolutions. That’s enough for
attempts to start the life anew, from a blank sheet.
Russia needs sufficiency, quietness, and order.

I know that Russian voters sympathize with those par-
ties, blocs, and movements that have moderate positions,
real goals, and the methods of their implementation rule
out extreme means and adventures. I like such a posi-
tion! We have been learning to live in new conditions
only for five years. And we have already learned some-
thing. Although the studies do not go very easily. Life
is difficult so far.

These are the difficulties which are borne by pensioners,
the military, the workers of defense branches of indus-
try, the Russian intelligentsia, and others. This is also
the crime rate and the corruption among many state of-
ficials. This is also the problems of millions of people
who have entrusted their savings to financial swindlers.

I am well aware of the scope of the problems. But I
also know what efforts have to be made to overcome
these difficulties. Still we are moving toward a quieter,
normal, and decent life.

Some work has already been done. With great difficulty
the cooperation between the president, the government,
and the parliament has been launched. This is a real
achievement of the last two years.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

You must agree that it should be preserved and strength-
ened. It is precisely this that allows us to build our
Russian home today, to gradually solve numerous prob-
lems. And believe me, it can’t be done faster. Let us
together preserve the so far fragile stability which we
have achieved with such a big effort, as well as accord
in our common home — Russia.

Esteemed fellow citizens! Today I want to address those
who have not made their choice yet, those who believe
that nothing depends on them, as voters, like it was
before. I cannot agree with such moods, both as the
president and as a Russian citizen. I am addressing my
young compatriots. The older generation did not have
the possibility to choose their own way of living and
thinking. Other people decided for us. Those who did
not obey had it very hard. Times have changed. You,
young people, have the right to decide your destiny for
yourselves. But, for this, it is necessary for you to come
to the polls.

If you fail to turn out, your indifference will vote for a
return to the past. And you yourselves wiil have to pay
for this first of all, you, who are young now.

You must not allow the forces of the past to seize power
again. You should not allow the country to be taken back
to the time when everyone was told what to think, what
clothes to wear, which hairdo to have, and what songs to
sing. All this brings to mind a popular saying: We value
little what we have but bemoan the loss of it. Freedom
is what we have. It is like air. You do not notice it when
you have it. But its absence will be immediately felt.

Today, life itself insistently asks every young person:
What Russia do you want for yourself and your chil-
dren? I do not believe that you do not care! For this
reason, come to the polling stations, come with your
friends.

Cast your ballots yourselves! Cast your ballots for
yourselves!

Your whole life lies ahead. And I very much want your
life to be happier than that of our generation.

I am addressing people of my age, middle-aged and
elderly people.

I ask you to remember your mother and your father
when you vote. Remember your childhood and adoles-
cence. How many troubles your parents lived through!
How many were the dangers they shielded us from and
protected us from! It is thanks to them that we remem-
ber all the good things we had in the past.

But their own life was very hard. They knew what
hunger was like, what real fear was like, and what
mass repressions were like. And they did not learn

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 69

about it from books. Do we wish the same for our
grandchildren? May your good sense prompt the correct
choice.

I learned recently that the word "candidate" means a
person clad in white. In ancient Rome those who aspired
to a state office wore white clothes. The white color is
a symbol of virtuous intentions.

It is up to you to judge the virtue and integrity of
those who seek election, their degree of proficiency and
good sense. How well they can strengthen stability and
concord in Russia. It is up to you to decide in whom to
place your confidence and whom to refuse it to.

Many people feel worked up. But may God protect you
against giving in to the sentiments of the moment and
quick temper and casting your ballot to spite somebody,
and subsequently looking again for somebody to blame
for our troubles. It has come to be the case in our country
that the president is responsible for everything. And
you, dear fellow citizens, judge me severely, sometimes
mercilessly. But on 17 December, it is up to you to
make your decision. It would be fair if everyone were
to be as strict in making demands on themselves on that
day. The destiny of Russia is in your hands!

Speech Seen as Anti-Communist Plea

MKI1612135795 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian
16 Dec 95 pl

[Report by Sergey Parkhomenko under the "Authority"
rubric: "A Last-Hope Foul. Boris Yeltsin Delivers an
Anti-Communist Plea"}

(FBIS Translated Text] Something astonishing has hap-
pened. Despite the order of business that has long and
firmly been established in the Kremlin, President Yeltsin
was briefed on what is actually going on in the country.

He was told that the most likely winner in tomorrow’s
parliamentary elections will be Zyuganov’s Communist
Party. He heard the admission that to all appearances
the Communists’ influence in the Sixth State Duma will
not just be considerable, but — in view of their natural
alliance with other leftist factions — he should expect
the emergence of a formidable (if not large enough to
change the Constitution) leftist opposition majority in
the new parliament. He was reminded that it will be ef-
fectively impossible to dissolve such a Duma, at least
within the first year of its existence. He was warned
that this means that a Duma dominated by a Communist
majority and apparently chaired by Comrade Zyuganov
will remain Russia’s supreme legislative body through-
out the preparations for the 1996 presidential elections,
during the election campaign itself, at the moment of the

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

70 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

June vote per se, and afterward, when its results will be
calculated.

In short, he was frankly told everything about how
things stand.

This made a strong impression on Boris Nikolayevich.

At any rate, yesterday’s presidential television address
was made precisely under the influence of this impres-
sion. We simply have no other explanations for this
speech’s obvious merits.

It is not a matter of the speech’s particular qualities as
a masterpiece of political rhetoric. A group of Kremlin
speech writers has, unfortunately, earned Boris Yeltsin
a firm reputation as a fairly inept public speaker, leaving
by their joint efforts no trace of his polemic talents that
went down so well with the Russian voter in 1989-
1990. Everything is past: Since then we have gradually
become accustomed to something like badly mixed
mashed potatoes pouring time and again from the screen
— without a clear form, more or less pronounced color,
smell, or taste...

All the more visible were the efforts taken by the
president and his staff this time to draw up his speech.
The difference is particularly striking if Boris Yeltsin’s
current electoral address is compared to the one he
made two days before "Russia went mad" — on 10
December 1993. Unlike that disaster — and that speech
was definitely a disaster — the current pre:idential
address meets at least two of the most important criteria
set for any statement addressed by a head of state to his

people.

First, the listener is left in no doubt as to who precisely is
targeted by the address. Those "who have not yet made
up their minds" and "those who think that, as before,
nothing depends on them as voters.” Here two clear
"target groups” were singled out: young people with
their “I-don’t-care-a-fig" attitude, who can probably
only be budged by such warnings — lest someone
should start telling them “what clothes they must wear,
what hairdos they must have, and what songs they must
sing;" and also people of advanced age, of whom few
indeed, being in their right mind, would wish their
grandchildren to find out through their own experience
"what real hunger, real fear, and mass repressions are
like."

Second, it becomes clear from the very first words why
the president is appealing to us. Of course, in order —
even if at the eleventh hour — to try to grab something
from the Communists.

President Yeltsin’s latest speech can with assurance be
called the most vivid and cutting anti-communist plea he

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

has made since August 1991. Thus, having renounced
the traditional and empty genre of didactic wish-you-
well speeches by "the father of the nation,” who looks
condescendingly at the wrangle of the political small
potatoes under his feet, the president finally brought
himself to make a statement in which he committed
himself as a participant in the electoral fray, who makes
no bones about his own stance, his preferences, or his
fears. This is the crux of the matter: Yesterday's speech
was made by a person who is clearly aware of the fact
that his future, too, depends on the 17 December vote.

This is no longer reminiscent at ali of assurances
heard a few weeks ago that there is "nothing terrible"
about the possible dominance of the leftist opposition
in parliament. This does not bear even the remotest
resemblance to his lukewarm preelectoral reasoning
of 1993 to the effect that "any differences can be
reconciled" and that the president himself is allegedly
“ready for cooperation with deputies regardless of their
party affiliations."

Now it turns out that somet’,ing terrible can occur after
all and that people will int-vitably show up among the
deputies with whom one cannot shake hands.

An example to follow was too fresh in everyone's
memory: In 1993 Viktor Chernomyrdin managed to
make a long detour to bypass the site of the candidates’
showdown — when it was necessary he fled from the
elections as far as Valday, and as a result he was
rightly recognized as the main winner of the elections,
exploiting them to his best political advantage. This time
around Boris Yeltsin attempted to repeat the trick: At the
start of the electoral campaign he set for himself fully
understandable personal rules of the game — to steer
Clear of the fray at any cost. The rules had been observed
for three months. But two days before the denouement
they were resolutely and deliberately broken.

The president resorted to what is called in sports “a last-
hope foul.”

Yet it seems to be too late.

Chernomyrdin Discusses Current Issues

LD1512174195 Moscow Mayak Radio Network
in Russian 1005 GMT 15 Dec 95

{Interview with Viktor Chernomyrdin, Russian prime
minister and leader of the Russia is Our Home Bloc, by
Yuliy Semenov; place, date not given — recorded]

{(FBIS Translated Text} [Semenov] When was it more
difficult, when you started out, or now?

[Chernomyrdin] I am happy to say — I have to say, of
course, that I seemed to have worked all my life — I

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

have been a director, a minister, head of industry, but
that seems a long time ago. Reminiscences aside, that’s
the first thing; the second thing is — when were things
harder, and when were they easier? Well, you know,
when I began things weren’t easy, and now even more
so; there have been unexpected things which certainly
didn’t come into my plans. The president asked me to
sort out the fuel-and-power complex, [words indistinct]
Otherwise I wouldn’t have gotten involved for the life
of me. Remember how they forced me, then a minister,
to leave the state dacha. This was all meant to humiliate
me. That is why I had difficulties at the start as well,
but these were other difficulties. Things needed sorting
out.

Now difficulties are of a totally different order. God
forbid anyone should ever again have to face the many
difficulties we have faced. They have been enormous
difficulties — to keep hold of the country, of the
economy, under any circumstances, and to move it today
toward recovery — one has to work hard to achieve this.

On the whole I believe that the government should never
have an easy life. I can also say that a leader, and
more so — a government leader, should never rejoice
at his achievement. This is the end. Either the man has
exhausted his resources, or he does not understand what
he should do.

{Semenov} My next question is on the elections: Our
voters have been virtually inundated by torrents of
words. All those seeking a Duma mandate are rushing
to promise something or other. You are saying however
that your promises are all realistic and that you take
responsibility for what you say. How can you confirm
this?

[Chernomyrdin] Everyone has two positions: first, to
promise and second, to criticize the government. What
else is there? As one of them told me today; I have to
prove myself in some way, don’t I? How would they
find out about me? [laughs]. Not a big deal. If you wear
three-piece suits, then you are a leader. So he says: What
are you upset about? Everybody is doing this. So I say:
Right, you are not doing anything, so how can they find
out about you? You say: Nobody knows me. And then,
God forbid, you'll come to power, they they'll find out
about you, and what next? What to do?

So I can only say one thing here, about my attitude to
this, and how I perceive this. I am being prompted from
all directions, even by some of my close comrades-in-
arms: Viktor Stepanovich, please promise something,
everybody promises things in all countries in all elec-
toral campaigns.

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 71

I can always say that from the new year wages will
be go up, taxes will be reduced and pensions will be
increased. They will believe me, I think. But this won't
be possible to do right away.

[Semenov] Or at least not in full measure...

(Chernomyrdin, interrupting] Not in full measure. We
will be increasing pensions and wages, too, but this will
be in the future and within the confines of the budget
which we have at our disposal...

{Semenov, interrupting] And the one which has been
adopted at last...

(Chernomyrdin, interrupting) And which was a big
present for me! You understand, we will do all this,
but to say something and then not do it — I think that
is an awful thing to do. I cannot do that sort of thing,
it would be deceitful, and I would even go as far as
to say, irrespective of whether it is Chernomyrdin in
charge or not, never mind this will never be done, it’s
impossible. But, of course, there are all sorts of things
that can be done, we can set the machines rolling and
print money, I can start throwing money around and
make increases, but then everything we have achieved
would be completely written off, and the pensions would
mean nothing. It would again be the pensioners and
those on low wages who would suffer most. People paid
out of the budget — the teachers and the doctors —
why treat these people like that? I cannot do that. That
is why it will take some doing, but that’s what I'll do,
and we will increase the pensions a little. Incidentally,
for the first time we have had the chance this year —
our economy has started to work in a different way
than we have for the past four years. We have paid out
pensions to the military — budget-funded organizations,
the teachers and doctors have been paid. The only hold-
ups have been with the local bodies. For the first time,
the miners have been paid from the budget. And how I
could promise something and then fail to do it, I can’t
imagine.

[Semenov] And now a question on voters. As every
electoral bloc, you have your own strategy, your own
plans. Each electoral bloc targets its own electorate.
What category of voters you count on?

[Chernomyrdin] I count on all sensible people, serious
and understanding. I think they are the majority. |
do not think that people are indiscriminate. There are
many well-known people, easily recognizable. Some
have already been in the government several times. And
did not do much for some reason. And some took part
in disintegration of a great power — our USSR — and
look what they are saying today. I do not think people
forget such things easily.

72 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

[Semenov] In political discussions many see a reflection
of the open or hidden scramble for property. The
example of this are passions that surround privatization
at its present stage. In our opinion, how this process can
be channeled into a normal course?

[Chernomyrdin] In a few words, we should cool down
a bit. We cannot hide the fact or refuse to admit that
mistakes were made here. Yes, there were mistakes.
Yes, we failed in something. And how could we avoid
this when no one had experience in such things. The
principle that we need privatization is right. We should
have owners, masters. Because a master will always do
things better. We are traversing a path which proved
itself in the rest of the world. But we are doing it on
Russia’s conditions. We cannot copy anyone and we
do not wish to do it. It is not even possible to copy
America, Germany, Japan, or France. We are Russians.
We are different even as people, our national character is
different. We are generous people, collectivist by nature.
We stand for each other and do not count the cost. We
can give away our last shirt. Other people will not do
such things. And it is sad to see such people living in
poverty With all our resources. And so, first of all, we
are setting up market conditions for Russia. Naturally,
this is not easy to do. And this includes privatization.
This is why I am saying — let’s cool it down a bit now.
After all, we are not being rushed to fulfill some plan,
why rush things?

At the moment we relieved some of our defense
enterprises from privatization drive and freed them from
taxes for a time being. When time is right, we shall
privatize them in due course, and will continue with
it. In Germany, by the way, I mean the former West
Germany, they still have some non-privatized industries
— railways, Lufthansa. And they are living well enough,
even though they never had socialism or were made
to build communism. They do what they can. And we
in Russia — we always had state ownership and will
continue to have. Many of our industries will be only
government or state-owned. And those who are trying
to accuse us, saying the government and Chernomyrdin
want everything to be privatized — nothing of the sort.
I know exactly that Russia cannot do without state
ownership. And we shall be doing this. But I also do
not support state or collective ownership of everything.
Collective property means nobody's property to me. I
do not accept that, either.

[Semenov] Viktor Stepanovich, some of your political
opponents blame you personally for patronizing the oil
and gas industry, which you have actually came from.
Is there some patronizing element? And another ques-
tion: Some publications — in particular, one American
diplomat who recently caused a stir with his publication

18 December 1995

— are saying that Russian politics are dominated by in-
dustrial clans which mess things up.

{Chernomyrdin} I shall begin with the second one,
the Americans, the clever chaps who so much want
us to make a lot of silly mistakes, especially in the
fundamental industries. And back at home there are
even more such voices. The only thing I can tell in my
defense is that I understand these things a little more
than most, especially in the fuel and energy complex.
And, despite everything, I believe that this industry is
fundamental. And God protect us if the fuel and energy
complex begins to disintegrate. Then everything else
will fall apart automatically. There will be nothing to
do. It does not matter whether it is a market-based,
joint-stock or state-owned industry. We simply cannot
afford this to happen. And I am doing everything ‘o
prevent this from happening. To say that I create special
conditions for some is silly. Even to my old Gazprom.
It is experiencing enormous difficulties at present. The
energy industry is in a grave situation, transport —
all the fundamental industries. It is because they are
made to be fundamental. They were created as such,
structurally. They were not set up like, say, in the United
States.

For example, I must say that the energy industry in
the United States is not worse than ours. We have not
yet managed to catch up with them. Eighty percent of
it is private. Imagine, even their hydro-electric power
Stations are private. Can you imagine such things
in our country? With our system? The state there
regulates only one major issue — God forbid something
goes beyond the United States boundaries without the
government knowing. But inside they make sure that
no one fails to provide energy to any consumer. These
are two main factors for which the state is responsible.
They are responsible for it and hold it in their hands.
Therefore, I want to say this again. I am especially
sensitive towards the fundamental industries. God forbid
that we make any silly mistakes here. In the fourth
quarter of this year, a decision was adopted not to raise
prices for gas, electricity, and railway transport.

[Chernomyrdin] A decision has been adopted. Every-
one was warned in advance, all the heads of adminis-
tration: Watch everyone and if anyone will misbehave
you should punish them, up to sacking those respon-
sible. And look what is happening. Krasnodar admin-
istration raised tariffs for domestic gas supplies from
20 rubles per cubic meter to 80. Imagine, a pensioner
Mashkin writes to me from Krasnodar Territory that
with his pension of 176,000 rubles per month he has
to pay 70,000 for heating and gas. And many others
trom Stavropol, Krasnodar, Kabardino-Balkar Territo-
ries, from Voronezh, Ivanovo, Lipetsk, Nizhniy Nov-

[Semenov] Some oil and gas companies and enterprises

[Chernomyrdin] This is right. And this is why I say

[Semenov] But what about clans — the gas, the defense,

supporting each other — fuel people keep to themselves,
defense too. Life itself makes them so.

[Semenov] But they are not to be taken as units in
political sense?

[Chernomyrdin] No, this is absolutely out of question.
At least for me. I thing this is all pre-election talk. It
will pass and everything will fall into place.

[Semenov] And now, if possible, say few words about
Chechnya. you name is being connected with the
beginning of political settlement in Chechnya. But
recently there has been a certain pause in the events.
How do you see the continuation of this process?

[Chernomyrdin] The pause is not connected with some
artificially imposed conditions. The matter is not that
easy to bring the peace process which we have started to
its conclusion in a short time. It is easy to start a war, but
difficult to end it. And the pause is first of all is forced
on us by people who keep on nudging us: why don’t you
blast them there, why don't you disperse them. This is
the problem. I would not like that. Some people forgot
very quickly that during the armed conflict hundreds
of our boys were killed there each day. I cannot say
that everything has been done. Many other things need
doing. But, at least, we did not stop the process of
restoration of Chechnya. We do absolutely everything
to ensure that negotiation process goes on. We make
every effort to avoid a wave of vengeance, to prevent
new clashes. We cannot allow that. Chechnya is Russia.
Russian people live there. We are going for elections
there because elections are needed. This is also a step
forward. Yes, I understand that it is difficult to hold
elections in such situation. People are being intimidated.
But we have to do it.

This is why we, the government, and the president who,
I know, pays great attention to this, are doing everything
to make sure it is all done peacefully. Unfortunately,
look what they are doing. Last explosions — all is done
to provoke us to take the same action. We would not
unleash military action.

[Semenov] You have already mentioned this, that hu-
man life is losing its value very rapidly. It has a price —
in rubles, in dollars. No one is spared if it suits some-
body. There was the last tragedy in Vladikavkaz where
a scoundrel killed little children. A new minister or a
new Prosecutor General, a program for fighting crime
— a great number of documents. But people still ask —
when will the government move from declarations, in-
tentions, and laments and get down to business? When
it is going to put an end to it?

[Chernomyrdin] I have little to say in defense here.
I myself do not like empty words, this is death to
me. Recently I inspected a program on fighting crime,
presented by the ministry. We could not adopt it. It its
impossible to adopt it in the light of what is going on
today. To take some measures just to add points —
we cannot allow it. We should have serious, properly

defined policy on fighting crime. The crime in Russia
has changed a great deal. Criminals are not the same.
You yourself said they now attack the most sacred
— the children. This is why adequate methods and
measures should be used.

Our new minister, Anatoliy Sergeyevich Kulikov under-
stands this. I see this problem as number one today. So-
ciety cannot constantly live in such situations. Therefore
the measures should be appropriate. We shall report to

[Semenov] Our conversation is moving to its end. With

Will Russia is Our Home be prepared to accept such a

Situation should arise, will you agree, in principle, to
lead a coalition government?

[Chernomyrdin] First of all, I should say for myself.
I am in favor of the government of professionals. The
government is not a political body. It is not a party, not
a CPSU Central Committee. We shall not again live in
such times. Only understanding, professionally educated
people who should work in the government. I can’: tell
you that in Chernomyrdin’s government today everyone
is. Russia is Our Home... [pauses] Nothing of the kind.
My deputy prime minister and a minister are agrarians,
Shakhray is from PRES [Party of Russian Unity and
Accord], I have a minister from Russia’s Choice. So
even today the government is not very homogenous, so
to say. As for the future, let me repeat — I am for
professionals, for thinkers, and more importantly, for
those who are able to do this very important job.

I can tell you and all our listeners that I know pretty
well many factions existing today and their leaders.
I have often asked those of them who criticize the
government too ofter. So you think we are doing it
wrong. Yes, they say. So then, you know how to do it?
Why don’t you come? I told this to Yavlinskiy, and to
many others. If you know, what are you waiting for, why
don’t you come? Are you enemies of your own people,
your country? Do come, I am prepared to sit next to
anyone. Not a single one responded. You understand,
not a single one. When we come, then we'll deliver,

they say. Can you believe it? That they will deliver? Of
course not.

Three years ago Chernomyrdin was a different person.
I was not inexperienced when I came, I did not come
from a research lab, I was not an instructor or a head
of lab. I came after I had achieved something in life.
So today, when I look back at my three years, I say to
myself, not to anyone else, but to myself that I am very
different from the Chernomyrdin of December 1992.
That is why those who say today that if they come to
power they would pay salaries, scholarships, pensions
tomorrow, stop this or that, and move ahead — all this
is garbage, yet more subterfuge, yet more deception.

It is undesirable, to say the least, to rock Russia again
today. We must move ahead. Under no circumstances
must we stop. We should correct our mistakes, bui we
must move forward. Processes should not stop, I am
convinced. Personally I am prepared to work with any
business-minded professionally trained people. I believe
that that’s what any government should be about.

[Semenov] And to round up, not a very serious ques-

"Russia is Our Home" succeed, which will be your per-

(Chernomyrdin, laughing] I am not gearing up for cither

now. I want everyone to understand what we like and
want to do. I want people to make the right choice. |
have seen so many successes and failures in my life, I
have gone through so much. I believe I will manage to
contain my emotions in either case.

[Semenov] Will you be able to have a long lie-in, at
least?

(Chernomyrdin, laughing] Thank you very much for a
good discussion. I wish kindness, patience and health
to all your listeners. Our country will never be on its
knees, never, although many want it. I am sure, I am
convinced. We have everything to provide well-being to
every family. We must and will do everything. While
I am here, and I am speaking now mainly on my own
behalf, I vl do all I can for a better life of our people,
for their wc ll-being. And we'll get there.

[Semenov] Thank you very much and all the best to
you.
