Problems Seen in Transition From Yeltsin

MKI1612153595 Moscow KOMMERSANT-DAILY
in Russian 16 Dec 95 p 3

{Report by Veronika Kutsyllo under the "Problem of
Authority in Russia" rubric: "Russia To Have a New
President in Six Month’s Time"}

[FBIS Translated Text] There are exactly six months left
before the presidential elections scheduled for 16 June.
They will differ strikingly from the current elections
for the State Duma, because after 17 December actual
authority will not change hands — Boris Yeltsin will re-
main in power. What steps might the current residents
of the Kremlin take to stay there even after 16 June,
and how will these steps be underpinned ideologically?
During the past few weeks KOMMERSANT-DAILY’s
Veronika Kutsyllo spoke on these subjects with repre-
sentatives of the presidential entourage and with presi-
dential loyalists and opponents in other political struc-
tures. KOMMERSANT-DAILY offers its readers the
resulting picture.

Boris Yeltsin’s Era and the Point of No Return

The standoff between the president and government on
the one hand and the parliament, on the other, which was
typical for 1992-1993, has become history. Presently,
it is not confrontation between the different branches
of power that makes the difference, but the horizontal
and ver‘ical ties between politicians representing these
branches. The Duma and the Federation Council are a
far cry from the Supreme Soviet, let alone the Congress
of People’s Deputies, which could make “momentous”
decisions. Within two years Yeltsin managed to become
the actual head of state, not the head of just one branch
of power — the executive — what he had actually been
before October 1993.

This achievement cost Yeltsin dearly — he paid for it
with a precipitous fall in his approval rating among the
people. The image of the president as fighter (against
privileges, communism, Soviets, and so forth), so dear
to people’s hearts, was replaced by the image of just a
president. Ironically, the more Yeltsin avoids head-on
confrontation with his opponents, the more difficult it
is for him to retain any chance of reelection. Yeltsin
the presidential candidate objectively stands to benefit
from a communist-patriotic comeback in the Duma
elections, for it will provide him with an actual enemy,
the struggle against whom could become the foundation
of his election campaign. Yeltsin the president needs

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

76 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

popular confidence that the authority will remain stable
— he is earnestly convinced that he was “called” to the
political Olympus not only to destroy the old system
(like many politicians of the transition period), but in
order to build a new one in its place.

Let us not touch on the problems of his health. Let us
imagine that the president has definitively improved his
health for a long time to come and will contest the 1996
elections (according to Aleksandr Korzhakov, Yeltsin
will announce his decision in February’. Elections entail
the possibility of a change in power, while the prospect
of power’s changing hands inevitably raises the problem
of the so-called point of no return — or, rather, whether
or not Russia has passed it. Curiously, nearly all those
who hope to leave their imprint on Russian history say
unanimously, regardless of their political views, that
the point of no return has not yet been passed. The
difference being that in the view of the opposition it is
Russia’s luck that these processes can yet be reversed,
one just needs to act quickly while there is still time to
do so. For the president’s followers, who acknowledge
this as if grudgingly, this is a threat that requires action.
Naturally, the question regarding the point of no return
is closely intertwined with another question: Is Boris
Yeltsin’s era drawing to an end, or is it still in full
swing?

The Kremlin’s official stance is evident: Boris Yeltsin’s
era goes on. First, because for all his achievements
Yeltsin’s reformist potential has not yet been exhausted.
Second, because only Yeltsin’s figure is keeping the
country now and will keep it in the future from
destabilization and sliding backward.

Of course, while being a good electoral move, the
slogan "Yeltsin is the country’s only hope," on the
other hand amounts to a recognition of the vulnerability
of the system he himself created. For this reason it
should be offset by actions of the authorities proving its
unshakable nature. It is fully evident that the Kremlin
is betting on this system and is really fearful of its
disruption (U.S. political scholar Aleksandr Yanov’s
book likening Russia to Germany during the Weimar
Republic is the publication quoted most frequently by
presidential loyalists). Virtually all the latest decisions
by the president and the government are aimed at
strengthening it and creating a framework for the
future, which is clearly not limited to June 1996. The
edict on delaying regional elections, the appearance
of several government decrees at one time on long-
term programs reaching into the 21st century, legal and
judicial reforms, and the creation of formidable financial
industrial groups — it is all designed to strengthen the
system regardless of who is in power.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

Kremlin Aikido

It has become a tradition to expect the worst from
the powers that be — the myth of Yeltsin's carefully
disguised perfidy (fraught with a postponement or
cancellation of the elections), created by the press
and political scholars, cannot be dispelled by his most
candid assurances to the contrary. The possibility of
canceling or postponing elections at the initiative of
the president or his entourage is still nearly one of
the most topical subjects. Meanwhile, this school of
thought does not take account even of elementary
logic. The simplest analysis of Yeltsin’s political actions
Suggests the evident conclusion that Yeltsin's career
and viability genetically stem from legitimate popular
support expressed by democratic methods. He will
simply be unable adequately to continue in office after
spurning scheduled elections.

Of course, it is an Asian-Kalmyk option that would suit
the Kremlin best (changing the law on referendums is
just a technical matter after all). The Kremlin, but not
Yeltsin. A politician of his psychological type needs to
be assured of popular support, which, moreover, must
be expressed legitimately.

The most diverse methods may be used in the struggle
for the presidency, varying from purely practical to
purely psychological, as well as mixed ones. "Flying
trial balloons into the future" can be called a practical-
psychological method, while the “struggle with the
communist-nationalist threat" is a purely psychological
one. Yet another method, widely used in politics, is the
"interception" of ideas and sentiments gaining currency
in society, a political aikido of sorts. You overpower
the enemy as if by giving way to him and continuing
his movement.

Boris Yeltsin has mastered this type of martial arts to
perfection, and he can gradually knock most propaganda
supports out from undcr his political opponents. The
ideology of statehood and patriotism, which has eclipsed
the ideology of “antisovietism,” has been borrowed from
the programs of the president’s opponents. This is a
large borrowing, so to speak. As for minor ones, Boris
Yeltsin resorts to them fairly often. The latest case
in point és his gracefully taking over the problem of
demobilizing "Chechnya" and "Tajikistan" compulsory
service soldiers: The president sent back to the Duma
the bill enabling recruits who participated in warfare
to be demobilized after 18 months, not two years, and
granted them the same right by his edict. So who is it
to whom the servicemen should be grateful?

The "interception" method (they in the Kremlin prefer
a less expressive term — “the integration of political

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

trends") will be used even more widely after 17 De-
cember. The range of borrowed solutions may be ex-
tremely diverse — from the continued manufacturing
of “socially-oriented” edicts, such as the recent “greed
and foolishness benefit” to [pyramid-scheme] investors
and partial deprivatization, to large-scale showcase tri-
als featuring corrupt officials from the top echelons of
power.

Skuratov and Dubinin Are Just the Beginning

A new line has also emerged in personnel policy. At first
sight, the president was making his latest appointments
according to “interception” methods, but in fact there
seems to be something more important to it. In the
Kremlin, this process is called the “new personnel
policy.” The appointments of Dubinin and Skuratov are
just the tip of the iceberg, which will fully emerge
above water only after the parliamentary elections. This
iceberg has a name that is very difficult to pronounce
— “the constitutionalization of authority." This is also
a method of strengthening the system. The legitimacy
of bureaucrats’ stay in power may not raise any doubts
in society.

Of course, the opposition’s talk of a broad alliance in
the new parliament that will allegedly automatically lead
to the formation of a new government is a complete
bluff. The Constitution does not oblige the president to
change the cabinet or the premier after the December
elections; the cabinet tenders its resignation only to
the newly-elected president. The conviction is gaining
ground in the Kremlin, however, that it would be fully
logical for Boris Yeltsin to get rid of the most often
criticized figures, such as Andrey Kozyrev and Pavel
Grachev. Although it would be preferable to carry
out this operation after 17 December, information is
already seeping out that Kozyrev is openly bracing
for resignation, while Grachev has heard at least two
warning calls — the dismissal of Vasiliy Vorobev and
Aleksandr Korzhakov's interview for Russian Public
Television [ORT] the day before yesterday, in which
Yeltsin's chief bodyguard listed the defense minister's
excessive love for his family and friends and a gift for
delivering reports and organizing parades as his best
qualities. Firing the ministers would be easy, however;
what would be more difficult is to find replacements
for them who would create at least a semblance of a
coalition cabinet. But the Kremlin is actively searching
for candidates, there is no doubt about it.

If these two processes — “constitutionalization” and
“interception” —- are carried to their logical extremes
(or absurdity, which is nearly the same in this particular
case), even absolutely fantastic scenarios will become
possible: The government, for instance, could tender a

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 77

resignation of its own accord. Or it may be asked to
do so by the president. Then Boris Yeltsin will have
two weeks to tell the Duma the name of the new prime
minister (with Chernomyrdin being his main support
base at the Duma) or... the old one — so as to reaffirm
his authority.

Yet, the Kremlin’s choice of one method or another will
depend on the situation that will come about after 17
December. An overly vigorous attack by the new Duma
on the cabinet could well backfire, and the cabinet might
be pointedly retained. The thing is that Yeltsin is not a
genius of “multi-move” combinations (however eagerly
his analysts might draw them up); his forte is to choose
at the crucial moment a stopgap measure from a pile
of recommended scenarios that would be most fitting
for this situation. He is perfectly aware of that and,
as far as KOMMERSANT-DAILY knows, sometimes
deliberately asks his advisers to leave certain matters
not fully resolved so as to cut the Gordian knot at the
last moment on his own.

Must Ne’er Be Harnessed to One Cart

It is this presidential skill that makes it virtually
impossible to predict the sequence of his moves. The
Situation is being made worse by the fact that the
Kremlin has no single brain trust, whereas the various
small trusts do not always have horizontally-arranged
links between them both at the stage of scenarios and
at the stage of concrete actions. Nor is there campaign
machinery that could take care of Yeltsin's campaign
in a centralized manner. It is perhaps better this way
after all. There was more than one attempt to create
such a rigid hierarchy — the history of the struggle
to restructure the administration adds up to months,
if not years, and it might not be at an end even if
Yeltsin signs an edict. Describing this struggle only as
a conflict between Ilyushin, Filatov, and Korzhakov is
unjustified. The ongoing struggle is not for soft jobs but
for Yeltsin himself, hence for Russia’s future. Indeed,
most dangerous is a “rollback” engineered from within.

However, for several years now, Yeltsin has been giving
lie to the adage that a steed and a deer cannot pull the
same cart [reference to a line from Pushkin] — he has
been able to use to his own advantage the disparate
aspirations of those around him thanks to his ability to
keep his bearings. In all likelihood, the ambitious plans
to make the administration into a single campaign staff
led by Ilyushin, with a behind-the-back Korzhakov, and
Filatov reduced to organizational work, are not going
to prove so ambitious after all. At least, according to
KOMMERSANT-DAILY’s information, the latest plan
only distantly resembles the original rigidly structured
variants.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

78 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

Incidentally, the inordinate growth of the Presidential
Security Service head’s political clout in 1994-1995
and his “reactionary” party is yet another myth. First,
Korzhakov’s influence could hardly Fave grown after
1991, since it was big right from the start. Second, many
people know that, if one can come up with a definition
of the political choices of such a whimsical figure as
Korzhakov at all, the only definition is: He is the main
opponent of “obkomovisy” [oblast Communist party
secretaries] (with the exception of the “obkomovets”
Yeltsin, naturally) in the Kremlin. (As for Ilyushin,
in addition to his apparat past, they in the Kremlin
have another bone to pick with him: It is owing to
his first aide that Yeltsin had the naive belief that
Yuriy Skokov had genuine respect for him). That is to
say, simplistically put, Korzhakov is a supporter of the
democrats as it were. It is another matter that he does not
include in this category many of those conventionally
regarded as people of a democratic orientation —
Filatov, for one.

One of the latest instances of division among Kremlin
services is the story of the “Russian Reforms — New
Course” movement. The Kremlin-Shumeyko idea —
according to KOMMERSANT-DAILY’s information,
taking part in developing it were both Yeltsin's aides
and the Presidential Security Service — was launched
too early, in the Kremlin's view. True, the situation can
be described in a different way: Yeltsin deliberately
“clamped down on" Shumeyko in a bid to stress the
independence of the initiative. Some Kremlin analysts
thought this haste disadvantageous, diverting attention
from the election. Other analysts were convinced of the
opposite — the earlier the better.

Generally speaking, the creation of this movement
is rather logical — this is a replacement for the
weak center-left bloc. How else to explain Shumeyko’s
sudden “going social democratic?” The idea to merge,
after 17 December, blocs loyal to Yeltsin to form a
single powerful pro-presidential force is not obsolete
yet. The reasons are as follows: If two blocs are not
strong enough, let’s add a third one (forth, fifth, etc.). As
they say in the Kremlin, Korzhakov’s deputy Georgiy
Rogozin, who is patronizing “Russian Reforms,” thinks
that one of the movement's missions should be to
protect the president as an institution. Whether the
movement justifies the hopes pinned on it is too early
to judge. It is clear, however, that its missions are quite
in agreement with “constitutionalization” if one sees it
as keeping the status quo.

Setting Limits on Charisma

Many people in the Kremlin are convinced that Russia
is not ready yet for a peaceful transfer (surrendering,

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

in the given case) of power to another force. It is
also believed that Yeltsin’s staying in power is the
only guarantee of keeping his entourage. This is not
necessarily so. Not all of the entourage, but some
people in it may be considering another leader — some,
Skokov, Soskovets, or Shumeyko; some, Luzhkov (he,
Korzhakov thinks, is unsuited for president either now
or in the year 2000). But all of these people realize
very well that there is yet no figure matching Yeltsin in
charisma who is also a figure of the system; this must be
nurtured first. Nor is there a procedure for transferring
power that is beyond doubt. The Constitution is only a
contour. Who is going to turn over to a new president
the “nuclear suitcase,” and how? Who is quitting the
Kremlin, who is staying on, who will be the “steward?”
Would Yeltsin's departure entail a massive “emigration”
of civil servants, including the “special servants" from
the power structures, as was the case in 1991? The
entourage is not sure yet of the reverse — they will
follow together with Yeltsin the procedural road.

It is this road that poses the biggest threat for Yeltsin.
The president is trying to force on society rules of the
game beyond which society would not be able to go
even if the leader is replaced. A paradox: The most
charismatic leader of Russia is doing everything to
reduce the role of charisma to the minimum in the
future. It is he who is the first to match the limits
on charisma. Yeltsin is constantly expected to make
sharp body movements, secretly prepared blows. He
is expected to clearly indicate the enemy — the new
Duma as a whole, or the Communists separately, or
General Lebed in particular. With the exception of some
screwups, Yeltsin constantly disappoints both his former
ardent supporters and the implacable opposition: He is
trying — successfully or otherwise — to play by the
rules laid down two years ago.

Should Yeltsin convince the people of Russia of this,
he would have a chance in June 1996. This is possible
— the president has yet another quality he can make
use of — the ability to pull apart, as it were, the
strands of political intricacies people do not quite
understand, appealing directly to the people. If not,
making procedures a fetish would blow into his face.
Be that as it may, Yeltsin is no longer the president he
was in 1991-1993. The ideology of expediency is still
going strong — which is being proven by Chechnya,
for example — but it is conceding its positions to
the “constitutionality” ideology. In principle, it can be
said that the Boris Yeltsin era — if seen as an era of
overthrowing past ideals, an era of forcible destruction
of the old system — is almost at an end. Whatever
another Duma is going to be, there will be no struggle
"to the death” between the authorities inside Russia.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

There will be political moves — good or bad. There
will be powerful propaganda for and against Yeltsin
— good or bad, but not more than that. Whatever
its outcome, the 16 June elections will return a new
president radically different from Boris Yeltsin — a
destroyer and implacable fighter. Even if the name of
that president is Boris Yeltsin.

Russia Said in Pre-Revolutionary Conditions

MM1812093395 Moscow SOVETSKAYA ROSSIYA
in Russian 14 Dec 95 p2

[Article by Professor Yuriy Kachanovskiy: "Nosedive.
Stabilization With Symptoms of a Coma"}

{[FBIS Translated Excerpt] Khabarovsk —- [Passage
omitted] The situation in Russia today corresponds
entirely to V.I. Lenin’s definition of the revolutionary
situation. "Generally speaking, what are the signs of a
revolutionary situation? We would surely not be wrong
if we pointed out the following three main signs: 1)
the ruling classes’ inability to maintain their dominance
unchanged: a crisis of the ‘upper classes,’ the crisis
of the policy of the ruling class, which creates a split
enabling the discontent and indignation of the oppressed
classes to erupt. The ‘refusal of the lower classes’ is
usually not enough to trigger a revolution, it needs
the ‘inability of the upper classes’ to live in the old
way to achieve that. 2} The needs and problems of
the oppressed classes have to become more acute than
usual. 3} There must be a considerable increase in the
activeness of the masses for the above reasons....” (V.I.
Lenin, Complete Collected Works, vol. 26, p. 218).

All these factors exist in Russia today but is it possible
to overthrow the Yeltsin-Chernomyrdin anti-people oc-
cupation regime "at one fell swoop” by relying on the
revolutionary situation? I do not think so. No revolu-
tion in history has been carried out “at one fell swoop.”
Each revolution — the English Revolution in the 17th
century, the French Revolution in the 18th century, and
the Great October Socialist Revolution — led to a long,
bloody struggle. In present-day Russia, which is filled
with nuclear missiles, nuclear power stations, and haz-
ardous production units, this would mean a global catas-
trophe both for us and for all civilization. Only madmen
can gamble on such a scenario. Any proposal aimed at
resolving everything “at one fell swoop" is not even
worth discussing.

However, it is impossible to ignore the existence of a
revolutionary situation in Russia. It is the duty of all
normal people to do everything possible to ensure:

— first, that it does not develop into a social explosion;

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 79

— and second, that society emerges from the revolu-
tionary situation onto the road of normal /crisis-free/
development.

Special responsibility rests with the Yeltsin-
Chernomyrdin regime. It is an indisputable fact
that they can no longer govern the country and cannot
resolve a single problem, starting with the salvation of
the economy and ending with the fight against crime.
But if they nevertheless cling onto power at all costs,
thereby inciting a social explosion, they will place
themselves in the position of dangerous criminals who
threaten all civilization.

Option Three: Instill Order

We must examine the following questions:
What are the aims of the struggle?
By what methods is it to be waged?

The aims of the struggle.

They are included in the election platform of the
Communist Party of the Russian Federation [CPRF]
(See SOVETSKAYA ROSSIYA, 31 August 1995).
[passage omitted]

People are being intimidated by the claim that if the
Communists come to power they will set Russia at
odds with the West and revive the “Cold War." The
Communists and patriotic forces have no interest in that.
We need peace and good-neighborly relations with the
West to ensure more favorable conditions for Russia’s
revival. We want to end the third world war by an
honorable peace. The West must understand (sensible
politicians already understand this) that the crises of
the 21st century can be favorably resolved only on
condition of equitable cooperation among the states
of the world. But there are attempts from a position of
strength using political and economic pressure to resolve
environmental problems and problems of resources in
favor of the "golden billion" to the detriment of the
third world countries and Russia, this will ultimately
prove disastrous for civilization. Communists support
the resolute defense of Russia’s national interests and
at the same time support the closest cooperation with
Western countries on the basis of equality and an
amicable and just consideration of each other’s interests.

I believe that the CPRF’s program and election platform
correctly point the way in the struggle to overcome the
crisis and ensure the motherland’s revival and progress.
There will be many difficulties to overcome on this path.
But the Communists would be deceiving the people if
they said that there is an easy road. There is no easy
road. The motherland is semi-destroyed, it has been
cut to the quick and become dependent for food and

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

80 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

finance on other powers. It will require strenuous work
and struggle to end all this and enable our children and
grandchildren to live a normal, happy life. The struggle
must be well thought out and purposeful if it is to be
conducted by peaceful methods alone.

At the present stage it is necessary to win the peaceful
battle on 17 December 1995. Over 2,600 candidates
have registered for the 225 State Duma seats in the
single-seat districts, an average of 12 candidates per
seat. Here in Khabarovsk there are 18 candidates in
the single-seat district. There are over 40 parties on the
federal electoral list. How is all this to be sorted out?
Is it not all that difficult. I urge people to vote for the
list of candidates from the CPRF, which is number 25
on the ballot paper. And in the single-seat district vote
for the candidate nominated by the CPRF.

Attempts To Restore Strategic Influence Viewed

MS1812093295 London THE TIMES in English
18 Dec 95 pill

[Article by Jonathan Eyal, director of studies at the
Royal United Services Institute in London: "How West-
ern Promises Failed to Kill Off Russia’s Dream of Past
Glories")

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Despite all the claims to the
contrary, no Western leader ever believed that Russian
democracy was a serious proposition. Indeed, support
for President Yeltsin is based on the assumption that
he is the only man able to ignore the wishes of his
parliament and, by implication, those of the Russian
electorate.

From this perspective, Western governments may be
right in ignoring the Russian parliamentary elections,
since the parliament has few real powers, apart from the
ability to generate a great deal of hot air. Furthermore,
the expected surge in the vote for the Communists is
a Europe-wide phenomenon. Finally, even if the result
is "unfortunate", as many Western politicians privately
expect, there is little that anyone can do about it.

Nevertheless, the elections are a clear indication that
Western policies to encourage economic reform and
partnership with Moscow have failed. Instead of a more
cooperative and benign partner, the world will be faced
with a morose Russia, neither pleasant nor pliant, and
determined to restore its strategic influence at all costs.

In the words of Grigoriy Yavlinskiy, a leading reformer,
the 43 political movements involved in the elections
promised everything “apart from the restoration of
virginity"; lower taxes and prices and higher salaries
anc pensions featured in all their platforms.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

Unremarkable, since elections everywhere are littered
with similar promises. Yet Russia is different. In all
the countries of Eastern Europe, political parties were
quickly formed after the collapse of communism and
many have survived. In Russia, however, parties are
created just before an election and just as quickly
disappear. Viktor Chernomyrdin, the Prime Minister,
founded his own movement only recently, having spent
his time in office pretending to be above politics.

Stripped of their pretences, these elections amounted to
little more than a tussle between a handful of politicians,
former army generals, comedians, madmen and faith
healers. The computers which now tally the votes are
controlled by the old KGB, a sure sign of Russian-style
impartiality. Finally, Russian elections merely legitimise
a transfer of power that has already taken place.

Many Russians believe that the disintegration of the
Soviet Union is a disaster that should be put right as
soon as possible and that the West's promises of co-
Operation are ruses meant to keep Russia weak.

The only dispute between politicians is whether the
re-creation of a Soviet sphere of influence should be
accomplished by force soon, or through persuasion and
economic pressure over time. It is a serious distinction,
but without much difference for countries likely to
receive Moscow's attentions.

Of much greater significance is the expected return
of the Communists to parliament in great numbers. In
Russia, this is again quite different from the case in the
rest of Eastern Europe, where communism was imposed
from outside after the Second World War.

In Russia, however, communism was home-grown and
is associated with a period when the Soviet Union was
either respected or feared. The West never had much
chance of influencing Russia's internal developments.
Nevertheless, much of Western policy was based on the
belief that precisely the reverse was the case.

In fact, the new parliament is guaranteed to be more
anti-Western despite all the international efforts at ap-
peasement. And for simple reason: what the West has to
offer, photo-opportunities and interminable conferences,
is no longer interesting for Russia. And what Russia
wants — influence, strategic space, games of balance
of power — the West has not been prepared contem-
plate.

But the dialogue of the deaf between the West and
Russia is now likely to end. Moscow will be ruled
by people who not only want to be considered as
representatives of a great power, but who fully expect
to exact the necessary price for future co-operation.

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

([FBIS Translated Text] Moscow, 18 Dec (ITAR-TASS)
— A civilian Mi-8 search helicopter has discovered
fragments of an aircraft while overflying the area of Bo-
Dzhaus mountain in Khabarovsk Kray. They are thought
to be the remains of the Tu-154 that went missing on 6
December.

Air Traffic Controllers’ Strike Closes Buryatia
Airport

LD1612114795 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
1139 GMT 16 Dec 95

[By Aleksey Subbotin]

([FBIS Transcribed Text] ULAN-UDE, December 16
(Itar-Tass) — Ulan-Ude, the capital of the Russian
component republic Buryatia, today closed its airport
which has been idling since Saturday, because of all
airtraffic controllers’ strike.

The strikers are pursuing economic demands. What was
left of the airport’s services by the strike is being run by
three administrators having qualifications of air traffic
controllers.

A city administration spokesman told Itar-Tass that
flights via Buryatia had been taken over by an auxiliary
regional centre.

The Ulan-Ude airport normally serves up to 15 flights
a day, and 30 other flights daily traverse the area of
responsibility of local traffic controllers.

Nuclear Research Institute Shown for First Time

MM1512161895 Moscow Russian Television Network
in Russian 1100 GMT 8 Dec 95

[From the "Vesti" newscast: Video report from Inor-
ganic Materials Scientific Research Institute by A. Dub-

{[FBIS Translated Text} [111513] [Dubnov over shots of
exterior, interior of institute] At the end of the forties the
first measurable quantities of weapons-grade plutonium
were obtained in this building. Journalists have never
before set foot in the All-Russian Inorganic Materials
Scientific Research Institute named for Academician
Bochvar. The "Vesti" camera team were the first ever
to have been admitted.

The institute was foundec in 1945 to resolve materials-
science and technological problems pertaining to the
production of nuclear weapons. Unique techniques have
been developed in the secret laboratories of this institute.
For instance the method for the safe production of
highly toxic polonium for nuclear weapons. A nuclear
power plant operating on polonium isotopes ensured the
successful operation of the Lunokhod moon buggies.

Over the past 50 years the range of research work
carried out here has broadened considerably. Now the
institute’s specialists are working on the design of
fuel elements for various types of nuclear reactors and
on perfecting means and methods of non-destructive
monitoring. Work in celd plasma makes it possible
to obtain materials not just for the nuclear power
industry, but also for everyday use. The institute’s work

on superconductors and beryllium is now part of an
interns ,onal project on controlled nuclear fusion.

The recent declassification of the work of the Inorganic
Materials Scientific Research Institute inspires hope that
contracts and joint projects will enable this leading
institute in the sphere of nuclear power engineering to
improve its financial situation. The low wages of our
scientists and engineers are no state secret. [111636]

Regional Affairs

Chechnya Reportage: Elections, Negotiations,
Military

Zavgayev Elected: ‘Fraud’ Observed

AU1812123895 Paris AFP in English
1215 GMT 18 Dec 95

[Report by Peter Graff]

([FBIS Transcribed Text] Groznyy, Russia, Dec 18
(AFP) — Voters in Chechnya have confirmed the
Moscow-installed prime minister as leader of the re-
public in a landslide election, officials said Monday {18
December], despite evidence of fraud and a mass boy-
cott of the polls.

"More than 90 percent" of those who voted chose
Doku Zavgayev, the former Chechen communist party
boss, who had already been installed as the Caucasus
republic’s interim "prime minister" by Russian forces
earlier this year.

Electoral commission chief Abdul-Kerim Arsakhanov
told AFP that according to preliminary results available
by midday Monday, 248,200 people, or 53.6 percent of
"registered" voters participated in the elections.

A 25 percent turnout was needed to validate the poll.

Since no voter registration took place in Chechnya,
authorities were using estimates from local election
officials of the number of voting-aged people living in
each district to determine the size of the electorate, he
said.

No preliminary results were available for simultaneous
voting for deputies to the Russian parliament.

No international observers monitored throughout the
four-day elections in Chechnya, but journalists observed
widespread fraud and muitiple voting at polling stations.

Several Russian journalists who were not residents of
Chechnya said that they cast votes without showing any
documents, often more than once, just to prove that it
could be done.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

One election official, who asked not to be named,
admitted to AFP that he himself voted for Zavgayev
twice, once on behalf of his daughter.

Rebels loyal to separatist leader Dzhokhar Dudayev
boycotted the vote and according to anecdotal evidence
most Chechens, including many who oppose Dudayev,
also stayed away from the polls.

Zavgayev has a base of support in parts of Chechnya
which opposed Dudayev even before the war, such as
the Nadterechny region to the north and the town of
Urus Martan in the south.

In Groznyy, much of the Russian-speaking population
and a fair number of Chechen intellectuals, many of
whom are on the payroll of Zavgayev’s lavishly funded
government, also said they support him.

But most Chechens who spoke to AFP said they saw
Zavgayev as a puppet of Moscow and assumed the vote
would be rigged.

Zavgayev faced only token opposition in the polling.
The other two candidates were a fortune-teller and
an obscure journalist who suspiciously used his free
electoral television time to pra:se Zavgayev.

Former Russian parliament speaker Ruslan Khasbula-
tov, considered by many to be a popular neutral choice
and a figure of authority in Chechnya, bowed out of the
race last week, calling the elections a sham.

Dudayev’s supporters vowed to stop the polling, and
separatist guerrillas launched several assaults, including
a major operation on Chechnya’s second largest town,
Gudermes, when the four-day polling period be, 1
Thursday.

The separatists were still in ceatrol of the city Monday.

Separatist forces also seized buildings in Urus Martan
and took control of highways in much of the Chechen
countryside for the first time since summer.

Although the Russians only appeared to be offering
resistance in Gudermes, troops tightened their grip on
the capital Groznyy and neighbouring Argun.

Russian forces rolled into Chechnya in December, 1994
to crush the Dudayev government's independence bid.

Some 30,000 people have died in the fighting since,
according to most estimates, and much of the republic
has been reduced to rubble.

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

18 December 1995

Khasbulatov on Quitting Chechen Elections

MKI1512160595 Moscow NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA
in Russian 15 Dec 95 p 3

{Interview with Ruslan Khasbulatov, former speaker of
the Russian Federation Supreme Soviet, by NEZAV-
ISIMAYA GAZETA correspondents under the "View-
point" rubric; in Moscow, date not given: “Elections
in Chechnya Will Lead to the Emergence of Two Re-
publics. According to Ruslan Khasbulatov’s Informa-
tion, Prepasations Are Under Way To Rig Election Re-
sults")

([FBIS Translated Text] After announcing his with-
drawal from the contest for the post of head of the
Chechen Republic at a news conference in Groznyy,
Ruslan Khasbulatov returned to Moscow. NEZAVISI-
MAYA GAZETA correspondents asked him to spell out
his stance in greater detail.

[NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA] Ruslan Imranovich,
what prompted your refusal to participate in the elec-
tions?

[Khasbulatov] As of today neither the organizational
and technical nor the psychological conditions for
holding elections have been created. The reasons for
that are more than evident — the idea of elections was
"thrown in" a month before the elections were to be
held. Within such a short period of time it is impossible
to prepare elections, if only because the latest census
was held in 1989, before the elections for the Supreme
Soviet of Checheno-Ingushetia. Later, when presidential
elections were held in Chechnya, the same lists were
used.

But much has changed in the meantime. Checheno-
Ingushetia ceased to exist, and the Dudayev regime
forced many people to leave the republic. The tragic
war, too, took its toll — hundreds of thousands of people
have left the Chechen Republic. Now many people are
returning, but they no longer have homes of their own.

I think that the federal authorities have missed a
favorable opportunity for holding elections. They could
have been held back in the summer. My conversations
with leaders of the resistance movement repeatedly
confirmed that. Then the elections were scheduled for
5 November, but, unfortunately, the federal authorities
embarked on the path of delays so as to keep the
provisionally appointed administration in power and did
not do anything to carry out a census. No one expected
the idea of holding elections to pop up so suddenly. On
the contrary, we agreed with Zavgayev, amojig others,
that the elections would be held in the spring — in
March — and that in the meantime we ought to work
hard in order to create the political prerequisites.

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 83

I drew up the key principle of a treaty between
the Russian Federation and Chechnya, and it was a
pleasant surprise for me to learn that the resistance
movement leaders, including Yarikhanov, head of its
Official delegation, accepted the draft. I think that
in the draft we defined very clearly the border that
may not be crossed from the point of view of the
Russian Federation's constitutional principles. These are
matters relating to Russia’s territorial integrity. I based
my draft on four postulates that are essential for the
Chechen Republic to make progress in relations with
the federal authorities. These are common citizenship,
open borders, a single monetary system, and Chechnya’s
renunciation of it own regular army. The proposed
version upheld all principles of the Russian Federation's
integrity stemming not only from the Constitution, but
also from the psychology of Russian society; for the
notion of Russian integrity has already become part of
citizens’ consciousness regardless of what is laid down
in the Constitution.

When our stance was made public and recognized in
the Chechen Republic, we hoped that this draft would
be assumed as a basis, because it called for specific steps
toward rapprochement and a further negotiating process.
We designed a mechanism for holding elections. I had
repeated conversations with Oleg Lobov and Vyach-
eslav Mikhaylov, who backed my plan. Doku Zavgayev
did not object either, but all of a sudden they called elec-
tions, and this inflamed the situation once again.

[NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA] What treaty did Cher-
nomyrdin and Zavgayev sign?

{[Khasbulatov] I do not know. No one in the republic
has seen it. People are not fuming because the treaty
is bad. Maybe it is even better than the one we had
drawn up. But we are worried by the fact that the
preparations for the signing were kept secret from
the entire people of the republic. It was signed by a
person who is not vested with any authority, who is
not popularly elected. The resistance movement said
bluntly that this was done in order to recognize the
current authorities in Groznyy as legitimate and to
declare their opponents bandits and political vagabonds.
After this treaty was signed the polarization between the
resistance movement and the provisional administration
in Groznyy increased. The Zavgayev Administration
chose completely to ignore the resistance movement.
But this should not be done, because the number of its
followers is growing. People think it dangerous that the
old party methods of governing — feuds and intrigues
— are returning, and they go over to the Dudayevites.

[NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA] And still, how are the
preparations for the elections in Chechnya faring?

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

oe] RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

[Khasbulatov] When asked how many voters there are in
Groznyy, members of the republic electoral commission
answered: 120,000. At the city electoral commission I
was given the figure of 250,000. Yet others say 310,000.
As a candidate, I requested lists of voters. They did
not give them to me. There are no lists, they are
classified. I asked: And where are the polling stations? It
turned out that they decided to set up semi-underground
[polupodnolnyy] polling stations. They ordered 1,500
ballot boxes that will be moved to and fro *’ bus.
Imagine what elections these will be!

At the same time I received information that in paral-
lel some underground ballots are being manufactured
that may already have been filled out. There have been
nearly ridiculous incidents. | was approached by orga-
nizers of these underground measures who offered to
reverse their work to my advantage.

It is quite unclear how strong an army contingent is
currently deployed on the territory of the Chechen
Republic. Figures that have been cited vary from 6,000
to 100,000. One officer came to our headquarters and
said that they had already held elections... This is simply
an absurd situation.

I have repeatedly been approached by women who
complained about threats being made against them: If
they vote “the wrong way,” they will no lonper be paid
compensation for family members killed. Militiamen
take passports from people and take down their contents.

But the most terrible things happen in the districts. One
half of the districts will not vote at all. The elector
commission in charge of Nozhay-Yurt district operatcs
in Groznyy. That is, election results will be registered in
Groznyy, while not even one person will turn out to vote
in the district. Meanwhile, Groznyy is 70 km off! Of
22 districts, the vote may be held only in four districts
of Groznyy at best. Even in the Urus-Martan district
the situation has changed drastically. When Dudayev
was in power, 99 percent of Urus-M’ stan residents were
opposed to him. Now 60-80 percent of local residents
are in Opposition to the current authorities and do not
want to participate in the election.

([NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA] Who stood behind the
choice of the election date? Both the federal authorities
and presidential representative Oleg Lobov could not
but realize what might follow after such elections.

(Khasbulatov] Unfortunately, Oleg Lobov backed these
elections; he said that conditions are ripe for holding
them. But he quickly realized that this is not so. A few
days ago, addressing local television, he said that the
initiative of holding elections originated with the local

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

authorities, not with Moscow, that is, he was careful to
deflect in advance possible criticism aimed at him.

Doku Zavgayev created the impression in Moscow that
there is no resistance movement at all, and that its
commanders are practically standing in line outside his
office to give up their weapons.

([NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA] Presently there is talk
once again about a military solution to the Chechen
problem. You must have spoken to military men. What
do the officers themselves think in that regard?

(Khasbulatov] Servicemen — both privates and officers
— are eager to be withdrawn. But a war has to be
finished in a dignified way. Our People’s Union for the
Revival of the Republic is perhaps the sole organization
that offers a specific peaceful way out. Those who say
that there is no peaceful solution are bending the truth.
Nowhere throughout the world do the military chart
ways of political development. In our country, however,
this has become commonplace.

[NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA] Is Zavgayev, however
bent he might be on his desire to take up the post of head
of the republic as soon as possible, unable adequately
to assess the situation?

[Khasbulatov] Unfortunately, currently elements of the
former party-administrative system are being restored in
Chechnya, and Zavgayev fits in well within this system.
He has never been able to make a clear analysis of the
situation. Neither in 1990 nor in 1991, when I several
times told him that the ground was already burning
under his feet. He replied that everything was under
control and he held consultations with Gorbachev. This
inadequacy did a disservice not only to him, but to our
desperate people as well.

I think that the Russian federal authorities and Cher-
nomyrdin should get the lay of the land on their own be-
fore giving Zavgayev their backing — if they are inter-
ested in resolving this crisis, of course. Sometimes one
gets the impression that the federal authorities are inter-
ested in keeping up the Chechen conflict, while making
it controllable. But I do not think that this conforms
with the interests of the Russian Federation.

[NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA] Who stands behind the
provocations and explosions?

{[Khasbulatov] It is difficult for me to say. Recently
we conducted surveys that produced the following
responses. A total of 39 percent think that the warring
side bears responsibility for them; 19 percent of those
polled think that these acts are on the conscience of
the provisional authorities; and 42 percent blame the
federal forces for the provocations. | touched on this

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

subject during my conversation with Maskhadov, but he
assured me that his military units would never commit
such baseness.

But the endless confrontation and mutual recriminations
add fuel to the fire and create favorable conditions for
terrorists. This is evident. There have been no talks
for more than two months now, but no one knows
about this here because all officials — Lobov and
Mikhaylov — keep talking about the talks, even though
they were thwarted by the federal side in early October
after an attempt on Romanov’s life. The situation has
also been compounded by the complete absence of any
communication between the provisional administration
and the resistance movement.

([NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA] Why does the situation
in Chechnya presently continue to deteriorate?

[Khasbulatov] This is very simple to explain. The
federal authorities back one side — the provisional
administration, while the other side, which currently has
the support of about 30 percent of the pepulation behind
it, is deemed to be illegal. In the absence of talks this
situation cannot but degenerate into a war.

[NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA] What will the 17 De-
cember elections in Chechnya lead to?

[Khasbulatov] The elections will lead to the emergence
of two republics — the Chechen Republic and Ichkeriya.
The results will Se rigged, there is no doubt about
it. But this worries me much less than the possible
consequences. These will be fatal. I think that elections
should be postponed until a later period of time, and
that talks should be resumed immediately.

Zorin: Elections Valid Despite Provocations

LD1612095995 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
0943 GMT 16 Dec 95

[By Sharip Asuyev, Ruslan Maisigov]

([FBIS Transcribed Text) GROZNYY, December 16
(ITAR-TASS) — Chechnya’s elections may be said
to have been valid in principle, Vladimir Zorin, first
deputy head of the federal executive administration in
Chechnya, told reporters today.

He said the third day of the elections of Chechnya’s new
leaders and of the Russian State Duma, held ahead of
Russia-wide legislative elections, was marked by voting
of Chechen elders at home and by voting visits of rural
residents to Groznyy.

As for the situation in Gudermes, Urus-Martan and
Shatoy, Zorin said rebel activation there was a part
of provocations which Dudayev’s supporters had been
planning after walking out ef the Groznyy peace talks
on October 12.

It is also a bid to wreck Chechnya’s elections and build
up tension in Russia on the eve of the December 17
elections to the State Duma, he said.

Rebels started hostilities in Gudermes two and a half
hours before the start of the Chechen elections, only
to have the counterproductive effect of stimulating the
turnout, Zorin said.

between Dudayev’s Chief of General Staff [Aslan]

the latter two men expressed concern over the large
number of casualties and the lack of support from the
people. They also demanded urgently reinforcements
from Maskhadov. At the end of the interception,
Geliskhanov angrily said, "I will make the people of
Gudermes die with me." Maskhadov, according to the

to use women and children to form a corridor along
which reinforcements could be sent to them.

a checkpoint on the western entrance to Groznyy, the
Chechen capital, from Urus-Martan, an INTERFAX
correspondent reports. The checkpoint’s defense has
been reinforced with armored vehicles. Trenches are
being dug there.

Deputy Premier Chubays Hails 1995 Economic
Results

MKI1612125495 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian
16 Dec 95 pl

[Report by Elmar Murtazayev: “Anatoliy Chubays Has
Celebrated All-Time Low Inflation Rate. First Deputy
Premier Has Summed Up 1995 Results]

(FBIS Translated Text] Addressing a news conference
yesterday, Anatoliy Chubays, Russian Federation Gov-
ernment first deputy premier, called this year’s eco-
nomic results complicated and controversial.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

88 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

Listing the accomplishments, the first deputy premier
mentioned “pronounced signs” of production stabiliza-
tion — its decline level for this year is forecast at 3-5
percent. Another of the government’s obvious assets,
Mr. Chubays thinks, is an “all-time low” inflation rate.
Thus, he predicts the December inflation level of 3 per-
cent, with the rate ranging from 4 to 1.5 percent monthly
in the next quarter.

Furthermore, alongside the strengthening of the Russian
ruble, the national gold and hard currency reserves have
considerably grown in 1995 (by a factor of 12 as against
last year). These indicators, the architect of the current
Stage of Russian reforms is confident, provide enough
reasons to believe that the next year is going to be the
first year of economic growth in Russia.

Nonetheless, Mr. Chubays acknowledged that the gov-
ernment has failed to resolve a number of complex eco-
nomic problems in 1995. Thus, the problem of nonpay-
ment remains unsettled. Owing to the efforts made by
the cabinet throughout spring and summer, the level of
nonpayment was considerably reduced, but, by Decem-
ber, its amount grew to equal that of the year’s start (12
percent of GDP).

The situation in the social sphere is still grave: At the
end of the year, about 30 million people in Russia
are finding themselves below the poverty line. In this
connection, Mr. Chubays mentioned that early in the
year the number amounted to 40 million people. Here,
too, there have appeared “encouraging signs." Thus,
since early spring, a trend toward bridging the gap in
the social level of the rich and the poor has been patent,
which, in his view, proves once again that inflation is
the most effective way of redistributing the national
income from the poor to the rich. Thus, by combating
inflation, Mr. Chubays concluded, the government is
virtually countering the fall of the standard of living
of the Russian people.

The passage of the federal budget through the State
Duma is also viewed as a positive result of this year.
As the first deputy premier put ‘t, this is indicative of “a
better understanding of the economic situation by the
deputies.” Among what was described as strong deci-
sions of the parliament's lower chamber, he mentioned
the adopted Civil Code and the Law "On Securities Mar-
ket.” At the same time, the activity of the Duma brought
about some negative results, too. Thus, the Law "On
Production Sharing” and the Land Code were described
by Mr. Chubays as “ineffective.”

Production Sharing Bill Said Illegally Amended

MK1612093595 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian
15 Dec 95 p2

[Report by Svetlana Lolayeva: “Authors of Produc-
tion Sharing Bill Accuse Sergey Glazyev and Yuriy
Boldyrev of Forgery"}

(FBIS Translated Text] The adventures of the draft law
"On Production Sharing Contracts,” which have been
continuing for over four months now, are becoming in-
creasingly scandalous in nature. At yesterday's news
conference, Aleksey Melnikov, an author of the bill, and
Andrey Konoplyannik, chief of the group of drafters,
accused Sergey Glazyev and Yuriy Boldyrev, cochair-
men of the commission Grawing up a new, coordinated
version of the bill, of forgery and falsification. In their
words, the night before the vote, Yuriy Boldyrev and
Adrian Puzanovskiy, member of the economic policy
committee, who introduced the coordinated version of
the draft law at the plenary meeting, made amendments,
which had not been supported by a majority of the State
Duma conciliation committee, to the text of the bill that
was submitted to the State Duma for consideration. The
deputies, who received the text 20 minutes before the
vote, did not have a chance to check it against the final
minutes of the conciliation committee meetings. In addi-
tion, Mr. Puzanovskiy confused the deputies by saying
that votes were recalculated because Vladimir Semago
had been recalled from the conciliation committee by
the Communist Party faction. As a result, the bill was
passed by 242 votes.

After that, it was sent to the State Duma’s legal ad-
ministration for a linguistic and legal expert appraisal.
Having checked the text of the law against the final min-
utes of the conciliation committee meetings, the legal
administration removed from the text the amendments
on which no coordinated decisions had been made. The
next day, however, these amendments were restored at
Sergey Glazyev’s directive, and Ivan Rybkin signed the
draft law and passed it to the Federation Council as
amended.

As a result, the bill, which is scheduled to be considered
by the Federation Council 19 December and which,
in Mr. Melnikov's view, has a good chance of being
approved, now includes such notorious provisions as
[1] the state’s right to unilaterally revise contracts in
case of “an essential change in circumstances,” [2]
the approval of production sharing contracts relating
to the use of continental shelf areas in its exclusive
economic zone and areas assigned to “special strategic
State interests” by the Federal Assembly, and [3] the
parliamentary approval of the lists of areas that can be
used under production sharing contracts. In the view

This report n.:y contain copynghted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

of the authors of the bill, these amendments render it
ineffective. Moreover, Mr. Konoplyannik said, since the
law was passed through an illegitimate procedure and,
therefore, may be revised any time now, if he were an
investor, he would not invest billions of dollars and run
the risk of losing them.

Yabloko, KRO Rivals Clash Over Production
Sharing Law

MKI1612152195 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian
16 Dec 95 p2

{Report by Svetlana Lolayeva: “Sergey Glazyev Ac-
cuses Author of Production Sharing Law of Forgery.
In His Words, There Was Attempt to Exclude Some
Provisions From Law Passed by Duma"]

([FBIS Translated Text] The situation surrounding the
agreed version of the law "On Production Sharing
Agreements" passed by the Duma is growing into a
scandal. Speaking in a press conference Thursday [14
December], one of the drafters of the law, Aleksey
Melnikov, accused Sergey Glazyev and Yuriy Boldyrev,
cochairmen of the commission from the State Duma
and the Federation Council, of falsifying the results
of the work of the conciliation committee owing to
which the deputies were submitted a text of the law
with amendments that were not supported by a majority
of the committee members.

Sergey Glazyev, chairman of the [Duma] Economic
Policy Committee and one of the KRO (Congress
of Russian Communities] leaders, sent a note to the
State Duma ethics commission where he, for his part,
assesses as “an attempted forgery of the State Duma-
passed law in the interest of commercial organizations,

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 89

which lobby for its passing,” the actions of another
drafter of the law, Aleksey Mikhaylov ("Yabloko"
faction). In Mr. Glazyev’s words, Mr. Mikhaylov,
“abusing his office of Economic Policy Committee
deputy chairman, undertook to submit for signature to
the State Duma chairman a text of the law which does
not correspond with the one passed in the State Duma
sitting.” Mikhaylov’s version, Sergey Glazyev pointed
out, did not include such important provisions of the law
passed by the deputies as “exclusion of customs duties
from the list of the taxes and duties from which investors
are exempt; an early termination of the right to use
mineral deposit plots under court rulings; the reference
to the fact that any change in terms of agreements shall
be made valid following the same procedure applicable
to the original agreements with the exception of the
competition procedure; taking account of the fact that
control on behalf of the state over the fulfillment of
agreements can be exercised not only by the executive
branch but also by the legislative branch.”

The fact that differences between members of the
conciliation committee which came into evidence while
the law was being fine-tuned in accordance with the
remarks of the upper house, which turned down the
Duma version of the draft, have taken the form of public
altercations can be explained not only by the importance
of the law itself for the country’s economy but also
by the fact that its passing coincided with the election
race. The passing of the law in this or that version is
an important stake in the election campaign both for
"Yabloko” members, who had the idea for the law, and
for Sergey Glazyev, one of the Congress of Russian
Communities top three men.

Kuchma: Business as Usual With Moscow After
Elections

LD1512180595 Moscow INTERFAX in English

[From the "Presidential Bulletin" feature]

(FBIS Transcribed Text] President Leonid Kuchma at a
news conference in London on Thursday said he did not
expect any policy change in Russia while Boris Yeltsin
remained president.

He does not believe the Russian election can in any
way affect the situation in Ukraine. Kuchma denied a
possible souring of relations with Moscow after Russia's
parliamentary election. "We are doing a lot to be able
to understand each other," he said adding that “at any

But the president stressed that equal partnership was the
only condition for bilateral relations.

On the domestic front, Kuchma referred to the need
for continued internal stability implying “stability of
policies, legislative basis and government." He said the
adoption of the new constitution was expected to help
towards what he called "macrostability."

On the diplomatic front, Kuchma pledged Kiev’s non-
involvement in any military blocs on the grounds that
“even our very statement of intent to join NATO could
become a factor of instability in Europe." He reaffirmed
Ukraine’s wish to remain a neutral state and serve as a
bridge to link West and East.

On the Balkans, the president announced government
plans to take an active part in postwar reconstruction
in Bosnia-Herzegovina once the Paris agreement was
signed. He spoke of substantial economic damage
sustained by his country from UN sanctions against
Belgrade though saying Kiev was well aware of how
complex the problem was and did not want to raise the
issue of compensation.

At the same time, Kuchma said proper conditions
must be set for Ukrainian participation and called on
Ukrainian diplomats to intensify effort. He also spoke of
getting British support for Kiev’s involvement in imple-
menting the Dayton accords. iF NOTE: Ukrainian For-
eign Minister Hennadiy Udovenko led an official dele-
gation to the London conference on postwar reconstruc-
tion in Bosnia-Herzegovina early this month. Ukraine is
also invited to take part a similar forum, but on the ex-
pert level, opening in Brussels on December 21.

Ukraine and Britain signed a joint declaration and two
agreements including a memorandum of understanding
on confiscation of illegal property.

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

Speaking after a reception by Queen Elizabeth on
Thursday, Kuchma said "I am inspired by this meeting
and I now understand why the Queen is so much
respected in Britain."

The Ukrainian leader returned home late on Thursday.

Speaking at the end of the London summit, presidential
chief of staff Dmytro Tabachnik said Britain had
now passed to active cooperation with Ukraine as
indicated by Kuchma’s meetings with British political
and business world.

He said the joint declaration the two countries had
Signed "indicates a new phase in bilateral cooperation.”

He also praised a range of loan agreements signed with
the European Bank for Reconstruction and Development
including those aimed at promoting small and private
business. Tabachnik said the latest agreement may well
make Ukraine the second large recipient of EBRD aid.

He reaffirmed the Bosnian peace settlement was also
on the agenda of the London summit adding that the
Ukrainian security council would discuss the deploy-
ment of Ukrainian peacekeepers in Bosnia at its next
session.

Effect of Russian Election on Ukraine Viewed

WS1812102995 Kiev INTELNEWS in English
0221 GMT 17 Dec 95

[By Oles Tereschenko, "Special to INTELNEWS"]

([FBIS Transcribed Text] Kiev, Dec. 16. The differing
perspectives on Sunday’s [17 December] election to
the Russian State Duma of actual citizens of Russia in
Ukraine and those who simply call themselves Russians
are telling.

Those with the right to vote (mainly military employees
who serve ships and objects of the Black Sea Ficet
[BSF]), are pessimistic. Two years ago they voted for
the Liberal-Democratic Party of Vladimir Zhirinovskiy,
hoping he would be able to preserve the unity of the
fleet. These hopes were in vain, however - the BSF's
division is a reality. Units are being disbanded, and
Russians are being transferred to Russia. These see their
future skeptically and doubt that their participation in
elections will change the situation of "forgotten Russian
people” for the better.

The carriers of the "Russian idea” without citizenship,
by contrast, are hoping that the new Duma will be able
to change the foreign policy course of Russia. According
to the chairman of Russian Culture Society in Crimea,
Vladimir Kazarin, Crimeans want a leader in the Duma

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

i report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination

FBIS-SOV-95-242
18 December 1995

who will go beyond protection of the rights of Russians
abroad and will seek "revival of the lost motherland.”

The past year has seen a weakening of the separatist
movement in Crimea. Instead, reunification with Russia
is now seen as possible only together with Ukraine in a
new union.

Because of this, pro-Russian politicians expect more
from Communists in Sunday's vote than from the lead-
ing nationalist party, the Congress of Russian Commu-
nities [CRC].

But both of these, despite their huge popularity in
Crimea, have weaknesses. The CRC’s is its unrealistic
idea of “supporting Russian speaking populations.”
This idea is hypothetical and has few chances for
implementation.

Grzjual, voluntary restoration of the union, for which
communists stand, is more attractive for voters of
Crimea. But the idea of revising the current Russian
constitution to include a “union policy” is out of the
hands of the not-yet-elected Duma.

The neo-communists, who consider themselves more
Soviet than Russian, could haidly become the exponents
of the Russian national idea of “gathering lands.”

Time will tell how quickly rightists and leftists will be
able to unite in the new Duma around their common

UKRAINE 91

interest, restoring some form of Russian-centered union
on the territory of the former USSR. The outcome is
critical for the Russian movement in Crimea.

While Russian citizens in Ukraine can hardly influence
the result of the Russian elections (because of their
small number), opinions about how these results may
influence Ukraine vary.

President Leonid Kuchma doesn’t expect the external
political course of Russia to change after the election.
After all, he says, foreign policy is the prerogative of
the President of Russia. As long as Boris Yeltsin is in
this post, Kuchma doesn’t expect cardinal changes in
relations between Ukraine and Russia.

But even assuming the Duma has a voice in foreign
policy, things are unlikely to change. Every party and
faction in Russia today stands for “changing Russian-
Ukrainian relations for the better,” although the candi-
dates prefer to ignore the fact that their positions are
identical.

They all speak instead about restoring “brother”, “sister”
or simply “friendly” ties by the method of all-sided
integration. So, attempts by Russia’s parliament to
correct the “near abroad" policy with the purpose of
creating a united state will not depend much upon the
political color of the majority in the next Duma.
